<!-- The catalog of the coaches' moves behind section 5 of `ops/facilitation ecology — six surrounding territories.md`, written 2026-09-26 at the author's request ("What about exploring the specific moves each of the coaches make in more catalogued or granular detail?") and recorded in ops/decisions.md. Two researchers gathered it, one for Byron Katie, Peter Crone, Michael Singer, Joe Hudson, Tony Robbins, and Thomas Hübl, and one for the Conscious Leadership Group, Jerry Colonna, Gay and Kathlyn Hendricks, John Wineland, and David Deida. Every quotation was compared by script with the raw text of the page or vault file named beside it, and the researchers' statements about the vault and the corpus were checked by hand. Filed under ops/ as a survey's reference, not as propositions of the book. -->

# The facilitation ecology — the coaches' moves

This file catalogues the moves, techniques, and theories of the eleven coaches and groups the corpus gathers in its survey of contemporary teachers: 111 entries, of which 11 are the coaches' own accounts of where suffering comes from, 60 are moves a coach makes within a session, and 40 are techniques, meaning structured practices with several steps. Each coach has one section, in alphabetical order by surname or by the group's name. Each section opens with an index table listing every entry with when the coach uses it and what it does, followed by the coach's theory and then the moves and techniques in the order the researcher recorded them, which for a procedure is the order in which it runs.

Each entry's table gives, in order: its type; when the coach uses it; what the coach does; the steps, in the coach's own words wherever a published session, transcript, or text supplies them; what the move does; what it facilitates and who supplies what; who decides the content, on the survey's five positions (A, the person decides all the content; B, the facilitator shapes the process; C, the facilitator offers content as an option; D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority; E, no live facilitator); the evidence; a researcher's reading of how it bears on the book and where it differs from the book; its limits and published critiques; its quotations; its date; the vault file nearest to it; its sources; and whether it was verified there.

The sources are the coaches' own published texts, program pages, and transcripts of their interviews and coaching sessions wherever these exist, with journalism and scholarship for the critiques. Each entry names its pages, and a quotation is a coach's own words only where its page is the coach's own or a transcript of them speaking. The corpus sorts these coaches into five theories of where suffering lives; section 5 of the supplement sets each coach's own account beside that sort, and this catalog is organized by coach rather than by the corpus's theories because for four of the eleven the coaches' own words do not fit the place the corpus gives them.

The same entries can be filtered by coach and by the book's concerns on the explorable page at https://claude.ai/artifact/DuVyj9ecaSZuBzXXgBEXHG, under the tab "Coaches' moves."

## Jerry Colonna

| Move | Type | When the coach uses it | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| Radical self-inquiry | a theory | Colonna gives this account in his books and interviews as the basis of his coaching of founders and executives. | The account explains a leader's self-defeating pattern as serving a need and makes self-inquiry, rather than advice, the way to see it. |
| “How have I been complicit in creating the conditions I say I don’t want?” | a move | Colonna asks it once a client trusts him, when the client describes conditions they say they do not want. | The question asks a person to find their own part in a situation they dislike without taking the blame for all of it. |
| The four questions of Reboot | a technique | Colonna and Reboot offer them to leaders as questions to return to rather than as a single exercise. | The questions turn a person's attention to what they are not hearing, what of theirs is not being heard, and what they are not saying. |
| “How is that being busy serving you?” | a move | Colonna asks it of a client who describes being overwhelmed or busy, once trust is established. | The question asks what a costly habit is giving the person. |
| Letting the crow speak | a technique | Colonna uses it in his morning journaling, which he does before meditating, and teaches it for self-criticism and anxiety. | The practice lets the inner critic speak on paper, names its protective purpose, and answers it with thanks. |
| The anxiety questions | a move | Colonna uses them when anxiety arises, in the journaling he does before meditation. | The questions ask an anxious mind to name its threat and whether it has heard the same alarm before. |
| Asking what made a person choose their career, and handing them a book | a move | He used it when a young professional asked him for help finding a new job. | The question turns a request for help with a job into a look at why the person chose their career. |
| Learning to speak like an adult | a move | He uses it when someone close to him has said something that hurt or frightened him. | The move names a hurt or fear to the person involved and states the choice to stay rather than withdraw. |
| The complicity question extended to systems (Reunion) | a move | Reunion offers the questions to leaders who hold power in organizations. | The questions ask a leader how they help keep exclusionary systems in place and what they would give up to change them. |
| Reboot bootcamps | a technique | Reboot runs bootcamps for startup leaders, co-founders, venture investors, and, as the Reunion Bootcamp, on the themes of the second book. | The retreat puts a leader among up to fifteen peers for several days of skills, self-inquiry, and shared experience. |

### Radical self-inquiry

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| When the coach uses it | Colonna gives this account in his books and interviews as the basis of his coaching of founders and executives. |
| What the coach does | Colonna holds that behaviors persist 'because they meet a need', often an unconscious need formed in childhood to feel loved, safe, and that one belongs, and he names love, safety, and belonging as 'three basic risks that we’re all trying to manage all the time'. He tells Tim Ferriss that 'the suffering that I encounter can almost always be rooted back to somebody not saying something that needs to be said.' He defines radical self-inquiry as 'the process by which the masks we wear, concealing ourselves from others but most importantly ourselves, are slowly and compassionately stripped away so there’s no place left to hide.' Reboot's page states that the aim of his central question is 'to restore a sense of authorship', and his second book extends the inquiry to a leader's part in systems of exclusion. |
| What it does | The account explains a leader's self-defeating pattern as serving a need and makes self-inquiry, rather than advice, the way to see it. |
| What is facilitated | The work makes a leader's own seeing of how their patterns serve them more likely, and Reboot states its aim as restoring authorship; the leader does the seeing. Colonna supplies questions, empathy, and at times an interpretation of what a pattern protects. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Colonna asks questions and the leader supplies the answers, though he also offers interpretations of what a pattern protects. |
| The evidence | The account rests on Colonna's coaching practice and his books; the sources opened report no outcome study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: Reboot's page states that the complicity question aims 'to restore a sense of authorship' rather than to reach a verdict. Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Reunion extends self-inquiry to a leader's part in systems of exclusion, which moves Colonna partway toward structural causes while keeping the person as the starting point. Protection and defense: Colonna reads a harmful pattern as serving an unconscious need for love, safety, or belonging, which is close to the book's reading of contraction as protection. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus files Colonna under disowned complicity and names the shared blind spot of all five camps as 'radical interiority of cause'. Colonna's own words separate complicity from responsibility and, in Reunion, name systems of oppression, so his account is less interior than the corpus's sort implies. |
| Limits and critiques | Reboot's page records that people hear the question as blame, 'When most people hear complicit, they assume “guilt” or responsibility', and it describes an essay in which Colonna reflects on how the question 'has drifted from its original intent over the years'. |
| Quoted from its sources | "behaviors, even those we deem to be negative, persist because they meet a need, and often that need is unconscious and, perhaps, developed as a child in order to feel loved, safe, and that we belong to our families." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "What I often say is there are three basic risks that we’re all trying to manage all the time: love, safety, and belonging." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "the suffering that I encounter can almost always be rooted back to somebody not saying something that needs to be said." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "the process by which the masks we wear, concealing ourselves from others but most importantly ourselves, are slowly and compassionately stripped away so there’s no place left to hide." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "The word radical here doesn’t mean harsh or extreme. It means going to the root." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "The goal of asking how you’ve been complicit is not to arrive at a verdict. It’s to restore a sense of authorship." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "When most people hear complicit, they assume “guilt” or responsibility." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "Jerry reflects on how his signature question has drifted from its original intent over the years" (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "unusual yet highly effective blend of Buddhism, Jungian therapy, and entrepreneurial straight talk" (https://www.reboot.io/reboot-leadership-and-the-art-of-growing-up/) "Since 2014, we’ve created a full suite of coaching experiences" (https://www.reboot.io/about/) "The necessary first step is for leaders and others who hold power to see themselves clearly." (https://www.reboot.io/reunion-leadership-and-the-longing-to-belong/) "create systemic belonging—homes—for everyone." (https://www.reboot.io/reunion-leadership-and-the-longing-to-belong/) "the American transformation canon's radical interiority of cause" (notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md) |
| Date | 2019 (Reboot: Leadership and the Art of Growing Up, HarperCollins); 2023 (Reunion: Leadership and the Longing to Belong, HarperCollins) |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault holds Colonna only through the corpus's quotation of his question in three documents. His own texts add a theory of why patterns persist, a claim about withheld speech, and a systemic extension that the corpus's sort does not record. |
| Sources | https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/; https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/; https://www.reboot.io/reboot-leadership-and-the-art-of-growing-up/; https://www.reboot.io/reunion-leadership-and-the-longing-to-belong/; https://www.reboot.io/about/; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=reboot+leadership+art+of+growing+up+colonna&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher&limit=2; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=reunion+leadership+longing+to+belong+colonna&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher&limit=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *radical self-inquiry* | Radical self-inquiry is Colonna's name for a slow, compassionate practice of seeing past the masks a person wears, 'radical' meaning going to the root. |
| *complicit* | Being complicit means enabling or taking part in a situation, as distinct from being its main cause. |
| *love, safety, and belonging* | Love, safety, and belonging are the three needs Colonna says every person is trying to protect. |
| *systemic belonging* | Systemic belonging is the goal Reunion sets for leaders: organizations and systems in which everyone belongs. |

### “How have I been complicit in creating the conditions I say I don’t want?”

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Colonna asks it once a client trusts him, when the client describes conditions they say they do not want. |
| What the coach does | Colonna's signature question asks a leader, 'How have I been complicit in creating the conditions I say I don’t want?' He chooses 'complicit' rather than 'responsible' on purpose, because most people first hear the question as blame, and because complicity relieves the person of responsibility for everything while letting them see how they are 'somehow being served' by their difficulty. Reboot's page gives his image of the getaway driver, 'present, involved, but not the one holding up the bank', and calls the question 'inquiry, not inquisition'. He asks it once trust and empathy are established, and he does not step in to fix. |
| Steps | (1) Colonna first establishes 'a certain level of trust and relating through empathy' and does not try to step in and fix the problem. (2) He asks the question in its usual form: 'How have I been complicit in creating the conditions I say I don’t want?' (3) He separates complicit from responsible, since, he says, 90% of the time people first hear 'How have I been responsible for the conditions?' (4) He invites the person to hold the question longer than feels comfortable and to observe their own actions rather than judge them. (5) He frames what the person finds as authorship: they have been part of creating the situation, so they can be part of creating something different. |
| What it does | The question asks a person to find their own part in a situation they dislike without taking the blame for all of it. |
| What is facilitated | The question makes a leader's own seeing of their part in a situation more likely without assigning fault; the seeing and the answer are the leader's. Colonna supplies the question and the distinction between complicit and responsible. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Colonna asks the question and the client finds the answer, after trust is established. |
| The evidence | Colonna's coaching experience is the only support in the sources opened; Reboot's page reports that the question is often misheard as an accusation. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The question leaves the discovery of the leader's own part to the leader and frames it as authorship restored rather than a verdict. Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Choosing 'complicit' over 'responsible' keeps other causes of a situation in view while asking about the person's part. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Colonna's split between complicit and responsible is close to the vault's note placing sovereignty between 'everything happens to me' and 'everything is my responsibility'. The corpus files the question under a camp it charges with placing all cause inside the person. |
| Limits and critiques | Reboot's page states that 'if complicity becomes “this is all my fault,” the question shuts us down to inquiry before it can do anything useful', and that shame 'tends to keep us exactly where we are'. |
| Quoted from its sources | "So the way I usually ask the question goes like this: “How have I been complicit in creating the conditions I say I don’t want?”" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "I like to use the word complicit and not responsible" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "90% of the time when I first ask that question, people hear the word “How have I been responsible for the conditions?”" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "Complicitness is important, because it’s relieving the person from the burden of feeling responsible for all the shit in their lives, because that’s not fair to carry that responsibility." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "But it’s helpful to think of ourselves as somehow being served by the challenges that we’re going through." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "once you’ve established a certain level of trust and relating through empathy, and don’t necessarily try to step in and fix it" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "To enable or to take part in." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "present, involved, but not the one holding up the bank" (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "It is inquiry, not inquisition." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "To hold a question longer than feels comfortable and see what surfaces when you stop trying to answer it." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "A more useful approach is to become a casual observer of your own actions rather than a judge of them." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "you have been part of creating this, which means you can be part of creating something different." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "But if complicity becomes “this is all my fault,” the question shuts us down to inquiry before it can do anything useful" (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "Shame, for all its intensity, tends to keep us exactly where we are." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "how am I complicit in creating the conditions I say I don't want?" (notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md) "Everything happens to me" (notes/sovereignty occupies the middle between everything happens to me and everything is my responsibility.md) "Everything is my responsibility" (notes/sovereignty occupies the middle between everything happens to me and everything is my responsibility.md) "Colonna’s “how have I been complicit?”" (.corpus/the arc and the gloss - second edition.md) |
| Date | 2019 (Reboot; Colonna's account of it on The Tim Ferriss Show, June 2019) |
| In the vault | `notes/sovereignty occupies the middle between everything happens to me and everything is my responsibility.md`; The vault's note on sovereignty, still nascent, meaning stated but not yet substantiated by a reading, describes the same middle Colonna reaches by separating complicit from responsible. The corpus's wording of the question in walked IX and atlas §IX.5 ('how am I complicit') differs from Colonna's ('How have I been complicit'); the arc-gloss's 'how have I been complicit?' is nearer his own. |
| Sources | https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/; https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/; https://www.reboot.io/reboot-leadership-and-the-art-of-growing-up/; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=reboot+leadership+art+of+growing+up+colonna&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher&limit=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *complicit* | Complicit, in Reboot's definition, means to enable or take part in, like the driver of a getaway car. |
| *responsible* | Responsible, in the same definition, means to be the main cause, like the robber who holds up the bank. |
| *inquiry, not inquisition* | 'Inquiry, not inquisition' is Colonna's phrase for asking the question with tenderness rather than accusation. |

### The four questions of Reboot

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Colonna and Reboot offer them to leaders as questions to return to rather than as a single exercise. |
| What the coach does | In Reboot, Colonna poses four questions that, in the words of Reboot's page, 'form the core of this inquiry': 'What’s being said that I’m not hearing?', 'What am I saying that’s not being heard?', 'What am I not saying that needs to be said?', and the complicity question. The page says they are not meant to be answered quickly, in order, or all at once, and describes them as a place to keep returning. Colonna told Tim Ferriss that in the crisis that led him to coaching, 'the way I was complicit was I wasn’t speaking.' |
| Steps | (1) The person holds a situation in mind. (2) The person asks 'What’s being said that I’m not hearing?' (3) The person asks 'What am I saying that’s not being heard?' (4) The person asks 'What am I not saying that needs to be said?' (5) The person asks 'How have I been complicit in creating the conditions I say I don’t want?' (6) The person returns to the questions over time, moving, in the page's words, from judge to observer. |
| What it does | The questions turn a person's attention to what they are not hearing, what of theirs is not being heard, and what they are not saying. |
| What is facilitated | The questions make a person's own noticing of what goes unheard and unsaid more likely; the person supplies all the content. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person asks the questions of themselves and returns to them over time; no facilitator is required. |
| The evidence | The source is Reboot's page and Colonna's interview; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Conflict: The questions examine a disagreement through what each side is not hearing and not saying. Being seen and recognized by another: Two of the questions ask whether one is heard and whether one is hearing, which is the condition for being recognized by another. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book is read by one person alone, while three of Colonna's four questions presuppose another person to hear and be heard by. |
| Quoted from its sources | "In his first book, Reboot , Jerry poses four questions that form the core of this inquiry." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "What’s being said that I’m not hearing?" (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "What am I saying that’s not being heard?" (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "What am I not saying that needs to be said?" (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "They’re not meant to be answered quickly, or in order, or all at once." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "Think of them less as a checklist and more as a place to keep returning" (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "The shift from judge to observer is smaller than it sounds, and it changes everything." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "So for me, the way I was complicit was I wasn’t speaking." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) |
| Date | 2019 (Reboot: Leadership and the Art of Growing Up) |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no note on these questions; the corpus quotes only the fourth. |
| Sources | https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/; https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=reboot+leadership+art+of+growing+up+colonna&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher&limit=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *from judge to observer* | Moving from judge to observer means noticing one's own story from a little distance without judging what one finds. |

### “How is that being busy serving you?”

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Colonna asks it of a client who describes being overwhelmed or busy, once trust is established. |
| What the coach does | Asked how he would work with a busy, overwhelmed person, Colonna says that after establishing trust and empathy, and without trying to fix it, 'the first question I would start to ask or elicit is: “How is that being busy serving you?”' He explains that busyness can feel good inside ('Look at all the great stuff I got done') and look important outside ('Look at how busy I am. I must be important'). He also asks a person in pain, 'How’s it working for you? Because if it’s not working for you, why are you in pain? Why are you doing it? And would you like a little relief?' |
| Steps | (1) Colonna establishes trust and relates through empathy without trying to fix the problem. (2) He asks: 'How is that being busy serving you?' (3) He explores how the pattern pays inside, 'Look at all the great stuff I got done', and outside, 'Look at how busy I am. I must be important.' (4) He asks whether it is working: 'How’s it working for you? Because if it’s not working for you, why are you in pain? Why are you doing it? And would you like a little relief?' |
| What it does | The question asks what a costly habit is giving the person. |
| What is facilitated | The question makes a person's own discovery of what a pattern does for them more likely; the answer is the person's. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Colonna asks what a pattern gives the person, and the person finds the answer. |
| The evidence | The source is Colonna's interview; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The question treats a costly pattern as doing something for the person, which is the vault's racket question in Colonna's words. Timing and pacing: Colonna asks it only after trust is established, which makes timing part of the move. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The question treats busyness as the person's own pattern and does not ask what in the workplace requires it, which is the structural question the corpus says the coaching field leaves out. |
| Quoted from its sources | "the first question I would start to ask or elicit is: “How is that being busy serving you?”" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "Like, “Look at all the great stuff I got done.” Externally, “Look at how busy I am. I must be important.”" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "“How’s it working for you? Because if it’s not working for you, why are you in pain? Why are you doing it? And would you like a little relief?”" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "The executive who says they’re exhausted and overwhelmed keeps scheduling more meetings." (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) "what does the hurting pay?" (notes/the racket is the attractor state with a salary.md) |
| Date | 2019 (The Tim Ferriss Show, episode 373) |
| In the vault | `notes/the racket is the attractor state with a salary.md`; The vault holds Landmark's diagnostic 'what does the hurting pay?'; Colonna's question asks the same of busyness in plain words. |
| Sources | https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/; https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *serving* | A pattern 'serves' a person when it gives them something, such as a feeling of importance, that keeps them repeating it. |

### Letting the crow speak

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Colonna uses it in his morning journaling, which he does before meditating, and teaches it for self-criticism and anxiety. |
| What the coach does | Colonna takes from his poetry teacher Marie Ponsot the image of 'the crow who sits on your shoulder' telling you your work is worthless. In his journal he sometimes takes a second pen of another color and lets the crow speak, rather than trying to silence it, which he calls 'a jiu-jitsu move'. Then he makes what he calls 'the move to make': recognizing that 'The crow’s mission is to preserve your ability to be loved, to feel safe, and that you belong', thanking it, and deciding to 'love them from afar' about the people the crow fears will be angry. |
| Steps | (1) In a journal, the person takes a second pen of a different color. (2) The person lets the critical voice write in its own words. (3) The person does not try to silence the voice; Colonna says the mistake people make is to 'throw rocks at the crow and shut the crow up'. (4) The person names the voice's aim: 'The crow’s mission is to preserve your ability to be loved, to feel safe, and that you belong.' (5) The person answers it, in Colonna's words: 'Thanks a lot, buddy. I really appreciate it.' (6) The person reminds themselves that the people who might be angry 'don’t really see me', and resolves to love them from afar. |
| What it does | The practice lets the inner critic speak on paper, names its protective purpose, and answers it with thanks. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it possible for a person to hear their self-criticism as protective and to answer it; the voice and the answer are the person's. Colonna supplies the image, the second pen, and the reframing of the voice's purpose. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person does this alone in a journal; Colonna teaches it but is not present. |
| The evidence | The source is Colonna's description of his own practice; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The practice reads the inner critic as protective, 'The crow is trying to keep you safe', and answers it with thanks rather than suppression. The container that holds people while they change: The journal and a second pen let a hostile voice write in its own words, in a separate color, while the person answers it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The practice aims at relief from a critic that interferes with a leader's work, while the book's aim is recognition; the two share the reading of the critic as protective. |
| Quoted from its sources | "She used to talk all the time about the crow who sits on your shoulder telling you what a piece of shit you are" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "sometimes I’ll take a second pen so that there are two different colors — I will allow the crow to speak." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "This is a jiu-jitsu move." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "the mistake I think a lot of people make is they try to throw rocks at the crow and shut the crow up" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "Now this is the move to make." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "The crow is trying to keep you safe." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "“Thanks a lot, buddy. I really appreciate it." ### The anxiety questions

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Colonna uses them when anxiety arises, in the journaling he does before meditation. |
| What the coach does | When anxiety rises, Colonna asks it questions: 'What’s the threat? What am I afraid of? Have I heard this before?' He links his rage, migraines, and anxiety to not speaking, and says that giving voice to the feelings, in a journal or aloud to another person, did not make them go away but lessened their power. He explains the questions as work on the amygdala that 'fire off the prefrontal cortex', an explanation the sources opened do not test. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices anxiety rising. (2) The person asks: 'What’s the threat?' (3) The person asks: 'What am I afraid of?' (4) The person asks: 'Have I heard this before?' (5) The person writes or says what surfaces. |
| What it does | The questions ask an anxious mind to name its threat and whether it has heard the same alarm before. |
| What is facilitated | The questions make it more likely that a person can tell a present threat from an old one; the person supplies the answers. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person asks the questions of their own anxiety. |
| The evidence | Colonna's explanation in terms of the amygdala and the prefrontal cortex is his own; the sources opened contain no test of these questions. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The third question asks whether a present alarm is an old protection firing again. Knowing that something happened: The questions give a person a way to tell whether a threat is present or remembered. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book asks its reader to meet a contraction with attention; Colonna's questions do so as well but frame the work as calming a threat response rather than as recognition. |
| Quoted from its sources | "“What’s the threat? What am I afraid of? Have I heard this before?”" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "Those questions fire off the prefrontal cortex, which can relieve the anxiety." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "And that didn’t magically make them go away, but it lessened the power of that anxiety." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "And by linking speaking to the rage and to the migraines and to the anxiety, I gave voice to the feelings." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) |
| Date | 2019 (The Tim Ferriss Show, episode 373) |
| In the vault | `notes/suffering is contraction never corruption.md`; The vault's doctrine reads suffering as a protective contraction; Colonna's third question asks the reader to check whether a protection is responding to the present or to the past. |
| Sources | https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *anxiety questions* | The anxiety questions are Colonna's three questions for identifying what an anxious feeling is responding to. |

### Asking what made a person choose their career, and handing them a book (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | He used it when a young professional asked him for help finding a new job. |
| What the coach does | Colonna tells how, before he trained as a coach, a young lawyer came to him to network for a startup job, and he said, 'I’m happy to help you, but just answer a question for me.' and then asked 'What made you [decide] to become a lawyer in the first place?' The man cried and spoke about pleasing his father; Colonna handed him Parker Palmer's Let Your Life Speak and said 'Here, read this. And then get back to me.' Colonna calls it 'kind of my first coaching question' and says the encounter led him to sign up for coach training within days, and that what he felt first was empathy because his own experience was so similar. |
| Steps | (1) Colonna agrees to help but asks one question first: 'I’m happy to help you, but just answer a question for me.' (2) He asks: 'What made you [decide] to become a lawyer in the first place?' (3) He listens while the person tells how they came to their career. (4) He offers a book rather than advice: 'Here, read this. And then get back to me.' |
| What it does | The question turns a request for help with a job into a look at why the person chose their career. |
| What is facilitated | The question makes a person's own review of how they came to their present life more likely; the answer and the next step are theirs. Colonna supplied a question and a book, not direction. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Colonna asked one question and gave a book; the young man supplied the answer and decided what to do next. |
| The evidence | The source is a single anecdote told by Colonna; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: Colonna answers a request for help with a question and a book and leaves the next step to the person. Recognition: Colonna describes recognizing himself in the young man: 'even though the content of the story was different, my experience was so similar.' |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move depends on a live encounter in which the person's tears are met; a book can offer the question but not the meeting. |
| Quoted from its sources | "“I’m happy to help you, but just answer a question for me.” It’s kind of my first coaching question, right?" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "“What made you [decide] to become a lawyer in the first place?”" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "I reached up to the shelf, and I pulled down a copy of Let Your Life Speak , and I said, “Here, read this. And then get back to me.”" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "I knew his feelings, because even though the content of the story was different, my experience was so similar." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "And so within a few days I had signed up for a coach training program." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "Probably four or five years later, I’m still working my way through all of the issues that I’m carrying at that point" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) |

### Learning to speak like an adult

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | He uses it when someone close to him has said something that hurt or frightened him. |
| What the coach does | Colonna describes the move of saying a feeling to the other person instead of withdrawing: 'Hey, that hurt me.' Or, 'Hey, I’m scared. That thing that you said last night scared me.' He adds the intention to stay: he would normally withdraw and cut off connection, 'but I’m going to stay here and be an adult and engage with you.' He says this does not make the anxiety go away but 'puts the adult me back in control'. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices the impulse to withdraw after being hurt or frightened. (2) The person names the feeling to the person involved: 'Hey, that hurt me.' or 'Hey, I’m scared.' (3) The person names their usual move, to withdraw and cut off connection. (4) The person states the choice to stay and engage. |
| What it does | The move names a hurt or fear to the person involved and states the choice to stay rather than withdraw. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it more likely that a relationship continues through a hurt; the feeling and the choice are the speaker's. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person decides what to say and to whom. |
| The evidence | The source is Colonna's account of his own practice; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Conflict: The move replaces withdrawal with a first-person statement of hurt or fear and a stated choice to stay. The container that holds people while they change: Staying in contact while hurt keeps the relationship going, so that change can happen within it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move requires another person to speak to, while the book is read alone; a text can prepare the words but not the encounter. |
| Quoted from its sources | "“Hey, that hurt me.” Or, “Hey, I’m scared. That thing that you said last night scared me." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "which is withdraw and cut off connection to you, but I’m going to stay here and be an adult and engage with you." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "That move, it doesn’t make the anxiety go away, but it puts me back in control. Puts the adult me back in control." (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) "the courage it takes to name what's true in a room" (https://www.reboot.io/how-have-you-been-complicit/) |
| Date | 2019 (The Tim Ferriss Show, episode 373) |
| In the vault | `notes/the third entity's failure mode is withdrawal and its core skill is staying.md`; The vault holds that the relational field's failure mode is withdrawal and its core skill is staying; Colonna's move states that skill as a sentence a person can say. |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|

### The complicity question extended to systems (Reunion)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Reunion offers the questions to leaders who hold power in organizations. |
| What the coach does | According to Reboot's page for the book, Reunion broadens the complicity question to 'How have I been complicit in maintaining systems of oppression that I say I don’t want?' and adds 'What do I need to give up that I love in order to have the systems of belonging that I want?' The page says the book guides leaders toward reunion with disowned parts of themselves, with 'the myths and truths of our ancestors', and with those most affected by systems of exclusion. It names two steps: leaders who hold power see themselves clearly, and then they see and change the effects of their unhealed wounds and beliefs on the people they lead. |
| Steps | (1) The leader asks: 'How have I been complicit in maintaining systems of oppression that I say I don’t want?' (2) The leader asks: 'What do I need to give up that I love in order to have the systems of belonging that I want?' (3) The leader sees themselves clearly, which the page calls the necessary first step. (4) The leader sees and changes the effects of their untended wounds and beliefs on those they lead. |
| What it does | The questions ask a leader how they help keep exclusionary systems in place and what they would give up to change them. |
| What is facilitated | The questions make a leader's own seeing of their part in, and benefit from, exclusionary systems more likely; the leader supplies the answers. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The questions are posed to the leader, who supplies the answers. |
| The evidence | The source is the description on Reboot's own site; the book itself was not read, and the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: The questions treat systems of oppression as real and ask about the person's part in maintaining them. Change at the scale of a team or organization: The book aims at reimagining businesses as collectives where a shared sense of belonging thrives. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus lists Colonna among teachers who share a blind spot for structural suffering; in Reunion he treats systems of oppression as real, though he still reaches them through the leader's self-inquiry. |
| Quoted from its sources | "How have I been complicit in maintaining systems of oppression that I say I don’t want?" (https://www.reboot.io/reunion-leadership-and-the-longing-to-belong/) "What do I need to give up that I love in order to have the systems of belonging that I want?" (https://www.reboot.io/reunion-leadership-and-the-longing-to-belong/) "with the disowned parts of ourselves, the myths and truths of our ancestors, as well as a deeper connection with those most affected by systems of exclusion" (https://www.reboot.io/reunion-leadership-and-the-longing-to-belong/) "The vital second step is to see and alter the effects of one’s untended, unhealed wounds and beliefs on those we are tasked to lead." (https://www.reboot.io/reunion-leadership-and-the-longing-to-belong/) "reimagine businesses as collectives where a shared sense of belonging thrives" (https://www.reboot.io/reunion-leadership-and-the-longing-to-belong/) "Jerry’s second book builds on this question, asking us what benefit we get from the conditions we say we don’t want." (https://www.reboot.io/reunion-leadership-and-the-longing-to-belong/) "radical interiority of cause" (notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md) |
| Date | 2023 (Reunion: Leadership and the Longing to Belong, HarperCollins) |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault note records the corpus's charge of 'radical interiority of cause' against all five camps and adds Hübl, Rosa, and Jain as holders of the external view. Colonna's Reunion puts one of the five camps' own holders partway toward that view, which the note does not record. |
| Sources | https://www.reboot.io/reunion-leadership-and-the-longing-to-belong/; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=reunion+leadership+longing+to+belong+colonna&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher&limit=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *systemic belonging* | Systemic belonging names organizations and systems in which everyone, including those who have been excluded, has a place. |
| *reunion* | Reunion, in the book's sense, is reconnection with disowned parts of oneself, with one's ancestors' stories, and with people excluded by systems. |

### Reboot bootcamps

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Reboot runs bootcamps for startup leaders, co-founders, venture investors, and, as the Reunion Bootcamp, on the themes of the second book. |
| What the coach does | Reboot's bootcamps gather a leader with up to 15 other founders, executives, or investors for about four days away from work. The page says they 'blend practical skills, radical self-inquiry and shared experiences to enhance leadership and resiliency', and that participants leave with a strategy for the leader they want to be and a network of peers. It names a tradition 'to cut through our own bullshit': early on the team creates the safety for people to look squarely at the truth of who they are and what their company is, which Reboot calls its 'This Being So, So What ? Moment'. The page states the goal as showing participants that they already have the capability to withstand the storms of their lives. |
| Steps | (1) Reboot gathers a leader with up to 15 peers for several days away from work. (2) Early on, the team creates the safety for people to stop pretending. (3) Participants look squarely at the truth of who they are and what their company is, in what Reboot calls the 'This Being So, So What ? Moment'. (4) Participants explore what they are up to so that their choices become conscious. (5) Participants leave with a strategy for the leader they want to be and a network of peers. |
| What it does | The retreat puts a leader among up to fifteen peers for several days of skills, self-inquiry, and shared experience. |
| What is facilitated | The bootcamp makes a group of peers into a setting where each leader can see their own habits and choices; the content is the leaders'. Reboot supplies the place, the safety, and a structure that combines skills and inquiry. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Reboot's team designs the retreat and creates safety, and the participants supply what they look at in themselves. |
| The evidence | The page offers participant testimonials only; one participant writes that 'the magic comes not from any particular exercises or tools, it comes from the people involved.' |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The page names creating safety early as the condition for people to stop the nonsense and tell the truth. Recognition: The stated goal is to show participants a capability they already have and simply need to access. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The bootcamp's aim is resilience and effectiveness in leadership, while the book's promised event is recognition for its own sake; the page's claim that participants already have what they need is the nearest point of contact. |
| Limits and critiques | The page's evidence is testimonials; it names the objection that participants must spend four days away from company, family, and inbox. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Join the Reboot Team and immerse yourself in the complexity and vulnerability of being a leader and founder with up to 15 other smart and courageous entrepreneurs." (https://www.reboot.io/service/bootcamps/) "They blend practical skills, radical self-inquiry and shared experiences to enhance leadership and resiliency." (https://www.reboot.io/service/bootcamps/) "One of the traditions at the Reboot boot camps is to cut through our own bullshit." (https://www.reboot.io/service/bootcamps/) "Early on, we create the safety for people to stop the nonsense–to look squarely at the truth of who they are and what the company is." (https://www.reboot.io/service/bootcamps/) "We call this our This Being So, So What ? Moment." (https://www.reboot.io/service/bootcamps/) "to show you that you already have the capability to withstand the storms of your lives. You simply need to access it." (https://www.reboot.io/service/bootcamps/) "Spend four days away from my company, away from my family, away from my inbox to look deeply into my own heart and figure out who I am as a leader?" (https://www.reboot.io/service/bootcamps/) "the magic comes not from any particular exercises or tools, it comes from the people involved." (https://www.reboot.io/service/bootcamps/) "Since 2014, we’ve created a full suite of coaching experiences" (https://www.reboot.io/about/) |
| Date | Reboot founded 2014; bootcamps continuing (a 2027 Leadership Bootcamp is announced) |
| In the vault | `notes/you are already free and the work removes a misunderstanding rather than adding an attainment.md`; The vault's note holds that the work removes a misunderstanding rather than adding an attainment; Reboot's stated goal, to show leaders a capability they already have, makes a similar claim about resilience rather than freedom. |

|---|---|

## The Conscious Leadership Group (Jim Dethmer, Diana Chapman, Kaley Warner Klemp)

| Move | Type | When the coach uses it | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| To me, by me, through me, as me: drama and presence in the Conscious Leadership Group's model | a theory | The group teaches this model in its book, trainings, and coaching as the frame for every practice it offers. | The model gives a person two named states to check themselves against and a set of commitments that describe the move from one to the other. |
| Locating yourself: above or below the line, with shift moves | a move | The coach uses the question at the start of an exploration of a leader's issue, and a person can use it at any moment they notice reactivity. | The move asks a person to name which of two states they are in and offers physical and verbal ways to move from one to the other. |
| The fifteen commitments, each paired with its 'to me' opposite | a technique | The group uses the list in its book and trainings, and Dethmer uses it when someone says conscious leadership is not for them. | The list asks a person or team to see which of two opposed commitments their results show and to choose. |
| Stepping into 100% responsibility (the practice for commitment one) | a technique | The group uses it with a person who brings an issue they experience as happening to them. | The practice moves a person from telling an issue as blame to naming, in their own words, how they keep it going and what it gets them. |
| Playing the drama triangle | a technique | The group uses it in every training and with teams working on a shared issue. | The exercise has a person act out their reactive story in three roles, exaggerated, until they can see the pattern and their own part in it. |
| The whole body yes | a move | The coach uses it when a person faces a decision and wants to 'get this right', and when a person makes or reviews an agreement. | The move has a person picture each option in turn, compare what their body does, and take the option the whole body agrees with. |
| Speaking unarguably (the practice for commitment four, candor) | a technique | The group offers it as a practice through the day, alone at first and then aloud with colleagues who are practicing candor. | The practice replaces claims about others with reports of one's own sensations, thoughts, and feelings. |
| Feeling feelings all the way through to completion (the practice for commitment three) | a technique | The group offers it as a daily practice and for any moment a feeling arises. | The practice has a person name a feeling, find it in the body, and breathe, move, or sound it until it releases. |
| The Clearing Model | a technique | The group uses it when a person feels disconnected from a colleague or partner, and advises waiting until the person has shifted above the line. | The model has one person disclose facts, stories, feelings, wants, their own part, and their projections while the other only reflects and asks for more. |
| Masterful appreciation (the practice for commitment seven) | a technique | The group offers it as a daily practice for individuals and as a thirty-day program for companies. | The practice has a person deliver three specific, sincere appreciations a day and count them with coins. |
| Owning your victimhood | a move | The coach uses it with a person who is overwhelmed and is denying that they feel like a victim. | The move has a person admit, without judgment, that they feel at the mercy of a situation before any attempt to shift. |

### To me, by me, through me, as me: drama and presence in the Conscious Leadership Group's model

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| When the coach uses it | The group teaches this model in its book, trainings, and coaching as the frame for every practice it offers. |
| What the coach does | The group teaches that at any moment a person is either below the line, experiencing life as happening to them and defending being right, or above the line, taking full responsibility and treating events as chances to learn. In its own words it places the source of suffering in stories rather than facts: its clearing model says that stories 'are the source of all drama, conflict, pain and suffering' and that feelings come from the stories people make up. It names four ways of being, 'To Me', 'By Me', 'Through Me' and 'As Me', calls them 'states of consciousness and not stages', and describes the last as 'the consciousness of oneness or nonduality'. It defines commitment 'as an energetic experience and not a moral issue' and holds that a person can tell what they are committed to by their results. |
| What it does | The model gives a person two named states to check themselves against and a set of commitments that describe the move from one to the other. |
| What is facilitated | The model is meant to make a leader's own shift from reactivity to learning more likely, and the shift belongs to the leader, since the group holds that each person chooses where they locate themselves. The coach supplies the distinctions and the practices; the group does not supply the content of a person's story or what they learn from it. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. The group offers its two-state model to leaders and teams, and each person decides which state they are in and whether to shift. |
| The evidence | The model rests on the founders' coaching of leaders and teams; the sources opened report no outcome study. The group names its debts on its own site: to Gay and Kathlyn Hendricks for much of the commitments, to Michael Beckwith for the four ways of being, and to Stephen Karpman and David Emerald for the drama triangle. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: The group places cause and control in the person by design, and Dethmer writes that radical responsibility does not depend on a person's role or decision rights. Remaining the author of one's own change: The model makes authorship its subject, and its last two ways of being describe authorship giving way to surrender and then to oneness. Recognition: The group's description of 'as me', 'nothing to do to become who you already are', states in plain words the kind of recognition the book promises. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | In its own words the group does not locate suffering only in disowned complicity, the corpus's fourth theory: its clearing model locates it in believed stories, its third commitment in resisted feelings, and its tenth commitment uses Byron Katie's four questions, so its practice spans the corpus's first, second, and fourth theories. Its four ways of being end in surrender and oneness, while the book's arc ends in integration, a return to ordinary life. |
| Limits and critiques | Dethmer reports that 'about 50% of the people I coached one on one in organizations—and about 20% of people in intimate relationships— chose to leave the organization and ended the relationship', and he writes that conscious leadership is 'not for most people'. He adds that no one he can think of who left an organization regrets it, while those who ended relationships are mostly glad, 'though that isn’t always the case for their partner'. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Stories are the source of all drama, conflict, pain and suffering." (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "Feelings come from the stories we make up, not from the facts."
