| Sources | https://conscious.is/15-commitments; https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/15-Commitments.pdf; https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/; https://conscious.is/blog/going-deeper-with-the-15-commitments-4-ways-of-being-with-new-years-resolutions/; https://conscious.is/blog/conscious-leadership-isnt-for-everyone/; https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-1-responsibility/; https://conscious.is/blog/why-you-shouldnt-take-radical-responsibility-for-your-life/; https://conscious.is/blog/a-bow-to-gay-katie-hendricks/; https://conscious.is/about/jim-dethmer/; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=15+commitments+of+conscious+leadership&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher,publish_year,isbn&limit=5 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *to me* | 'To me' names the state in which a person experiences the cause of their experience as outside themselves; the group calls it victim consciousness. |
| *by me* | 'By me' names the state in which a person takes responsibility for being the creator of their life and lets go of blame, including self-blame. |
| *through me* | 'Through me' names a state that follows a choice to surrender, in which a person listens for what wants to happen rather than declaring goals. |
| *as me* | 'As me' names what the group calls the consciousness of oneness or nonduality, in which nothing is lacking. |
| *drama* | Drama is the group's word for reactive, defensive living below the line, which it contrasts with presence. |

### Locating yourself: above or below the line, with shift moves

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | The coach uses the question at the start of an exploration of a leader's issue, and a person can use it at any moment they notice reactivity. |
| What the coach does | The coach opens with 'Where are you?', and the person answers above or below the line and then looks at which of the three victim roles, victim, villain, or hero, they are playing. The group's handout lists statements, behaviors, and beliefs that mark each side, for example 'It’s not my fault' below the line and 'What can I learn from this?' above it. For a person below the line the handout lists shift moves, among them breathing, 'Significantly change my posture', questioning one's beliefs, and speaking unarguably. The group's willingness questions ask whether the person is fully willing to shift before they try, because 'our heads say yes'. |
| Steps | (1) The coach asks the first question the group asks leaders in an exploration: 'Where are you?' (2) The person answers above or below the line, and then looks at whether they are in the victim, villain, or hero role. (3) The person compares their own words with the handout's lists; below the line they may hear themselves saying 'I have to', 'It’s not my fault', or 'You made me', and above the line 'What can I learn from this?' or 'How is this familiar?' (4) If the person is below the line, they choose a shift move from the handout, such as 'Breathe', 'Significantly change my posture', 'Question my beliefs', 'Feel emotions', 'Speak unarguably', 'Appreciate', or 'Play'. (5) Before shifting, the person asks the group's willingness questions, such as 'Are you willing to let go of being right?' |
| What it does | The move asks a person to name which of two states they are in and offers physical and verbal ways to move from one to the other. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes a person's noticing of their own state quicker and more exact; the person does the locating and the choosing. The coach supplies the two categories and the list of shift moves, not the answer. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. The coach offers the two positions and the list of shift moves, and the person decides which position they are in and which move to try. |
| The evidence | The sources are the group's handouts and blog; the sources opened contain no outcome study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The handout's lists give a person observable signs, in their own words and posture, of which state they are in. Protection and defense: The model treats defensiveness as a state to notice and leave, while the book reads a contraction as protection to be understood before it opens. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book reads a contraction as protection that can open, while the group asks a person to shift out of a defensive state by choice. A 2025 post by one of the group's coaches moves closer to the book by acknowledging the defended state first (entry clg-owning-victimhood). |
| Limits and critiques | The group's drama-triangle handout lists 'You are below the line.' among the things a villain says, which records the group's own awareness that its distinction can be used to blame others. |
| Quoted from its sources | "That’s why the first question we ask leaders doing an exploration is “Where are you?”" (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "It’s not my fault" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/Locating-Yourself.pdf) "What can I learn from this?" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/Locating-Yourself.pdf) "How is this familiar?" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/Locating-Yourself.pdf) "Significantly change my posture" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/Locating-Yourself.pdf) "Speak unarguably" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/Locating-Yourself.pdf) "Fight/Flee/Freeze/Faint" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/Locating-Yourself.pdf) "Are you willing to let go of being right?" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/gated/Willingness-Questions.pdf) "Most often we want to shift, our heads say yes." (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/gated/Willingness-Questions.pdf) "“You are below the line.”" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/gated/The-Drama-Triangle.pdf) "The 15 Commitments of Conscious Leadership , an Amazon bestseller (2015)." (https://conscious.is/about/jim-dethmer/) "They also taught us many other concepts, such as the Four Pillars of Integrity ; shift moves ; Fact vs Story ; and Masterful Appreciation." (https://conscious.is/blog/a-bow-to-gay-katie-hendricks/) "The Conscious Leadership Group, above and below the line" (.corpus/the whole field walked.md) |
| Date | 2015 (The 15 Commitments of Conscious Leadership, published by Dethmer, Chapman & Klemp; Open Library records 2015); the opening question quoted from a November 2023 post |
| In the vault | `notes/suffering is contraction never corruption.md`; The vault holds the group only as the corpus's phrase 'above and below the line'; the book's own reading of a defended state is in this note, which holds that suffering is contraction, almost always protective, rather than damage. |
| Sources | https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/Locating-Yourself.pdf; https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/gated/Willingness-Questions.pdf; https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/gated/The-Drama-Triangle.pdf; https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/; https://conscious.is/blog/a-bow-to-gay-katie-hendricks/; https://conscious.is/15-commitments; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=15+commitments+of+conscious+leadership&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher,publish_year,isbn&limit=5; https://conscious.is/about/jim-dethmer/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *locating* | Locating means noticing, in the moment, which side of the line one is on. |
| *shift moves* | Shift moves are the group's list of acts, such as breathing, changing posture, or appreciating someone, that help a person move from below the line to above it. |
| *willingness questions* | Willingness questions are yes-or-no questions the group asks before a shift, to find out whether a person is fully willing rather than willing only in their head. |

### The fifteen commitments, each paired with its 'to me' opposite

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The group uses the list in its book and trainings, and Dethmer uses it when someone says conscious leadership is not for them. |
| What the coach does | The group states fifteen commitments, each written twice: once as the 'by me' commitment and once as the 'to me' commitment a person may be living instead, for example 'I commit to being right and to seeing this situation as something that is happening to me.' A person or team reads the pairs and asks which one their results show, and Dethmer asks people to answer without judging either way of living as better. When someone says conscious leadership is not for them, he goes through the first seven pairs and asks 'Which of these is not for you? Or for your team?' By the group's own account, commitments one and two 'mirror' the Hendricks' work 'nearly word-for-word', commitment eight draws on Gay Hendricks's The Big Leap, and its practice for commitment ten is Byron Katie's The Work. |
| Steps | (1) The person reads a commitment and its opposite, for example the second commitment's 'to me' form: 'I commit to being right and to seeing this situation as something that is happening to me.' (2) The person looks at their results rather than their intentions and asks, in Dethmer's words, 'If I look at the results of my life and how I am actually living, what am I committed to?' (3) The person answers 'without any judgement that one way of living is better or worse than the other'. (4) With a team, the group holds 'a lively conversation about which of these commitments they want to characterize their workplace and culture'. (5) Where a commitment has its own practice, the person uses it; for the tenth, the group reproduces Byron Katie's four questions and turnaround. |
| What it does | The list asks a person or team to see which of two opposed commitments their results show and to choose. |
| What is facilitated | The pairs make a person's own choice between two ways of living explicit, and the person makes the choice. The group supplies the list and the practices and asks the person to answer without judgment. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. The group supplies the fifteen pairs, and the person or team decides which commitment their life or workplace shows and which they want. |
| The evidence | The sources are the group's book, handouts, and blog; the sources opened contain no outcome study. The group names its debts on its own site: to the Hendricks for commitments one, two, and eight and for the four pillars of integrity, and to Byron Katie for the practice of the tenth. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Decisions: The list turns a vague wish to change into a choice between named commitments, which a team can also make together. Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: The results test reads an outcome as evidence of the person's commitment, which places the cause in the person. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The vault's note on sovereignty places authorship between 'everything happens to me' and 'everything is my responsibility'; the group's 'to me' matches the first failure, and its results test leans toward the second. The book states no list of commitments for its reader. |
| Quoted from its sources | "I commit to being right and to seeing this situation as something that is happening to me." (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/15-Commitments.pdf) "committere, which means to gather one’s energy and move it in a chosen direction" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/15-Commitments.pdf) "If I look at the results of my life and how I am actually living, what am I committed to?" (https://conscious.is/blog/conscious-leadership-isnt-for-everyone/) "Try to answer without any judgement that one way of living is better or worse than the other." (https://conscious.is/blog/conscious-leadership-isnt-for-everyone/) "“Which of these is not for you? Or for your team?”" (https://conscious.is/blog/conscious-leadership-isnt-for-everyone/) "a lively conversation about which of these commitments they want to characterize their workplace and culture" (https://conscious.is/blog/conscious-leadership-isnt-for-everyone/) "Commitments 1 and 2— 100% Responsibility and Curiosity—mirror Gay and Katie’s work nearly word-for-word." (https://conscious.is/blog/a-bow-to-gay-katie-hendricks/) "Commitment 8, Zone of Genius, draws on Gay’s seminal book, “The Big Leap.”" (https://conscious.is/blog/a-bow-to-gay-katie-hendricks/) "Use “The Work” by Byron Katie to practice looking at the opposite of your stories." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-10-opposite-of-my-story/) "I commit to creating win for all solutions (win for me, win for the other person, win for the organization, and win for the whole)" (https://conscious.is/15-commitments) "Everything happens to me" (notes/sovereignty occupies the middle between everything happens to me and everything is my responsibility.md) "Everything is my responsibility" (notes/sovereignty occupies the middle between everything happens to me and everything is my responsibility.md) |
| Date | 2015 (The 15 Commitments of Conscious Leadership, published by Dethmer, Chapman & Klemp; Open Library records 2015); the 'not for everyone' post, May 2021 |
| In the vault | `notes/sovereignty occupies the middle between everything happens to me and everything is my responsibility.md`; The note, still nascent, meaning stated but not yet substantiated by a reading, places sovereignty between the passivity of 'everything happens to me' and the over-responsibility of 'everything is my responsibility'. The group names the same two failures in its own terms: taking less than 100% responsibility is the victim or villain, and taking more than 100% is the hero. |
| Sources | https://conscious.is/15-commitments; https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/15-Commitments.pdf; https://conscious.is/blog/conscious-leadership-isnt-for-everyone/; https://conscious.is/blog/a-bow-to-gay-katie-hendricks/; https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-10-opposite-of-my-story/; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=15+commitments+of+conscious+leadership&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher,publish_year,isbn&limit=5 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *commitment* | The group uses the Latin 'committere', 'to gather one’s energy and move it in a chosen direction', and treats a commitment as a fact about how energy is spent rather than a promise. |
| *unconscious commitment* | An unconscious commitment is the commitment a person's results reveal when it differs from the one they state. |
| *win for all* | 'Win for all' names the fourteenth commitment's aim of solutions that are a win for oneself, the other person, the organization, and the whole. |

### Stepping into 100% responsibility (the practice for commitment one)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The group uses it with a person who brings an issue they experience as happening to them. |
| What the coach does | The practice has a person state an issue first in 'unenlightened' terms, dramatically and with open blame, and then physically find a place in the room that stands for being 100% responsible. From that place the person completes five sentence stems, each several times, until something shifts, and returns to the start if nothing does. Dethmer later names a Level 2 of the practice in which a person asks how they created the circumstance itself, for example 'How did I create this situation?', and he says a person can take as much responsibility as they want, even for war or starvation. |
| Steps | (1) The person picks an issue and speaks about it in 'unenlightened' terms: 'Be dramatic. Ham it up. Blame overtly.' (2) The person physically finds a place in the room that represents their commitment to being 100% responsible for the situation. (3) The person completes each of these statements several times: 'From the past this reminds me of...', 'I keep this issue going by...', 'What I get from keeping this issue going is...', 'The lifelong pattern I'm noticing is...', and 'I can demonstrate 100% responsibility concerning this issue by...'. (4) If no shift comes, the person returns to the first step and repeats the process. (5) At what Dethmer calls Level 2, the person asks of a circumstance 'How did I create this situation?' or, of another person's behavior, how they are co-creating it. |
| What it does | The practice moves a person from telling an issue as blame to naming, in their own words, how they keep it going and what it gets them. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it more likely that a person discovers what they gain from keeping an issue going; the person supplies every answer. The coach supplies the stems and the move across the room, not the content. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The coach supplies the physical move and the sentence stems, and the person supplies every completion. |
| The evidence | The sources are the group's practice page and Dethmer's blog; the sources opened contain no outcome study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The stem 'What I get from keeping this issue going is...' asks what a pattern pays, which is the vault's racket question. Harm and its conditions: Dethmer's Level 2 example asks a person whose partner yells at them 'How am I showing up in this relationship such that I’m getting yelled at?', a question that could deepen harm for a person who is being mistreated. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book keeps the question of what a contraction protects but reads the contraction as protection rather than as something the person keeps going. Dethmer's Level 2 moves toward the over-responsibility the vault's sovereignty note names as a failure. |
| Limits and critiques | Dethmer writes that at Level 2 'You can take as much responsibility as you want for' the political environment, war, starvation, and sex trafficking, and he frames this as a game a person chooses to play. He also writes that taking responsibility 'isn’t about taking the blame'; the sources opened do not address how the Level 2 question should be used with a person in an abusive situation. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Speak about the issue in “unenlightened” terms. Be dramatic. Ham it up. Blame overtly." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-1-responsibility/) "Physically find a place in the room that represents your commitment to being 100% responsible for the situation." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-1-responsibility/) "From the past this reminds me of..." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-1-responsibility/) "I keep this issue going by..." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-1-responsibility/) "What I get from keeping this issue going is..." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-1-responsibility/) "The lifelong pattern I'm noticing is..." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-1-responsibility/) "I can demonstrate 100% responsibility concerning this issue by..." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-1-responsibility/) "If during Step 3, you don’t experience a shift, go back to Step 1 and repeat the process." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-1-responsibility/) "If you get fired, you ask, “How did I create this situation?”" (https://conscious.is/blog/why-you-shouldnt-take-radical-responsibility-for-your-life/) "If your partner yells at you, you ask, “How am I showing up in this relationship such that I’m getting yelled at?”" (https://conscious.is/blog/why-you-shouldnt-take-radical-responsibility-for-your-life/) "You can take as much responsibility as you want for:" (https://conscious.is/blog/why-you-shouldnt-take-radical-responsibility-for-your-life/) "Again, taking responsibility isn’t about taking the blame." (https://conscious.is/blog/why-you-shouldnt-take-radical-responsibility-for-your-life/) "You can take more than 100% responsibility, which is playing the hero (rescuing and offering temporary relief)." (https://conscious.is/blog/why-you-shouldnt-take-radical-responsibility-for-your-life/) "what does the hurting pay?" (notes/the racket is the attractor state with a salary.md) "“How is that being busy serving you?”" (https://tim.blog/2019/06/14/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-jerry-colonna-373/) |
| Date | 2015 (The 15 Commitments of Conscious Leadership, published by Dethmer, Chapman & Klemp; Open Library records 2015); Dethmer's Level 2 post, September 2024 |
| In the vault | `notes/the racket is the attractor state with a salary.md`; The vault holds the payoff question from Landmark's Forum, 'what does the hurting pay?'. The group's stem and Colonna's 'How is that being busy serving you?' ask the same question in the Hendricks and Conscious Leadership Group lineage and in Colonna's coaching; the sources opened do not say whether either took it from Landmark. |
| Sources | https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-1-responsibility/; https://conscious.is/blog/why-you-shouldnt-take-radical-responsibility-for-your-life/; https://conscious.is/15-commitments; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=15+commitments+of+conscious+leadership&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher,publish_year,isbn&limit=5 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *100% responsibility* | The group holds that taking more than 100% responsibility is the hero's role, taking less is the villain's or the victim's, and taking exactly 100% is seeing oneself as the creator of one's experience. |
| *Level 1 and Level 2* | Level 1 is responsibility for one's reactions to circumstances; Level 2 is curiosity about how one created the circumstances themselves. |
| *unenlightened terms* | Speaking in unenlightened terms means telling the issue as blame, dramatically, before the shift. |

### Playing the drama triangle

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The group uses it in every training and with teams working on a shared issue. |
| What the coach does | Each participant picks a real issue on which they are below the line and places three cards, victim, villain, and hero, on the floor. Each person has two minutes to stand on the triangle and tell the issue 'in dramatic exaggerated form', moving from base to base as they switch roles, while a witness says only 'Then what happens?' or 'Can you make it bigger?'. Dethmer writes that exaggeration lets a person see their drama as silly, which loosens it, and the group teaches Emerald's creator, coach, and challenger as the positions above the line. A team may then do the same with a shared business issue, two minutes each. |
| Steps | (1) The person picks a real-life issue on which they are below the line. (2) The person puts the victim, villain, and hero cards on the floor and steps onto the triangle; the group says 'The act of standing and stepping into the triangle is the beginning of ownership.' (3) For two minutes the person tells the issue in exaggerated form, moving their feet from base to base and role to role, sometimes with one foot on each of two bases. (4) A witness who gives no advice says only 'Then what happens?' when the person runs out of energy, or 'Can you make it bigger?'. (5) The person asks 'What roles are other people playing in your drama and how are you requiring them to play those roles?' (6) The person or team names the above-the-line roles from Emerald: creator, coach, and challenger. (7) A team repeats the exercise on a business issue that is real, current, and affecting all of them. |
| What it does | The exercise has a person act out their reactive story in three roles, exaggerated, until they can see the pattern and their own part in it. |
| What is facilitated | The exercise makes a person's own recognition of their reactive pattern more likely by having them enact and exaggerate it in front of a witness; the story and the insight are the person's. The witness supplies attention and two prompts only. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The facilitator supplies the three roles and the two-minute structure, and the person supplies the issue and discovers the pattern. |
| The evidence | Karpman's triangle comes from transactional analysis; Wikipedia's article notes that its Victim 'is not intended to represent an actual victim, but rather someone feeling or acting like one'. The sources opened contain no controlled study of the exercise, and the group reports that participants often resist it and rarely practice it after trainings. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Participation rather than watching: The exercise works only when the person stands, moves, and exaggerates in front of others; Dethmer says the benefits go up when a person takes that risk. Being seen and recognized by another: The witness is limited to two prompts so that being witnessed without advice is what helps the person see themselves. Harm and its conditions: Dethmer writes that telling how one sees an issue from below the line can be threatening to others on the team. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The group's version places the drama in the person's need to cast others in roles, while the Wikipedia account of Karpman's model describes a pattern among several people whose often unconscious needs keep it going. |
| Limits and critiques | Dethmer reports that 'Many people resist doing this activity especially if the training is happening at work', that people play 'with some cajoling by the facilitator', and that the exercise 'can be threatening to others on the team'. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Emerald added the roles of creator, coach, challenger (above the line) to victim, villain, hero (below the line)." (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "Learning the drama triangle and how to use it is part of every training CLG offers." (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "Everyone is given two minutes to stand on the triangle and talk about their issue in dramatic exaggerated form" (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "The act of standing and stepping into the triangle is the beginning of ownership." (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "They can only say one of two things: “Then what happens?” or “Can you make it bigger?”" (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "“What roles are other people playing in your drama and how are you requiring them to play those roles?”" (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "But, the learnings and potency go up dramatically when someone else is witnessing you do your drama." (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "Many people resist doing this activity especially if the training is happening at work" (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "With some cajoling by the facilitator and modeling by the team leader" (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "it can be threatening to others on the team" (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "Answering it honestly gives you tremendous insight into how your racket is showing up." (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "when I was first introduced to it by Katie Hendricks 20 years ago" (https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/) "The Victim in this model is not intended to represent an actual victim, but rather someone feeling or acting like one." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karpman_drama_triangle) |
| Date | 1968 (Karpman); 2009 (Emerald); the group's post on its use at work, November 2023 |
| In the vault | `notes/the racket is the attractor state with a salary.md`; The vault holds Landmark's racket as a fixed complaint with a fixed payoff; the group's post uses the same word for the pattern the triangle exposes. The vault holds no note on Karpman or Emerald. |
| Sources | https://conscious.is/blog/how-to-use-the-drama-triangle-at-work/; https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/gated/The-Drama-Triangle.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karpman_drama_triangle |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *victim, villain, hero* | Victim, villain, and hero are what the group calls the three flavors of victimhood; Karpman's original names are victim, persecutor, and rescuer. |
| *creator, coach, challenger* | Creator, coach, and challenger are David Emerald's roles for the three positions above the line. |
| *make it bigger* | 'Make it bigger' is the witness's prompt to exaggerate the drama until the person sees it as play. |
| *racket* | The group uses Landmark's word 'racket' for the pattern the triangle exposes. |

### The whole body yes

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | The coach uses it when a person faces a decision and wants to 'get this right', and when a person makes or reviews an agreement. |
| What the coach does | Chapman describes a chief executive deciding whether to let a head of sales go. She invited him to quiet his mind, relax his breath, and listen to what the group calls three centers of intelligence, head, heart, and body; then asked him to picture each option in turn and asked 'What do you notice in your body?'. With one option he reported constriction in the belly, tightness in the neck, flat energy, and fear and anger; with the other, more energy and easier breathing. The group defines a whole body yes as 'the experience in the body of alignment with the decision to be made', and its willingness questions ask people to renegotiate any agreement they no longer have a whole body yes to keeping. |
| Steps | (1) The coach reminds the person that from above the line there is no right answer. (2) The coach invites the person to quiet their mind, relax their breath, and listen without judgment to the three centers of intelligence. (3) The coach asks the person to picture the first option and asks 'What do you notice in your body?' (4) The coach asks the person to picture the second option and asks the same question. (5) The person compares sensations such as constriction, breath, and energy, and feelings such as fear or anger. (6) The person takes the option that is a whole body yes; the group's rule is 'If it’s not a whole body yes, it’s a no.' (7) For agreements, the person renegotiates any agreement they no longer have a whole body yes to keeping and makes only agreements they have a whole body yes to. |
| What it does | The move has a person picture each option in turn, compare what their body does, and take the option the whole body agrees with. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes a person's bodily response to each option available to their decision; the person decides. The coach supplies the sequence and the question, not the preferred answer. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The coach leads the person through the sequence and asks the question, and the person reports what they notice and decides. |
| The evidence | The source is one coaching story told by the coach; the sources opened contain no study. The post reports the cost of the decision in the story: 'a couple of key clients left too'. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Decisions: The move makes the body a named source of evidence in a decision, beside reasoning and feeling. Knowing that something happened: The move treats easier breathing and more energy as the sign of alignment, a bodily assent that the vault records can also respond to how an option is framed. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus also makes the body the deciding test where doctrine cannot decide ('the body seated as referee'), but the vault records a measured catalogue of counterfeit felt truth, so the book cannot treat a bodily yes as proof without a further check. |
| Limits and critiques | The sources opened give no guidance on telling a whole body yes from relief at avoiding a feared conversation, and the story's decision had a cost the post reports: 'a couple of key clients left too'. |
| Quoted from its sources | "I reminded him that from above the line there is no right answer." (https://conscious.is/blog/are-you-leading-from-your-whole-body-yes/) "I invited Mark to quiet his mind, relax his breath and listen without judgment to his three centers of intelligence." (https://conscious.is/blog/are-you-leading-from-your-whole-body-yes/) "“What do you notice in your body?”" (https://conscious.is/blog/are-you-leading-from-your-whole-body-yes/) "A whole body yes (as opposed to only a head or heart yes) is the experience in the body of alignment with the decision to be made." (https://conscious.is/blog/are-you-leading-from-your-whole-body-yes/) "“If it’s not a whole body yes, it’s a no.”" (https://conscious.is/blog/are-you-leading-from-your-whole-body-yes/) "listen to your IQ, EQ (Emotional Intelligence) and BQ (Body Intelligence) and learn from your results" (https://conscious.is/blog/are-you-leading-from-your-whole-body-yes/) "Mark let Steve go, and as he had feared, a couple of key clients left too." (https://conscious.is/blog/are-you-leading-from-your-whole-body-yes/) "Are you willing to renegotiate all agreements related to this issue that you no longer have a whole body yes to keeping?" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/gated/Willingness-Questions.pdf) "Are you willing to only make agreements about which you have a whole body yes and around which you have control?" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/gated/Willingness-Questions.pdf) "the body seated as referee" (notes/surrender and will are adjudicated from the body rather than from doctrine.md) |
| Date | 2015 (Chapman's post, July 2015) |
| In the vault | `notes/surrender and will are adjudicated from the body rather than from doctrine.md`; The vault's live tension makes the reader's own felt check the deciding test between surrender and will; the whole body yes is a working procedure for that test in a decision. The vault's note on the documented counterfeit catalogue of felt truth (notes/the felt sense of truth has a documented counterfeit catalogue.md) records why a bodily assent alone does not certify a claim. |
| Sources | https://conscious.is/blog/are-you-leading-from-your-whole-body-yes/; https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/gated/Willingness-Questions.pdf; https://conscious.is/15-commitments |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *whole body yes* | A whole body yes is the group's name for alignment with a decision that is felt in the body, as opposed to a yes of the head or heart alone. |
| *three centers of intelligence* | The three centers are the head, heart, and body, which Chapman also calls IQ, EQ, and BQ. |
| *impeccable agreements* | Impeccable agreements are agreements a person has a whole body yes to and keeps or renegotiates openly. |

### Speaking unarguably (the practice for commitment four, candor)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The group offers it as a practice through the day, alone at first and then aloud with colleagues who are practicing candor. |
| What the coach does | The group defines candor as 'the revealing of all thoughts, feelings and sensations in an open, honest and aware way' and withholding as refraining from revealing. Its practice is to speak unarguably, reporting only what no one else can dispute: a body sensation ('I notice…'), a thought owned as a thought ('I’m having the thought that…'), and a feeling named with one of five words. A person practices the sentences silently first and then aloud with others who are also practicing candor. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices any physical sensations, any emotions, and the stories they are telling themselves. (2) The person completes 'I notice…' with a sensation in or on the body, such as 'Heat rising up the center of my chest.' (3) The person completes 'I’m having the thought that…' with a thought, such as 'My daughter doesn't respect me.' (4) The person completes 'I feel…' using only feeling words, from the five the group uses: sad, angry, scared, joyful, or sexual. (5) The person says the sentences to themselves first and then out loud to others who are committed to practicing candor. |
| What it does | The practice replaces claims about others with reports of one's own sensations, thoughts, and feelings. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it easier for a person to say what is true for them without claiming facts about others; the person supplies the content. The group supplies three sentence forms and five feeling words. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The group's page gives the sentence forms for a person to practice alone and then with others who have agreed to practice candor. |
| The evidence | The source is the group's practice page; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Conflict: Owning a thought as 'I’m having the thought that…' lets a person say a hard thing without asserting it as a fact about the other. Being seen and recognized by another: The practice asks a person to reveal sensations, thoughts, and feelings so that others can know their experience. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus lists among Authentic Relating's protocols revealing one's experience in the first person, the present tense, and the body (arc-gloss p. 1), which the author practices; the group's version limits feeling words to five and ties candor to a leader's success. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Candor is the revealing of all thoughts, feelings and sensations in an open, honest and aware way." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-4-candor/) "Withholding is refraining from revealing everything to all relevant parties." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-4-candor/) "A key to the masterful practice of candor is learning to speak unarguably." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-4-candor/) "I notice… [report a sensory experience in or on the body]" (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-4-candor/) "Heat rising up the center of my chest." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-4-candor/) "I’m having the thought that…" (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-4-candor/) "The key to this practice is to answer only with feeling words." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-4-candor/) "Begin by simply saying them to yourself and then you can practice saying them out loud to others who are committed to practicing candor" (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-4-candor/) "Leaders and teams have found that seeing reality clearly is essential to being successful." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-4-candor/) "*Based on the work of The Hendricks Institute" (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-6-integrity/) |
| Date | 2015 (The 15 Commitments of Conscious Leadership, published by Dethmer, Chapman & Klemp; Open Library records 2015) |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no note on revealing practice; the corpus names Authentic Relating's reveal protocol only in the arc-gloss's list of the facilitation practices. |
| Sources | https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-4-candor/; https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-6-integrity/; https://conscious.is/15-commitments; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=15+commitments+of+conscious+leadership&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher,publish_year,isbn&limit=5 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *candor* | Candor is the group's word for revealing thoughts, feelings, and sensations openly and honestly. |
| *withholding* | Withholding is refraining from revealing everything to all relevant parties. |
| *unarguable* | An unarguable statement reports the speaker's own sensation, thought, or feeling, so another person cannot dispute it. |

### Feeling feelings all the way through to completion (the practice for commitment three)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The group offers it as a daily practice and for any moment a feeling arises. |
| What the coach does | The third commitment asks a person to feel feelings 'all the way through to completion', locating them in the body and then moving, breathing, and vocalizing them so they release. The practice has two steps: stopping during the day to ask 'What am I feeling right now?' and answering with one of five core feelings without analyzing why, and, when a feeling arises, locating the sensation, naming what it is doing with an '-ing' word, breathing, and moving or making a sound that matches it. The group gives each feeling a message for a leader; for example, sadness tells a leader that something needs to be let go of. |
| Steps | (1) The person stops periodically and asks 'What am I feeling right now?' (2) The person answers with one or more of five core feelings, sad, scared, angry, joyful, or creative/sexual, and does not search for why. (3) When a feeling arises, the person locates the sensation in the body and gives it an '-ing' word, such as throat closing or eyes watering. (4) The person breathes and allows the sensations to do what they do. (5) The person moves or makes a sound to match what the sensations are doing. |
| What it does | The practice has a person name a feeling, find it in the body, and breathe, move, or sound it until it releases. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it more likely that a person's feeling runs its course in the body rather than being analyzed or suppressed; the feeling and its movement are the person's. The group supplies the question, the five labels, and the instruction to match the sensation with movement or sound. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The group's page gives the two steps for a person to practice alone through the day. |
| The evidence | The source is the group's practice page; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The commitment's 'to me' form names resisting, judging, repressing, and withholding feelings as the pattern the practice replaces. Knowing that something happened: The practice defines completion as release after the sensation has been matched, which gives a bodily sign that the feeling has run its course. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The group states as its own commitment the completion wording that the corpus attributes to Joe Hudson and that the vault found Hudson does not use. The group's version adds a coach-supplied list of five feelings and a message for each, which the book does not supply. |
| Limits and critiques | The page states that because of the abuse of sexual feelings in the workplace, society has tried to legislate them out of that environment, and that 'in doing so, we have limited creative potential'; the page names the abuse and does not describe safeguards. |
| Quoted from its sources | "I commit to feeling my feelings all the way through to completion. They come, and I locate them in my body then move, breathe and vocalize them so they release all the way through." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/) "Stop periodically throughout your day and simply ask yourself the question, “What am I feeling right now?”" (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/) "Do not analyze the feeling and search for “why” you are feeling the feeling." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/) "Breathe and allow the sensations to simply do what they do." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/) "Move and/or make a sound to match what the sensations are doing." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/) "Great leaders learn to access all three centers of intelligence – head, heart and gut." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/) "Sadness tells a leader that something needs to be let go of, said goodbye to, moved on from." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/) "Countless leaders fail to create a culture of celebration and appreciation because they’re cut off from their joy." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/) "Because of the abuse of sexual feelings in the workplace, our society has tried to legislate them out of that environment." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/) "We understand the reason behind this but we believe that in doing so, we have limited creative potential." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/) "I commit to resisting, judging and apologizing for my feelings. I repress, avoid, and withhold them." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/) "and "complete" never appears as a predicate of a feeling in his material read 2026-09-02" (notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md) "The clause is the corpus's; the welcome is his" (notes/joe hudson.md) |
| Date | 2015 (The 15 Commitments of Conscious Leadership, published by Dethmer, Chapman & Klemp; Open Library records 2015) |
| In the vault | `notes/joe hudson.md`; The vault's Hudson note records that 'complete' does not appear as a predicate of a feeling in Hudson's material read on 2026-09-02, so the corpus's completion axiom is the corpus's own wording. The group's third commitment uses the completion wording directly, which gives the axiom a documented holder in the coaching field, one the corpus files under a different theory. |
| Sources | https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-3-feelings/; https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/15-Commitments.pdf; https://conscious.is/15-commitments; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=15+commitments+of+conscious+leadership&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher,publish_year,isbn&limit=5 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *five core feelings* | The group's five core feelings are sad, scared, angry, joyful, and creative or sexual. |
| *three centers of intelligence* | The group names the head, heart, and gut as three centers a leader needs access to. |
| *completion* | Completion is the group's word for a feeling released all the way through after it has been located, breathed, and moved. |

### The Clearing Model

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The group uses it when a person feels disconnected from a colleague or partner, and advises waiting until the person has shifted above the line. |
| What the coach does | A person who feels disconnected from someone affirms a positive intention and then shares, in order, the facts ('unarguable data that a video camera would record'), their stories, their feelings, and their wants. Dethmer writes that 'the deep learning occurs' in two further steps, naming one's own part in the disconnection and naming one's projections. The receiver listens and says only 'What I hear you saying …', 'Is that right?', 'Is there more?', and 'Are you clear?'. Dethmer warns that a person below the line uses the model to prove they are right, which makes it a weapon, and advises clearing only after one has shifted. |
| Steps | (1) The person clearing affirms a positive intention for the clearing. (2) The person shares the facts, defined as unarguable data that a video camera would record. (3) The person shares their stories: opinions, thoughts, beliefs, and judgments. (4) The person shares their feelings: sad, angry, scared, joy, or creative. (5) The person shares their wants. (6) The person names their own part in creating the disconnection, for example that they withheld, gossiped, or felt entitled. (7) The person names their projections: what is true about them that they project onto the other rather than face in themselves. (8) The receiver stays open, listens, and says 'What I hear you saying …', repeating as exactly as possible, then 'Is that right?', 'Is there more?', and 'Are you clear?' (9) The receiver may then look for how, not whether, the clearer's story about them is true. |
| What it does | The model has one person disclose facts, stories, feelings, wants, their own part, and their projections while the other only reflects and asks for more. |
| What is facilitated | The model makes it more likely that two people restore connection after a disconnection and that the person clearing learns about their own projections; both people supply the content. The model supplies the order of disclosure and the receiver's four lines. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The model fixes the order of disclosure and the receiver's lines, and both people supply the content. |
| The evidence | The source is the group's blog, which gives a worked example and no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Conflict: The model separates facts from stories and feelings in a disagreement and asks each person to name their own part. Being seen and recognized by another: Dethmer says a person clears with another rather than alone 'to make yourself known and to give others a chance to grow, which you can’t do on your own'. Harm and its conditions: The group names the condition under which the model does harm: a person below the line using it to justify their position. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The model tells the clearer that 'the clearing is all about you and not the other person' and treats complaints about others as disowned parts of oneself; the book's concern with the other person's reality leaves more room for a complaint that is simply accurate. |
| Limits and critiques | Dethmer writes that 'The clearing model can absolutely be used as a below the line weapon.' The post's worked example concerns effort on a project; the sources opened do not address clearing about mistreatment. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Affirm a positive intention for the clearing and then share…" (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "Facts (unarguable data that a video camera would record)" (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "Stories (opinions, thoughts, beliefs, judgments)" (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "Feelings (sad, angry, scared, joy or creative)" (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "Your part in creating the disconnection with the other person" (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "Projections (what is true about me that I project onto you rather than face it in myself)." (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "What I hear you saying …" (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "Is that right?" (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "Is there more?" (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "Are you clear?" (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "The clearing model can absolutely be used as a below the line weapon." (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "When you use the clearing model to prove you’re right, it becomes a divisive weapon instead of a bridge to connect." (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "Our answer is that you clear to make yourself known and to give others a chance to grow, which you can’t do on your own." (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "You realize that the clearing is all about you and not the other person." (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "when used wisely and with right intention" (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) "These four steps are the basics of the clearing model, but the deep learning occurs with the next two:" (https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/) |
| Date | 2018 (Dethmer, 'The Clearing Model: Connective Bridge or Divisive Weapon', April 2018) |
| In the vault | `notes/grief cannot complete alone.md`; The vault holds that grief cannot complete without a witness; Dethmer gives a parallel reason for clearing with another person rather than alone, 'you clear to make yourself known and to give others a chance to grow, which you can’t do on your own'. |
| Sources | https://conscious.is/blog/the-clearing-model-connective-bridge-or-divisive-weapon/; https://conscious.is/blog/a-bow-to-gay-katie-hendricks/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *facts and stories* | Facts are what a video camera would record; stories are the meanings a person adds, which the group holds are the source of drama. |
| *projection* | A projection is something true about oneself that one sees in another person instead of facing it in oneself. |
| *clearing from below the line* | Clearing from below the line is using the model to prove oneself right, which the group says turns it into a weapon. |

### Masterful appreciation (the practice for commitment seven)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The group offers it as a daily practice for individuals and as a thirty-day program for companies. |
| What the coach does | The group defines appreciation as two parts, 'sensitive awareness and an increase of value', and sets it against entitlement. Its practices include placing three dimes where one can see them, finding three people to appreciate each day, and flipping a dime after each appreciation delivered according to four keys: sincere, unarguable, specific, and succinct. It also offers a chart for choosing a form, spoken or written, public or private, expected or unexpected, professional or personal, and a thirty-day appreciation practice for companies. |
| Steps | (1) The person places three dimes, heads up, where they can regularly see them. (2) Each day the person commits to finding three people to appreciate. (3) The person delivers each appreciation following the keys to mastery: sincere, unarguable, specific, succinct. (4) After each appreciation the person flips a dime. (5) The person chooses the form that feels most authentic for the person and situation: spoken or written, public or private, expected or unexpected, professional or personal. |
| What it does | The practice has a person deliver three specific, sincere appreciations a day and count them with coins. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes a person's noticing of value in others a daily habit; the appreciations and their content are the person's. The group supplies the counting device and the four keys. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The group's page gives the device and the rules for a person to practice daily without a facilitator. |
| The evidence | The source is the group's practice page; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: The keys require that an appreciation be specific and unarguable, which makes it a report of what the giver actually saw in the other person. Change at the scale of a team or organization: The group offers a thirty-day appreciation practice to change a workplace culture of entitlement. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book's concern with being seen is about being seen accurately as oneself; the group frames appreciation by its value to leaders and organizations, to 'grow value and connection in the workplace'. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Appreciation is comprised of two parts: sensitive awareness and an increase of value." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-7-appreciation/) "Place three dimes, heads up, where you can regularly see them." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-7-appreciation/) "sincere, unarguable, specific, succinct" (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-7-appreciation/) "Choose the method that feels most authentic for the person and situation you are appreciating" (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-7-appreciation/) "Committing to appreciation, along with avoiding entitlement, helps leaders and organizations grow value and connection in the workplace." (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-7-appreciation/) "Do thirty days of corporate appreciation practice" (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-7-appreciation/) "I commit to feeling entitled to “what’s mine,” resenting when it’s not acknowledged in the way I want." (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/15-Commitments.pdf) "Think of someone who loves you. Describe how they see you." (ops/facilitation ecology — management and organizational psychology.md) |
| Date | 2015 (The 15 Commitments of Conscious Leadership, published by Dethmer, Chapman & Klemp; Open Library records 2015) |
