### Breathing sexual energy in a circle (The Enlightened Sex Manual, chapters 27 and 28)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The manual teaches it for solo practice first and then for couples during sex. |
| What the coach does | Deida's manual teaches that when a person's 'natural internal circuitry' is open, 'a main conduit of your energy moves in a circle, up the back of the body and down the front'. The exercise has a person relax, keep the breath full, notice where arousal is felt and which way it moves, and, before release, lift the pelvic floor, which he identifies with the yogic mula bandha and Kegel exercises, while breathing through the nose and feeling the energy rise along the spine. He advises practicing alone first and later with a partner, and he states a gendered principle for couples: the power of the energy is set by 'the feminine partner’s openness to love and pleasure' and its depth by 'the masculine partner’s capacity to circulate energy consciously', while noting that anyone can play either role, 'whether we are man or woman, gay or straight'. |
| Steps | (1) The person relaxes the body as much as possible and keeps the breath full and deep. (2) The person develops sensitivity to where the energy is felt and in which direction it moves. (3) Before release, the person contracts and lifts the pelvic floor, either as one long hold of fifteen to thirty seconds or as quick rhythmic pulses. (4) The person breathes through the nose, feels the energy move up the spine, and lets it come down the front of the body to complete the circle. (5) The person practices alone first and later with a partner, for several minutes at a time, and rests if tired. |
| What it does | The practice uses breath and a lift of the pelvic floor to circulate arousal up the spine and down the front of the body instead of releasing it. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it more likely that a person sustains and spreads arousal through the body rather than releasing it; the experience is the person's. The manual supplies the sequence and the gendered division of roles for couples. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The manual gives the exercise, and a person or couple practices it without a facilitator. |
| The evidence | The source is a chapter of Deida's manual; the sources opened contain no study of the practice. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: In couples the manual assigns the direction of the shared practice to the partner playing the masculine. Harm and its conditions: The gendered assignment of direction to the masculine partner is one of the points critics of Deida's essentialism contest. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book includes no sexual practice; this entry records the breath-and-attention structure of Deida's central couples practice and its division of roles by pole. |
| Limits and critiques | The chapter gives brief cautions ('Rest if you feel tired. Don’t overdo it.') and no medical screening. |
| Quoted from its sources | "a main conduit of your energy moves in a circle, up the back of the body and down the front." (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/Enlightened-Sex-Manual/4_Part_Four_-_Sexual_Energy_Exercises.pdf) "This upward contraction of your pelvic floor is known in yogic terms as mula bandha. In medical or therapeutic terms it is known by many names, including PC or Kegel exercises." (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/Enlightened-Sex-Manual/4_Part_Four_-_Sexual_Energy_Exercises.pdf) "they have been discovered and rediscovered for thousands of years around the world, in China, Tibet, Japan, India, Europe, and the Americas." (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/Enlightened-Sex-Manual/4_Part_Four_-_Sexual_Energy_Exercises.pdf) "mined by the feminine partner’s openness to love and pleasure" (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/Enlightened-Sex-Manual/4_Part_Four_-_Sexual_Energy_Exercises.pdf) "determined by the masculine partner’s capacity to circulate energy consciously" (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/Enlightened-Sex-Manual/4_Part_Four_-_Sexual_Energy_Exercises.pdf) "whether we are man or woman, gay or straight." (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/Enlightened-Sex-Manual/4_Part_Four_-_Sexual_Energy_Exercises.pdf) "Rest if you feel tired. Don’t overdo it." (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/Enlightened-Sex-Manual/4_Part_Four_-_Sexual_Energy_Exercises.pdf) "Make these exercises your own." (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/Enlightened-Sex-Manual/4_Part_Four_-_Sexual_Energy_Exercises.pdf) "One way is to contract the pelvic floor and hold the contraction for fifteen to thirty seconds while continuing to breathe normally." (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/Enlightened-Sex-Manual/4_Part_Four_-_Sexual_Energy_Exercises.pdf) |
| Date | 2004 (The Enlightened Sex Manual; the free chapter is from a 2024 printing) |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no note on Deida's sexual practices. |
| Sources | https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/Enlightened-Sex-Manual/4_Part_Four_-_Sexual_Energy_Exercises.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Deida |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

|---|---|
| *circle* | The circle is the path Deida says energy takes when a person's circuitry is open: up the back of the body and down the front. |
| *mula bandha* | Mula bandha is the yogic name Deida gives for the upward contraction of the pelvic floor, also known as Kegel exercises. |

### Staying at zero with a partner's upset (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Deida teaches it for moments when a partner comes with upset or criticism and the more masculine partner feels a flinch. |
| What the coach does | Asked how a man can give undistracted attention to a partner's upset when he does not want to, Deida says the first thing is to understand the 'not-wanting-to', a flinch he attributes to the masculine love of emptiness. The first step is to 'notice what threatens you, and then sink into the nothingness', finding 'the same awareness before your partner comes to you, while your partner is with you, and after your partner leaves', and relaxing as it. The next step is to 'feel her energy as energy', without running from it, freezing it, or holding it, and then to feel under it to 'a kind of universal desire for love and awareness', resting 'being-to-being'. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices what threatens them in the partner's approach. (2) The person sinks 'into the nothingness': they find the awareness that is the same before, during, and after the encounter, and they relax as it. (3) The person feels the partner's emotion as energy: 'Don’t run from it, don’t freeze it, don’t hold it; just completely be with it.' (4) The person feels under that energy to the desire for love and awareness that Deida says is in every heart. (5) The person rests in the depth of being that Deida says both people share. |
| What it does | The move has a person rest in the awareness present before, during, and after the encounter, then feel the partner's emotion as energy and the shared wish for love beneath it. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it more likely that a person stays present to a partner's upset instead of flinching away; the staying is the person's, and the partner is met rather than managed. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person who feels the flinch does the practice inwardly; the partner is not asked to do anything. |
| The evidence | The source is Deida's 2026 interview; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Conflict: The move addresses the moment a partner arrives with upset or criticism and asks for presence instead of resolution. Recognition: The first step rests on 'a beingness that’s there all the time', which is recognition of what is already present. Being seen and recognized by another: The second step feels beneath the partner's emotion to a shared desire for love, which is a way of meeting the other person. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Deida frames the move for a masculine partner meeting a feminine partner's energy, while the book addresses a reader of any gender; the second step, feeling beneath emotion to a shared desire for love, is not gendered. The move's own words, 'no resistance' and being with emotion without running from it or holding it, fit the corpus's second theory better than the fifth, where the corpus places Deida. |
| Quoted from its sources | "So the first thing to do is notice what threatens you, and then sink into the nothingness." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "Find the place in you that’s the same awareness before your partner comes to you, while your partner is with you, and after your partner leaves." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "By relaxing as that beingness, you stabilize as that presence more and more over time." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "The next step is to feel her energy as energy." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "Don’t run from it, don’t freeze it, don’t hold it; just completely be with it." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "there’s a kind of universal desire for love and awareness in her heart" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "There’s just a quality of love when you rest being-to-being with any creature." | Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *zero* | Zero is Deida's term for no resistance to what is. |
| *testing* | Testing is Deida's name for the feminine partner's unintended checking of whether the masculine partner stays present and relaxed. |

### Witnessing inherited patterns (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Deida teaches it for thoughts or urges a person does not endorse, such as violent thoughts or addictive tendencies. |
| What the coach does | Deida says people inherit patterns from childhood, from their parents' and grandparents' childhoods, from ancestors, and from 'mammalian patterns'. His practice is to witness such thoughts, for example violent thoughts: 'He lets them move through him. He doesn’t contract around them, doesn’t push them away, doesn’t enact them.' He adds that 'a man of zero doesn’t feel shame or guilt for these thoughts arising', and that presence does not stop inherited contractions from arising; for trauma he points to somatic releasing techniques and family constellation work. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices an inherited pattern arising, such as a violent thought or an addictive urge. (2) The person witnesses it and lets it move through them. (3) The person neither contracts around it, pushes it away, nor acts it out. (4) The person does not feel shame or guilt for its arising and understands it as inherited. (5) Where trauma is held in the body or the family, the person uses somatic releasing techniques or family constellation work, as Deida advises. |
| What it does | The move has a person watch an inherited impulse pass without resisting it, acting on it, or feeling ashamed of it. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it more likely that an unwanted impulse passes without being acted on or fought; the witnessing is the person's. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person witnesses their own thoughts; Deida refers trauma to other practitioners. |
| The evidence | The source is Deida's 2026 interview; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Deida places the origin of some contractions in inheritance, which removes personal blame while keeping the practice personal. Protection and defense: The move asks a person not to contract around an unwanted impulse, neither suppressing nor enacting it. Harm and its conditions: Deida refers people with trauma to somatic and family-systems work, which marks a limit of witnessing alone. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Deida's account of inheritance from ancestors resembles Thomas Hübl's collective-trauma account that the vault holds as an independent arrival; Deida adds mammalian inheritance and gives a solo witnessing practice rather than a group process. The move's own words, witnessing without contracting, pushing away, or enacting, fit the corpus's first and second theories better than the fifth, where the corpus places Deida. |
| Quoted from its sources | "We inherit patterns from our past—from our childhood, from the way our parents treated us, but also from our parents’ childhoods and our grandparents’ childhoods." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "Not only human ancestors, but animal ancestors—mammalian patterns." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "A man of zero simply witnesses those thoughts. He lets them move through him. He doesn’t contract around them, doesn’t push them away, doesn’t enact them." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "And a key difference: a man of zero doesn’t feel shame or guilt for these thoughts arising." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "It’s really important to understand that being a very present and loving person doesn’t mean that these contractions no longer arise." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "There are very specific techniques you could use—if you have trauma in the body, somatic releasing techniques; if you have trauma inherited through family patterns, family constellation work." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) |
| Date | 2026 (The Man of Zero; Insights at the Edge interview with Tami Simon, published 9 June 2026) |
| In the vault | `notes/what was never metabolized lives on as absence and hubl reached it through inherited trauma.md`; The vault holds Hübl's account of inherited trauma as an independent arrival at the book's idea of what was never metabolized; Deida's 2026 account also places some contractions in inheritance and adds a witnessing practice and a referral to family constellation work. |
| Sources | https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *inherited patterns* | Inherited patterns are tendencies Deida says come from family, ancestors, and mammalian history rather than from the person's own choices. |
| *purification* | Purification is Deida's word for inherited patterns weakening over time as they are released in awareness. |

### Riverbanking

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Deida teaches it for moments when a partner is undecided among many directions. |
| What the coach does | Deida says that when a person 'is in a very feminine-identified moment and is having trouble making a decision because they’re flowing in many directions at once', it helps for their masculine partner to 'provide river banking—to offer an option'. He adds 'Not that they would necessarily listen to that option or obey', and says the partner's decisiveness lets the other feel 'the power of her own flow'. His 1997 chapter on the same theme is stronger, saying 'A woman must be able to trust you to take charge if she relaxes her own masculine edge' in financial, sexual, emotional, and spiritual matters, while adding 'This doesn’t mean you need to boss her around.' |
| Steps | (1) The partner in the masculine role notices a partner who is having trouble deciding because they are moving in many directions at once. (2) The partner offers one concrete option. (3) The partner leaves the other free not to take it. (4) The partner stays decisive and clear, so that the other can feel their own direction. |
| What it does | The move offers a concrete option to an undecided partner without requiring that they take it. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes a partner's decision easier by giving them something concrete to respond to; the decision stays with them in the 2026 wording. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. The partner in the masculine role offers an option, and the other partner decides whether to take it. |
| The evidence | The sources are Deida's 2026 interview and a 1997 chapter; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Decisions: The move gives an undecided partner a concrete option rather than a decision. Who decides the content: The 2026 wording leaves the choice with the partner, while the 1997 chapter asks the man to 'take charge'. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book does not assign direction to one partner by pole; the move presupposes Deida's division in which the masculine gives direction and the feminine is, in his words, 'the energy and flow of the river'. |
| Limits and critiques | The 1997 chapter frames this as a man taking charge so that 'his woman' can relax, which is the division of roles critics of Deida's essentialism contest. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Often, if a person is in a very feminine-identified moment and is having trouble making a decision because they’re flowing in many directions at once, it’s helpful for their masculine partner to provide river banking—to offer an option." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "Not that they would necessarily listen to that option or obey, but just to have a concrete option, to be with someone who is decisive." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "That decisiveness allows the feminine partner to feel the power of her own flow." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "So river banking is the loving and gentle offering of banks to the flow of the river—so that it’s not just flowing randomly, but flowing in a direction." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) "A woman must be able to trust you to take charge if she relaxes her own masculine edge." (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/The-Way-of-the-Superior-Man/BK00906-interior-pages-34.pdf) "This is true financially, sexually, emotionally, and spiritually." (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/The-Way-of-the-Superior-Man/BK00906-interior-pages-34.pdf) "This doesn’t mean you need to boss her around." (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/The-Way-of-the-Superior-Man/BK00906-interior-pages-34.pdf) "She Wants to Relax in the Demonstration of Your Direction" (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/The-Way-of-the-Superior-Man/BK00906-interior-pages-34.pdf) "but he must be able to steer the course if his woman is going to relax into her feminine without fear." (https://daviddeida-freechapters.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/The-Way-of-the-Superior-Man/BK00906-interior-pages-34.pdf) "The feminine is the energy and flow of the river" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/david-deida-the-way-of-zero/) |

## Gay and Kathlyn Hendricks

| Move | Type | When the coach uses it | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| Essence and persona, fear in the body, and the Upper Limit Problem | a theory | The Hendricks teach this account in their books and seminars as the frame for their body-centered and relationship practices. | The account explains suffering as fear held in the body, roles that hide a person's essence, and a limit on how much good a person allows. |
| Catching the upper-limit thought | a move | Hendricks uses it with clients and seminar participants after a success or a good period, and with couples whose good times end in fights. | The move has a person catch the worry, quarrel, or mishap that follows a success and name it as a self-imposed limit. |
| The zone of genius, later called the genius spiral | a technique | Hendricks uses it with people who feel stuck or want to contribute more, and the Conscious Leadership Group uses it with leaders and teams. | The practice helps a person find, and move their time toward, the work they most love and are uniquely able to do. |
| Wonder questions | a move | Hendricks uses it when a person or couple is stuck on something they cannot explain. | The move replaces analysis with an open question the person wants answered and waits for the answer to arise. |
| Presencing | a technique | The Hendricks teach it for moments of overwhelm and as the first of seven practices in their program. | The practice turns full attention to present sensations, opens posture and breath, and moves attention between oneself and another. |
| Fear Melters® | a technique | The Hendricks teach it for moments when a person feels 'overwhelmed, reactive or stuck'. | The practice has a person identify which of four fear responses is running and change it with slow oozing, a wide stance, wiggling, or gathering movements. |
| F.A.C.T. | a technique | The Hendricks teach it for when a person is 'stuck or struggling with a decision or issue'. | The practice has a person physically face an issue, alternate wanting and not wanting to accept it, ask what they really want, and choose one pleasurable step. |
| Matching | a technique | The Hendricks teach it for moments when a person feels something but has no words for it. | The practice has a person move their hands, body, or voice to match an inner state until it settles. |
| The Persona Interview | a technique | The Hendricks use it after a reaction that surprised the person or seemed bigger than the situation called for. | The practice has a person step into an exaggerated role, name it, and answer six fixed questions in its voice before stepping out. |
| The Ten-Minute Heart Talk, with the Ten-Minute Stuff Talk | a technique | Couples use it twice a week, and the Hendricks recommend it for problems that keep returning. | The practice gives a couple a timed, touch-first structure for saying feelings and wants without blame and ending in appreciation. |
| Naming the feeling under the gesture | a move | Hendricks uses it when a person describes anger and their body suggests another feeling. | The move names, tentatively, a feeling that the person's gesture suggests lies under the one they are expressing. |

### Essence and persona, fear in the body, and the Upper Limit Problem

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| When the coach uses it | The Hendricks teach this account in their books and seminars as the frame for their body-centered and relationship practices. |
| What the coach does | The Hendricks' persona teaching holds that babies receive attention 'for their essence', that children develop roles, or personas, once their being becomes 'inconvenient for their caretakers', and that 'all personas carry aspects of essence' which can be freed. Their current program treats fear as a physiological response of 'fight, flee, freeze, or faint' and aims to help people 'move from fear to love through presence'. Gay Hendricks's Upper Limit Problem holds that each person has a limit on how much good they allow and that, on exceeding it, a person creates a worry, an illness, an accident, or an argument that returns them to the familiar; he traces it to fears such as feeling 'fundamentally flawed'. He also describes 'two cosmic pulls in us. One is to expand, and one is to contract', and says that people create 'enormous dramas' to avoid a short honest conversation. |
| What it does | The account explains suffering as fear held in the body, roles that hide a person's essence, and a limit on how much good a person allows. |
| What is facilitated | The work aims to let a person's essence, feelings, and creativity be expressed and to raise how much love and success a person can allow; the person does the feeling, noticing, and choosing. The Hendricks supply body practices, questions, and commitments, and in sessions they say what they see. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. The Hendricks offer their account and practices, and each person decides what applies to them. |
| The evidence | The account rests on the Hendricks' clinical and seminar work over about forty years; the sources opened report no controlled study. Wikipedia's article records Gay Hendricks's doctorate and professorship and lists the books named here. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The persona teaching holds that essence is present from infancy and is freed by befriending the roles that hide it, which is a model of recovering what was there rather than adding something new. Protection and defense: The account reads personas as ways a child kept attention, fear as a body state, and the Upper Limit Problem as a return to the familiar after an expansion. Timing and pacing: The Upper Limit Problem predicts a contraction just after an expansion, which tells a facilitator when a setback is most likely. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus names the Hendricks once and places them in none of its five theories. In their own words they locate suffering in fear and feeling held in the body (nearest the second theory, a resisted feeling), in an unconscious commitment to keeping a problem going (near the fourth theory; the Conscious Leadership Group says its commitment to 100% responsibility mirrors the Hendricks' work 'nearly word-for-word'), and in the Upper Limit Problem, a limit on how much good a person allows, which none of the five theories names. |
| Limits and critiques | The Hendricks' materials report no controlled outcome study in the sources opened. Gay Hendricks presents his 'cosmic theory' of expansion and contraction as his own view, and the Fear Melters page cites research it does not name (entry hendricks-fear-melters). |
| Quoted from its sources | "Most babies receive attention for their essence, for simply being who they are." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "However, sooner or later their being becomes inconvenient for their caretakers." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "The good news is that all personas carry aspects of essence" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "Fear is a physiological response that shows up as fight, flee, freeze, or faint." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "You will learn how to move from fear to love through presence." (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/) "I realized it’s like I have an allergy to feeling good all the time, and so I dial up something to happen that makes me not feel good." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "You get sick or have an accident or start an argument." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "So I think we have these two cosmic pulls in us. One is to expand, and one is to contract." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "Well human beings are capable of creating enormous dramas to avoid what I call the 10-second, sweaty conversation." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "They call it the tall poppy syndrome." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "And I have a kind of a cosmic theory about that." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "the most fundamental one is the belief that I don't deserve the good things of life. I'm fundamentally flawed in some way." (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "At the bottom of all issues lies the central one: learning to love yourself." (https://hendricks.com/every-aspect-of-you-wants-to-be-loved-by-you/) "After receiving his Ph.D. in psychology from Stanford University in 1974" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_Hendricks) "Hudson, Robbins, Katie, Singer, Hendricks, the CLG trio, Colonna, Wineland, Deida, Hübl" (.corpus/the arc and the gloss - second edition.md) "Hendricks has zero occurrences anywhere in the corpus" (notes/the routing surfaces carry four more names alone than the sort filed as company.md) "Their teachings form the bedrock of much of what we now share with leaders and teams." (https://conscious.is/blog/a-bow-to-gay-katie-hendricks/) "Commitments 1 and 2— 100% Responsibility and Curiosity—mirror Gay and Katie’s work nearly word-for-word." (https://conscious.is/blog/a-bow-to-gay-katie-hendricks/) |
| Date | 1982 (Learning to Love Yourself) through 2015 (Conscious Loving Ever After), with Gay Hendricks's interviews of 2020 and 2024 |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *essence* | Essence is the Hendricks' word for who a person is before they learn roles to get attention. |
| *persona* | A persona is a role a person developed to keep receiving attention and avoid unwanted attention. |
| *Upper Limit Problem* | The Upper Limit Problem is Gay Hendricks's name for the self-created setback that follows when a person exceeds their usual level of success, love, or good feeling. |
| *10-second, sweaty conversation* | The 10-second, sweaty conversation is Gay Hendricks's name for a short, uncomfortable, direct statement of the truth, which he says people build dramas to avoid. |

### Catching the upper-limit thought

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hendricks uses it with clients and seminar participants after a success or a good period, and with couples whose good times end in fights. |
| What the coach does | Hendricks teaches people to notice the moment after a success, a good day, or a close weekend when a worry thought, an argument, an illness, or an accident arrives, and to recognize it as an upper-limit move. His own first example is a worry about his daughter at camp that arrived during a good afternoon; his executives told him of a promotion followed that night by 'the most awful fight' with a spouse. Because, he says, the problem is 'almost always beyond our conscious minds ability to perceive', he pairs noticing with a wonder question and with a new commitment, such as 'I commit to using the wholeness of myself and going all the way to my full genius.' |
| Steps | (1) The person notices a moment of expansion: a success, a good day, or a close time with a partner. (2) The person watches for what follows: a worry thought, criticism, an argument, an illness, or an accident. (3) The person names it as an upper-limit move; Hendricks says such a thought 'takes a 10th of a second, but it has the capacity to go whomp.' (4) Because the problem is usually outside awareness, the person asks a wonder question, such as 'what is the limiting belief that's keeping me stuck?' (5) The person replaces the limiting belief with a commitment, such as 'I commit to using the wholeness of myself and going all the way to my full genius.' |
| What it does | The move has a person catch the worry, quarrel, or mishap that follows a success and name it as a self-imposed limit. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it more likely that a person recognizes a self-created setback after a success and chooses differently; the noticing and the new commitment are the person's. Hendricks supplies the name, the pattern, and forms for the question and the commitment. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. Hendricks offers the name and the pattern, and the person decides whether a given setback fits it. |
| The evidence | The concept rests on Hendricks's observations of himself, executives, and seminar participants; the sources opened contain no study of it. A podcast host summarizes The Big Leap's four hidden fears as feeling fundamentally flawed, disloyalty and abandonment, believing that more success brings a bigger burden, and 'the crime of outshining'; only the first appears in Hendricks's own words in the sources opened. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: The move tells a person and a facilitator that the time just after a breakthrough is when a setback is most likely to be self-made. Knowing that something happened: The move reads a sudden worry or quarrel after a good experience as a sign that an expansion took place, not only as trouble. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book's arc includes a descent after the peak as one of its phases; Hendricks reads the fall after an expansion as a problem to be overcome by raising one's limit. None of the corpus's five theories names this difficulty; the move's own words place suffering in a limit on how much good a person allows. |
| Quoted from its sources | "And so I started calling that the ULP or the upper limit problem." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "I got a big promotion at work, and then I went home that night, and then we got in the most awful fight with my wife." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "because that one little thought, a thought doesn’t weight anything. And it takes a 10th of a second, but it has the capacity to go whomp." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "term upper limit problem, because it's a problem that occurs because you've hit the upper limit of how much positive energy you can allow it to flow through you." (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "the upper limit problem is almost always beyond our conscious minds ability to perceive" (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "what is the limiting belief that's keeping me stuck?" (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "I commit to using the wholeness of myself and going all the way to my full genius." (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "the crime of outshining" (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "I'm now I was probably 29." (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) |
| Date | 2009 (The Big Leap, HarperOne); first noticed by Hendricks, by his account, at about age 29 |
| In the vault | notes/the pulse has a counterfeit at each phase, a false peak of expansion and a racket of forgotten contraction.md; The vault's nascent note names a false peak of expansion and a racket of forgotten contraction; the Upper Limit Problem describes a contraction that an expansion itself sets off, which neither counterfeit covers. |
| Sources | https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/; https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=the+big+leap+hendricks&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher&limit=2; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_Hendricks |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *upper limit* | The upper limit is the level of success, love, or good feeling beyond which a person, in Hendricks's account, brings themselves back down. |
| *upper-limit thought* | An upper-limit thought is a brief worry or doubt that arrives just after an expansion and starts the return to the familiar. |
| *commitment* | A commitment, in Hendricks's use, is a stated choice that replaces a limiting belief, for example to express one's full genius. |

### The zone of genius, later called the genius spiral

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Hendricks uses it with people who feel stuck or want to contribute more, and the Conscious Leadership Group uses it with leaders and teams. |
| What the coach does | Hendricks separates a zone of genius, where a person does what they most love and are uniquely able to do, from lesser zones; the Conscious Leadership Group's commitment names them as incompetence, competence, and excellence. The Hendricks' site says living in the zone of genius 'produces the highest ratio of satisfaction, productivity, and contribution to time spent', that the first step is 'an approach of lighthearted, cheerful wonder', and it poses the question 'What is my genius? And how can I bring it forth so it serves me and others simultaneously?'. By 2020 Hendricks said he no longer calls it a zone but 'the genius spiral', because it is 'completely open ended'. The Conscious Leadership Group's version asks a person to list 25 work activities from the last two weeks and mark each by whether their energy rose, stayed flat, or fell. |
| Steps | (1) The person adopts an approach of lighthearted, cheerful wonder and releases judgment. (2) The person asks: 'What is my genius? And how can I bring it forth so it serves me and others simultaneously?' (3) The person makes a commitment, such as 'I commit to discovering my genius and learning how to express it in the world to my and others’ benefit.' (4) In the Conscious Leadership Group's version, the person lists 25 activities from the last two weeks of work and marks each with an arrow up, flat, or down for their energy. (5) The person estimates the share of time spent in activities that raised, flattened, or lowered their energy. |
| What it does | The practice helps a person find, and move their time toward, the work they most love and are uniquely able to do. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes a person's own discovery of what they most love to do more likely; the answers are the person's. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. Hendricks offers the zones and questions, and the person discovers their own genius. |
| The evidence | The sources opened contain no study; Hendricks's claim that genius work gives the highest ratio of satisfaction and contribution to time is untested there. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The future: The practice directs a person's planning toward the activities that raise their energy. Recognition: Hendricks calls the genius 'your innate genius, your natural genius', something to discover rather than acquire. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book's promised event is recognition of what a person already is; the genius practice also looks for something innate but directs it toward productivity and contribution at work. None of the corpus's five theories names this difficulty; the practice's own words place it in time spent outside one's genius. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Living in your Zone of Genius produces the highest ratio of satisfaction, productivity, and contribution to time spent." (https://hendricks.com/big-leaps/approach/) "Adopt an approach of lighthearted, cheerful wonder." (https://hendricks.com/big-leaps/approach/) "What is my genius? And how can I bring it forth so it serves me and others simultaneously?" (https://hendricks.com/big-leaps/approach/) "Well I used to call it the zone of genius, and I don’t call it the zone of genius anymore. I call it the genius spiral" (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "And the beauty of the genius spiral is it’s completely open ended, and it doesn’t stop." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "that’s what I call your innate genius, your natural genius." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "I commit to discovering my genius and learning how to express it in the world to my and others’ benefit." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "make a list of 25 activities you performed in the course of doing your job" (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-8-genius/) "Conscious leaders build organizations that allow all team members to realize their full potential" (https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-8-genius/) "living in areas of incompetence, competence and excellence" (https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/15-Commitments.pdf) |
| Date | 2009 (The Big Leap); 'genius spiral' in a 2020 interview |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no note on the zone of genius. |
| Sources | https://hendricks.com/big-leaps/approach/; https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/; https://conscious.is/resource/commitment-8-genius/; https://files.conscious.is/hubfs/resources/fundamentals/15-Commitments.pdf; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=the+big+leap+hendricks&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher&limit=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *zone of genius* | The zone of genius is the work a person most loves and is uniquely able to do. |
| *zones of incompetence, competence, and excellence* | These are the zones below genius: work one does badly, adequately, or very well without love for it. |
| *genius spiral* | The genius spiral is Hendricks's later name for the zone of genius, chosen because expressing one's genius keeps opening further. |

### Wonder questions

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hendricks uses it when a person or couple is stuck on something they cannot explain. |
| What the coach does | Hendricks defines a wonder question as 'something you really would love the answer to, but you really don't know', asked in an attitude of wondering rather than analysis. He spent ten minutes with an executive wondering 'what is my true genius?', and with a couple after one partner's affair he asked the other, 'Why was it inevitable that this particular thing happened?'. A day later the man connected the event with his mother leaving his father when he was a year old. The Hendricks' Heart Talk asks the listener to generate 'wonder rather than blame'. |
| Steps | (1) The person chooses a question they would love the answer to but do not know. (2) The person asks it as wonder, for example 'wonder, what is my true genius? Just wondering about that.' (3) The person stays with the question without forcing an answer, for ten minutes or, in Hendricks's couple example, for a day. (4) In a relational impasse, Hendricks asks: 'Why was it inevitable that this particular thing happened?' |
| What it does | The move replaces analysis with an open question the person wants answered and waits for the answer to arise. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it more likely that a person's own answer to an open question arises; the answer is the person's and may take a day to come. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Hendricks proposes the question, and the person lets the answer arrive in their own time. |
| The evidence | The sources are Hendricks's own anecdotes; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The question is one the person does not know the answer to, so the answer that arrives is theirs. Harm and its conditions: Asking the partner of someone who had an affair why it was 'inevitable' looks for his part in an event another person chose, which carries the risk of over-responsibility the vault's sovereignty note names. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The 'inevitable' question has the same structure as Colonna's complicity question and the Conscious Leadership Group's Level 2 responsibility, so the Hendricks' own practice belongs with the camp the corpus calls disowned complicity, although the corpus does not place them there. |
| Quoted from its sources | "So a wonder question is something you really would love the answer to, but you really don't know." (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "So wonder, what is my true genius? Just wondering about that." (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "And we're going to start right now with focusing on our genius for 10 minutes." (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "Why was it inevitable that this particular thing happened? That's a great wonder question." (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "I wonder if it has something to do with my father, and what happened with my mother?" (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "Now I'm 79." (https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/) "generating wonder rather than blame" (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "Everything happens to me" (notes/sovereignty occupies the middle between everything happens to me and everything is my responsibility.md) "Everything is my responsibility" (notes/sovereignty occupies the middle between everything happens to me and everything is my responsibility.md) |
| Date | In Hendricks's books by his account; described in a 2024 interview (Hendricks gives his age there as 79) |
| In the vault | `notes/sovereignty occupies the middle between everything happens to me and everything is my responsibility.md`; The vault's nascent note places sovereignty between 'everything happens to me' and 'everything is my responsibility'; the 'inevitable' question, asked of a person whose partner had an affair, tests where that middle lies. |
| Sources | https://zackarnold.com/episode/breaking-through-upper-limits-living-zone-genius-gay-hendricks/; https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_Hendricks |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *wonder question* | A wonder question is a question a person would love the answer to but does not know, held with curiosity rather than analysis. |

### Presencing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The Hendricks teach it for moments of overwhelm and as the first of seven practices in their program. |
| What the coach does | The Hendricks define presencing as 'an integrative, body-mind tool that helps you welcome feelings, sensations, thoughts, and any other experiences into the wholeness of yourself', using attention 'with curiosity, rather than from a critical or ‘what’s wrong’ point of view'. The steps turn attention to the present, open the posture, breathe into the belly, notice sensations, take three breaths and change posture, appreciate oneself and another, and move attention between oneself and another person in an easy rhythm. The page says 'Presencing is what happens when all of you arrives in the same moment.' |
| Steps | (1) The person turns their attention fully toward what is happening in the present moment. (2) The person opens their posture, with arms and legs uncrossed and face and body relaxed. (3) The person breathes fully and easily into the belly. (4) The person notices physical sensations, such as tightness in the chest or sweaty hands. (5) The person takes three breaths and changes posture. (6) The person appreciates themselves and another person. (7) The person moves attention from themselves to another person and back again, repeating with an easy rhythm. |
| What it does | The practice turns full attention to present sensations, opens posture and breath, and moves attention between oneself and another. |
| What is facilitated | Presencing makes it more likely that a person's feelings, sensations, and thoughts are welcomed into awareness rather than judged; the experience is the person's. The page supplies seven steps. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The module page gives the steps for a person to practice alone, with short videos. |
| The evidence | The source is the program's module page; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The page says presencing uses attention with curiosity rather than from a 'what’s wrong' point of view, which meets a defended state with welcome. Being seen and recognized by another: The final step moves attention between oneself and another person, so presence to oneself and presence to the other are practiced together. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Presencing includes changing posture and appreciating oneself, which adds deliberate change of state to the welcoming the book asks of its reader. The practice's own words, which ask a person to 'welcome feelings, sensations, thoughts', fit the corpus's second theory, a resisted feeling that is allowed. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Presencing is an integrative, body-mind tool that helps you welcome feelings, sensations, thoughts, and any other experiences into the wholeness of yourself." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/presencing/) "Presencing invites you to use your attention with curiosity, rather than from a critical or 'what’s wrong' point of view." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/presencing/) "Presencing is what happens when all of you arrives in the same moment." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/presencing/) "Turn your attention fully towards what is happening in the present moment." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/presencing/) "Open your body posture (arms and legs uncrossed, face and body relaxed)." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/presencing/) "Breathe fully and easily into your belly." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/presencing/) "Take three breaths and change body posture." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/presencing/) "Appreciate yourself and another." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/presencing/) "Shift your attention from yourself to another person and then shift back to yourself (repeating frequently with an easy rhythm)." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/presencing/) "At the Speed of Life, Gay and Kathlyn Hendricks: Chapter 5, “The Presencing Principle: The Starting Point of Mindbody healing”." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/presencing/) "These practices were developed by Kathlyn (Katie) Hendricks and a founding faculty of coaches, therapists, teachers, and facilitators" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/) |
| Date | 1993 (At the Speed of Life, Bantam); current module page undated |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no note on this practice; the survey's entry on Theory U (presencing) uses the same word for Otto Scharmer's different step. |
| Sources | https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/presencing/; https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=at+the+speed+of+life+hendricks&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher&limit=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *presencing* | Presencing is the Hendricks' word for choosing to attend, with curiosity, to what is happening in the present; Otto Scharmer uses the same word for a different step. |
| *wholeness* | Wholeness is the state into which presencing welcomes all of a person's experiences. |

### Fear Melters®

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The Hendricks teach it for moments when a person feels 'overwhelmed, reactive or stuck'. |
| What the coach does | Kathlyn Hendricks's practice first has a person try on the four types of fear in the body: fight, with the chin up and weight forward; flee, with a shoulder or the weight moving back; freeze, with the body stiff and thoughts looping; and faint, with foggy thinking and a drained feeling. The person then tries four Fear Melters: oozing slowly 'as if you are warm chocolate oozing off a spoon', rooting with a wide stance and bent knees, wiggling from the fingers and toes to the core, and 'love scoops', gathering energy and love toward oneself. The page says the moves 'restore presence and allow choice'. |
| Steps | (1) The person tries on the four types of fear: fight, flee, freeze, and faint, each with its described posture and movement. (2) The person oozes: moves slowly 'as if you are warm chocolate oozing off a spoon', including the jaw, and slows their words. (3) The person roots: widens the stance, bends the knees, spreads the toes, and imagines, in the page's words, 'beautiful roots extending from your feet into the nourishment of the earth'. (4) The person wiggles: fingers and toes first, then hips and shoulders, and finally the core. (5) The person does love scoops: reaches out as if gathering energy and love and brings the hands to the head, chest, belly, or wherever love is needed. (6) The person notices the shifts in the body; the page recommends about two minutes. |
| What it does | The practice has a person identify which of four fear responses is running and change it with slow oozing, a wide stance, wiggling, or gathering movements. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes a shift from a fear state toward presence and choice more likely by changing how the body moves; the shift happens in the person's own body. The page supplies the four fear descriptions and the four movements. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The module page and poster give the moves for a person to do alone. |
| The evidence | The page states that 'Research has shown that doing two minutes of Fear Melters® achieves the best results' but names no study, and the sources opened contain none. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The practice treats fight, flee, freeze, and faint as the body's fear responses and changes them through movement rather than argument. Timing and pacing: The practice is designed for the moment of overwhelm and takes about two minutes. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The practice changes a fear state directly through movement, while the book asks the reader to meet a contraction and learn what it protects. The practice's own words, 'to shift our state', fit the corpus's third theory, a state that is changed, more than its second. |
| Limits and critiques | The page's claim that research supports a two-minute dose is unsourced on the page. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Fear Melters® is a technique developed by Kathlyn Hendricks to shift our state and get unstuck." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "Fear Melters® restore presence and allow choice." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "FIGHT: Chin comes up and out; weight is forward" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "FREEZE: a person’s body becomes tight and stiff, may be accompanied by a nervous smile." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "FAINT: thinking becomes foggy, confused, the person feels drained, sleepy" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "OOZE: Begin to move slowly as if you are warm chocolate oozing off a spoon." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "ROOT: Widen your stance and bend your knees a little or a lot." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "WIGGLE: Begin to wiggle your fingers and toes, eventually wiggle your hips and shoulders" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "LOVE SCOOPS: Reach your arms out around you as if you are gathering energy and love towards you." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "Research has shown that doing two minutes of Fear Melters® achieves the best results." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "Use simple movements to shift from fear into choice when you’re feeling overwhelmed, reactive or stuck." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "imagine beautiful roots extending from your feet into the nourishment of the earth" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/) "dispel the fear trance" (https://bigleapcoaches.com) |
| Date | undated on the module page (Restoring Resourcefulness, a program of the Foundation for Conscious Living) |
| In the vault | `notes/suffering is contraction never corruption.md`; The vault's doctrine reads suffering as protective contraction; the Hendricks name four bodily forms of it and offer a movement for each, which the vault does not hold. |
| Sources | https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/fear-melters/; https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/; https://bigleapcoaches.com |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *fight, flee, freeze, faint* | These are the four fear responses the practice teaches a person to recognize in posture and movement. |
| *Fear Melters* | Fear Melters are four movements, ooze, root, wiggle, and love scoops, meant to shift a person out of a fear response. |
| *fear trance* | The fear trance is the program's name for the state in which fear runs a person's responses. |

### F.A.C.T.

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The Hendricks teach it for when a person is 'stuck or struggling with a decision or issue'. |
| What the coach does | The page calls F.A.C.T. 'a roadmap for moving from stress to ease' and says it combines the skills of the program's other practices; the text read does not spell out the letters. A person picks a place in the room where the issue seems located, turns toward and away from it with the whole body, and then faces it, asking with a pleasant 'Hmmm' what they have not fully faced. The person then asks what is hardest to accept, alternates 'I want to accept this' with 'and I don’t want to', asks 'What do I really want?' with varied tones and movements, and ends by choosing one simple, pleasurable action step with a time. |
| Steps | (1) The person picks a place in the space around them where the question or issue seems to be located. (2) The person turns their body toward the issue, explores for a couple of minutes how they turn toward and away from it, and then faces it directly. (3) With an open posture and a pleasant 'Hmmm' on the out-breath, the person asks: 'Hmmm…What about this question/issue have I not fully faced directly?' (4) The person relaxes the breath and asks: 'Hmmm…What’s the hardest thing for me to accept about this?' (5) Holding out one hand, the person says 'I want to accept this'; holding out the other, they say 'Hmmm…and I don’t want to,' or 'I feel scared', and they go back and forth between the two. (6) With small friendly movements and varied tones of voice, the person asks: 'Hmmm…What do I really want?' (7) Moving around the room, the person asks: 'Hmmm… What is one simple, pleasurable action step that will lead me to what I really want?', writes down the step and when they agree to do it, and appreciates themselves. |
| What it does | The practice has a person physically face an issue, alternate wanting and not wanting to accept it, ask what they really want, and choose one pleasurable step. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it more likely that a person faces an issue, accepts what is hard about it, and chooses an action; every answer is the person's. The page supplies the sequence, the questions, and the movements. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The module page gives the sequence for a person to do alone. |
| The evidence | The source is the program's module page; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Decisions: The practice ends in one chosen action and an agreed time to do it. Remaining the author of one's own change: Every answer comes from the person's own questions, asked aloud in their own voice. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The practice moves from facing to action within minutes, while the book's arc takes a reader through a longer sequence before integration. The practice's own words, facing and accepting what is hard before choosing, fit the corpus's second theory, and the practice ends in an action. |
| Quoted from its sources | "F.A.C.T. is a roadmap for moving from stress to ease." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) "This tool integrates the skills from the other modules." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) "Pick a place in the space around you where the question or issue seems located." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) "Turn your body towards the issue so that you are facing it." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) "“Hmmm…What about this question/issue have I not fully faced directly?”" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) "“Hmmm…What’s the hardest thing for me to accept about this?”" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) "say “I want to accept this.”" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) "say “Hmmm…and I don’t want to,” or “I feel scared.”" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) "“Hmmm…What do I really want?”" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) "“Hmmm… What is one simple, pleasurable action step that will lead me to what I really want?”" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) "When you find yourself stuck or struggling with a decision or issue in your life" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) "Appreciate yourself for doing F.A.C.T." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/) |
| Date | undated on the module page (Restoring Resourcefulness, a program of the Foundation for Conscious Living) |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no note on this practice. |
| Sources | https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/f-a-c-t/; https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *F.A.C.T.* | F.A.C.T. is the program's name for a four-part roadmap from stress to ease that ends in a chosen action. |
| *Hmmm* | The 'Hmmm' is a pleasant sound made on the out-breath while asking each question, used to keep the inquiry relaxed. |

### Matching

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The Hendricks teach it for moments when a person feels something but has no words for it. |
| What the coach does | Matching brings, in the page's words, 'congruence between our inner experience and our outer expression'. With gentle curiosity and a 'Hmmmm…', a person notices breath, sensations, words, sounds, the speed of thoughts, or emotions, and moves the hands and arms to 'sculpt or paint' what they notice, for example fast, jagged movements for fast, jagged thoughts. The person continues 'until you feel complete'; the page names the result as a natural settling of the breath and a sense of being more connected to oneself. Variants match with sound, such as growling for anger, or with words that need not make sense. |
| Steps | (1) With gentle curiosity, the person turns attention to their inner experience and says 'Hmmmm…' aloud or inwardly. (2) The person notices breath, body sensations, words, sounds, the speed of thoughts, or emotions. (3) The person moves the hands and arms to 'sculpt or paint what you notice inside'. (4) The person lets the body keep moving 'until you feel complete'. (5) The person may instead match with sound, such as growling for anger, or with words that need not be complete sentences. |
| What it does | The practice has a person move their hands, body, or voice to match an inner state until it settles. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it more likely that an inner state is expressed and settles; the state and its expression are the person's. The page supplies the instruction to match and a list of what to attend to. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The module page gives the practice for a person to do alone. |
| The evidence | The source is the program's module page; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The practice names its end sign, a natural settling of the breath. Being seen and recognized by another: The practice lets a person express what they feel before they have words, which makes an inner state visible to others. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book works in words; matching works through movement and sound before words, so a reader alone could do it only if the text gives the instruction. The practice's own words, moving 'until you feel complete', fit the corpus's second theory, a resisted feeling that is allowed to complete. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Matching is a way of bringing congruence between our inner experience and our outer expression." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/matching/) "With gentle curiosity, turn your attention to your inner experience, saying “Hmmmm…” either out loud or inside." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/matching/) "Begin to move your hands and arms to sculpt or paint what you notice inside." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/matching/) "Continue to allow your body to move until you feel complete." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/matching/) "The end result of matching is a natural settling of your breath and a sense of being more connected to yourself." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/matching/) "Anger might be matched with growling." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/matching/) "These do not need to be complete sentences, nor do they need to make sense." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/matching/) "expresses coherently when we may not have the words" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/matching/) "None of the three uses "completes itself."" (archive/reading — the completion family (hudson's art of accomplishment, mclaren's language of emotions).md) "sculpt or paint what you notice inside" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/matching/) |
| Date | undated on the module page (Restoring Resourcefulness, a program of the Foundation for Conscious Living) |
| In the vault | archive/reading — the completion family (hudson's art of accomplishment, mclaren's language of emotions).md; The completion-family reading found that 'completes itself' is the corpus's wording rather than Hudson's. The Hendricks' instruction to move 'until you feel complete' is a documented completion instruction from the body-centered line, and the Conscious Leadership Group's third commitment uses the same instruction in coaching. |
| Sources | https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/matching/; https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *matching* | Matching is making an outer movement, sound, or phrase that has the same shape as an inner state. |
| *complete* | Complete, in this practice, names the point at which the breath settles and the person feels more connected to themselves. |

### The Persona Interview

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | The Hendricks use it after a reaction that surprised the person or seemed bigger than the situation called for. |
| What the coach does | The Persona Interview starts from a recent reaction that 'surprised you or seemed bigger than what was called for'. The person blurts about it for 30 seconds, notes the body sensations and feelings, names the persona, exaggerates its posture, gestures, and voice, and physically steps forward into it. An interviewer addresses the persona by name and asks only six questions, about what matters most to it, what it is proud of, when it first appeared, who it learned its style from, what it fears most, and what it most wants. Afterward the person steps out, does a pleasurable movement, and appreciates themselves, and the page advises that 'Analysis is more effective after a period of integrating the experience.' |
| Steps | (1) The person brings to mind a recent situation in which their reaction surprised them or seemed bigger than the situation called for. (2) The person takes 30 seconds to express and blurt about it, and then notices body sensations and feelings. (3) The person names the persona by answering 'What would this persona like to be called?' (4) The person exaggerates the persona's voice, gestures, stance, and facial expressions, and physically steps forward into it. (5) The interviewer addresses the persona by name and asks only these questions: 'what’s the most important thing to you?', 'what are you most proud of?', 'when did you make your first appearance?', 'who did you learn your style from?', 'what are you most afraid of?', and 'what do you most want?' (6) The person steps out of the persona, does a pleasurable movement such as Fear Melters, and appreciates themselves. (7) Later, the person may express the persona's gift more directly in some part of their life. |
| What it does | The practice has a person step into an exaggerated role, name it, and answer six fixed questions in its voice before stepping out. |
| What is facilitated | The interview makes it more likely that a person distinguishes a learned role from their essence and finds the role's gift; all content comes from the person. The interviewer supplies six fixed questions and the structure of stepping in and out. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The interviewer asks six fixed questions, and the person supplies everything the persona says. |
| The evidence | The source is the program's module page; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The interview treats a surprising reaction as a role learned to secure attention and asks when it first appeared and what it fears. Recognition: The page says 'all personas carry aspects of essence', so the interview looks for what the person already is inside the role. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book's doctrine is recognition of what never left; the persona teaching agrees that essence is present and hidden, but it reaches essence by exaggerating and befriending a role. None of the corpus's five theories names this difficulty; the practice's own words place it in roles learned to get attention that hide a person's essence. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Bring to mind a recent situation where your reaction may have surprised you or seemed bigger than what was called for." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "Take 30 seconds to express and blurt about this experience." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "What would this persona like to be called?" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "The key to really benefitting from this activity is exaggeration (voice, gestures, stance)." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "When you are ready to let your persona fully respond, physically take a step forward into this persona." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "Address the persona by name with each question and use just these questions for the most effective result." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "what’s the most important thing to you?" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "when did you make your first appearance?" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "who did you learn your style from?" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "what are you most afraid of?" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "Analysis is more effective after a period of integrating the experience." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "The Persona Interview will assist you in distinguishing persona from essence." (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "Everyone wore silly costumes, exaggerating and playing with different aspects of themselves." (https://conscious.is/blog/a-bow-to-gay-katie-hendricks/) "what are you most proud of?" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) "what do you most want?" (https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/) |
| Date | undated on the module page (Restoring Resourcefulness, a program of the Foundation for Conscious Living) |
| In the vault | `notes/you are already free and the work removes a misunderstanding rather than adding an attainment.md`; The vault holds that the work removes a misunderstanding rather than adding an attainment; the persona teaching's claim that every persona includes aspects of essence is a body-centered form of the same claim. The survey holds Voice Dialogue, in which a facilitator also speaks with a self by name. |
| Sources | https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/modules/persona/; https://hendricks.com/restoring-resourcefulness/; https://conscious.is/blog/a-bow-to-gay-katie-hendricks/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *persona* | A persona is a role a person took on to get attention, which the Hendricks say carries some quality of the person's essence. |
| *essence* | Essence is who a person is before the roles, which the interview helps distinguish from the persona. |

### The Ten-Minute Heart Talk, with the Ten-Minute Stuff Talk

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Couples use it twice a week, and the Hendricks recommend it for problems that keep returning. |
| What the coach does | The Hendricks recommend two ten-minute sit-down conversations a week: a Stuff Talk for 'Necessary Trivia', in which each item ends in a 'Conversation for Action' ('Who agrees to do What by When'), and a Heart Talk for feelings, wants, and needs. The Heart Talk opens with a minute of touch and breathing together, then each partner states willingness and commitment to say what needs saying in ten minutes, then the partners alternate speaking feelings and wants in first-person forms while the listener keeps an open posture and generates 'wonder rather than blame', and it closes with a silent moment and a spoken appreciation. They hold that problems recur partly because people have 'an unconscious commitment to perpetuating the problem that was bigger than their conscious intention to solve it'. |
| Steps | (1) The partners begin with touch and presence: each sits close enough to rest one hand on their own heart and one on the partner's, and they breathe together for a minute without talking. (2) Each partner states willingness and commitment to communicate everything that needs saying in the next ten minutes, and each gets a 'Yes' to that commitment. (3) The partners take turns speaking feelings, wants, and needs, sentence by sentence or by timer, using forms such as 'I’m scared about . . .' and 'I felt hurt when . . .', avoiding forms such as 'You always . . .', and stating wants as 'Here’s something I’d really like . . .'. (4) The listener keeps an open posture, breathes easily, and generates wonder rather than blame. (5) The partners end with appreciation: they keep the ten-minute limit or agree together to continue, pause in silent connection, and each says 'I appreciate . . .'. |
| What it does | The practice gives a couple a timed, touch-first structure for saying feelings and wants without blame and ending in appreciation. |
| What is facilitated | The structure makes it more likely that partners say their feelings and wants regularly and keep their agreements; the content is the partners'. The book supplies the time limit, the opening touch, and the sentence forms. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The couple follows the book's structure without a facilitator and supplies all the content. |
| The evidence | The source is the book's chapter; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The Heart Talk sets touch, a time limit, and an agreed 'Yes' before any hard feeling is spoken. Conflict: The talk gives first-person forms for anger and hurt and names blaming forms to avoid. Being seen and recognized by another: The Hendricks write that having someone listen regularly to your evolving heart creates 'a kind of sweet closeness'. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The practice is for a couple in an ongoing relationship, while the book is read by one person; its ten-minute limit sets a pace the book's longer arc does not use. The practice's premise, an unconscious commitment to keeping a problem going, fits the corpus's fourth theory, and its feelings step fits the second. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Most couples suffer from a lack of intimacy—but the loss often goes undetected because it erodes so slowly they don’t notice it." (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "they didn’t realize they had an unconscious commitment to perpetuating the problem that was bigger than their conscious intention to solve it."
