(https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "Who agrees to do What by When" (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "Sit across from each other, close enough so you can rest one hand on your heart and one hand on your partner’s heart." (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "Enjoy a minute of breathing together without talking." (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "It’s important to get a “Yes” to that commitment" (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "“I’m scared about . . .”" (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "“I felt hurt when . . .”" (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "“You always . . .”" (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "“Here’s something I’d really like . . .”" (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "generating wonder rather than blame" (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "Make it simple: “I appreciate . . .”" (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "heart creates a kind of sweet closeness that can permeate your" (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "You don’t even need a partner to get value out of a Stuff Talk." (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) "started to do our own Stuff Talks in the early 1980s" (https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf) |
| Date | 2015 (Conscious Loving Ever After, Hay House, ch. 8); the Stuff Talk in their own marriage since the early 1980s |
| In the vault | `notes/the field between two people does work.md`; The vault holds that the field between two people does work; the Heart Talk is a scheduled procedure for tending that field in a couple, opened with touch and shared breathing. |
| Sources | https://foundationforconsciousliving.org/wp-content/uploads/consciouslovingeverafter-freechapter.pdf; https://openlibrary.org/search.json?q=conscious+loving+ever+after+hendricks&fields=title,author_name,first_publish_year,publisher&limit=2 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Heart Talk* | The Heart Talk is a ten-minute structured conversation for feelings, wants, and needs. |
| *Stuff Talk* | The Stuff Talk is a ten-minute conversation that gathers the practical business of a relationship into one sitting. |
| *Conversation for Action* | A Conversation for Action completes one sentence: who agrees to do what by when. |
| *unconscious commitment* | An unconscious commitment is a hidden commitment to keeping a problem going that is stronger than the conscious wish to solve it. |

### Naming the feeling under the gesture (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hendricks uses it when a person describes anger and their body suggests another feeling. |
| What the coach does | Consulting with a senior executive who often exploded in anger at colleagues, Hendricks noticed that he touched his chest several times while talking about it and said, 'You know, Mort, it looks to me like underneath that anger that you blew up is probably some sadness.' The executive agreed. Hendricks then showed him where feelings are usually held, anger in the upper body, 'in our jaws, fists and shoulders', sadness in the middle of the chest, and fear lower in the belly, which he teaches in seminars as 'a parfait of feeling with the anger on top, the sadness in the middle and the fear at the bottom of everything.' |
| Steps | (1) Hendricks watches the person's gestures while they describe a charged event. (2) He notices where the hand goes, for example to the chest. (3) He offers a tentative name: 'it looks to me like underneath that anger that you blew up is probably some sadness.' (4) He waits for the person to confirm or reject the name. (5) He teaches where feelings are usually held: anger in the jaws, fists, and shoulders; sadness in the middle of the chest; fear in the belly. |
| What it does | The move names, tentatively, a feeling that the person's gesture suggests lies under the one they are expressing. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it more likely that a person recognizes a feeling beneath another feeling; the person confirms or rejects the name. Hendricks supplies an observation and a tentative word. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. Hendricks offers a tentative name for a feeling he sees, and the person confirms or rejects it. |
| The evidence | The source is one anecdote; Hendricks's account of where feelings are held is his teaching claim, untested in the sources opened. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: The move is a facilitator reporting what he sees in the person's body and offering a word the person can confirm. Protection and defense: The move reads anger as covering sadness, a layered reading in which the surface feeling protects a deeper one. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move depends on a facilitator watching the person's body; a reader alone has no one to see the gesture, though a text could teach where feelings are usually held. The move's own words, finding sadness under anger, fit the corpus's second theory, a feeling that has not been felt. |
| Quoted from its sources | "I pointed out one little piece of body language, which was when he was talking about that, he touched his chest a few times." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "“You know, Mort, it looks to me like underneath that anger that you blew up is probably some sadness.”" (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "And I showed him how most times our anger is kind of stored up in the upper part of our body, in our jaws, fists and shoulders and that kind of thing, whereas sadness is more down in the middle part of our chest and fear." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "we’re a parfait of feeling with the anger on top, the sadness in the middle and the fear at the bottom of everything." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "So I pointed out something to Mort that turned out to be life changing and I think changed something about the culture of the company too." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "I had the great opportunity back during the ’90s after my book, the Corporate Mystique, came out." (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) "this podcast was actually recorded not today but in early March" (https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/) |
| Date | 1990s consulting at Dell, as Hendricks told it in an interview recorded in early March 2020 (transcript published 3 May 2020) |
| In the vault | not yet met; The vault holds no note on reading feeling from gesture. |
| Sources | https://caneel.com/transcripts/gay-hendricks-conscious-luck-genius-spirals-transcript/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *parfait of feeling* | The parfait of feeling is Hendricks's image for layered feeling, with anger on top, sadness in the middle, and fear at the bottom. |

## Thomas Hübl

| Move | Type | When the coach uses it | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| Collective trauma as frozen, unintegrated life | a theory | Hübl states this account at the opening of his programs, talks, and books on collective trauma. | The account tells people that their stuck reactions carry unprocessed individual, ancestral, and collective history that a group can help integrate. |
| Collective Trauma Integration Process (CTIP) | a technique | Hübl uses the process in his retreats, trainings, and large events on inherited and collective trauma, such as gatherings of Germans and Israelis around the Holocaust. | The process builds a group's shared safety and then lets buried collective history come up, be digested, and yield learning. |
| Synchronization and sensing (the 4-sync meditation) | a technique | Hübl assigns the practice for calm moments between difficulties, and uses a group version at the start of his process. | The practice has the person, or the group, build a steadier state that they can return to under stress. |
| The dyadic share with embodied witnessing | a technique | Hübl uses the form throughout his retreats and trainings, whenever a participant wants to share or ask a question. | The form has one participant work with the facilitator while the whole group witnesses and then reflects. |
| You know your process | a move | Hübl uses the move when a participant asks him to interpret their inner state. | The move hands the question back to the participant and reflects what their own answer showed. |
| Do you have a sense that I'm here? | a move | Hübl uses the move when a participant is in a strong feeling during a share, to bring the relationship into awareness. | The move has the participant notice and report how present the facilitator feels to them, and has the facilitator report back. |
| Include it rather than overcome it | a move | Hübl uses the move when a participant names a numbness, distance, or protection and tries to push past it. | The move has the participant include a protective response in what they feel instead of trying to overcome it. |
| Soften a bit into the fear | a move | Hübl uses the move when a participant is in fear or anxiety during a share. | The move has the participant, with the facilitator and group, soften their bracing so that the fear can be digested. |
| Honoring numbness and the denial phase | a move | Hübl uses the move at the start of collective trauma work, when a group or a person meets numbness or a wish to look away. | The move treats numbness and denial as survival intelligence to be honored and held until feeling can begin. |
| Sequencing collective trauma work by how close the violence is | a technique | Hübl applies the sequence when deciding what kind of collective trauma work a region in or after conflict can take on. | The sequence orders the work from ending violence and giving aid, through trauma healing and reparation, to group processes. |
| The facilitator's own integration work | a move | Hübl states the requirement when training facilitators and describing what his work asks of those who lead it. | The move has the facilitator keep doing their own trauma integration so they can tell their own reactions from the group's. |

### Collective trauma as frozen, unintegrated life (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| When the coach uses it | Hübl states this account at the opening of his programs, talks, and books on collective trauma. |
| What the coach does | Hübl holds that trauma, whether individual, ancestral, or collective, leaves parts of life 'frozen': a 'nonmoving, frozen part of life' that looks stable, while the rest of life keeps moving, and 'the frozen parts of life create the tension with the original movement of life. That's what we call pain and suffering.' Trauma that is not integrated repeats itself, and collective trauma is systemic and cumulative, so that its effects seem 'normal' to people born into them. He names separation as the basic symptom: 'I feel numb, I feel dissociated, I feel removed, I feel disengaged, I feel hyper-activated.' He holds that good trauma work restores 'the fabric of life' and an ethical line that was violated. |
| What it does | The account tells people that their stuck reactions carry unprocessed individual, ancestral, and collective history that a group can help integrate. |
| What is facilitated | On Hübl's account a group's shared presence lets frozen material come up, be digested, and be integrated, which ends its repetition; the facilitator supplies the conditions and the holding, not the content that emerges. |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| The evidence | No controlled study of Hübl's process was found; the 2022 article describing it is a descriptive framework illustrated by one participant's account, and his site's claim to integrate 'evidence-based research' cites no study of the process itself. In the 2020 interview Hübl draws on polyvagal theory and on inherited epigenetic stress; Wikipedia's article on polyvagal theory reports that critics find 'broad consensus among experts' that its physiological assumptions are untenable, and its article on transgenerational epigenetic inheritance reports 'severe limitations' in applying it to human trauma. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Hübl locates suffering in systemic, inherited trauma carried by everyone ('We are all shareholders of the collective trauma field'), and in acute conflict he puts changing conditions on the ground first. Harm and its conditions: Hübl names unintegrated collective trauma as a root cause of many social problems, including school shootings and climate change, which are causal claims stated without evidence in the sources read. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus summarizes Hübl as holding that 'what was never metabolized lives on as absence'; in the texts read his main image is a frozen, stuck part of life that creates tension, which is a presence held in place rather than an absence, while his named symptoms (numbness, dissociation, disengagement) do describe absence. The book's privation is an absence where something should be, so the correspondence holds for the symptoms and is weaker for the mechanism. |
| Limits and critiques | The claims that collective trauma causes school shootings and climate change, and his reliance on contested polyvagal and epigenetic science, are the weaknesses documented in the sources read; no published criticism of his conduct was found in two searches. His own site says he was inspired by Ken Wilber's writings from the age of twenty, and Wikipedia records Wilhelm Reich among his early readings. |
| Quoted from its sources | "They're frozen, so that frozen substance looks, in a way, stable, but it's a nonmoving, frozen part of life." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "Now, the nature of life, as we know, is movement. Everything is changing and the frozen parts of life create the tension with the original movement of life. That's what we call pain and suffering." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "And if it's not being integrated, like any personal trauma, it's going to repeat itself." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "The basic trauma symptom is that I feel separate, because I feel numb, I feel dissociated, I feel removed, I feel disengaged, I feel hyper-activated." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "I believe every good trauma work leads to a restoration of the fabric of life." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "We are all shareholders of the collective trauma field." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "When the deeper roots of this dysfunction are not seen, we simply experience it as "normal" – it is the water we are all swimming in." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "Healing from collective trauma cannot happen for large groups and countries while violent conflict is actively unfolding and people are suffering." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "If we do not work to heal the wounds of collective trauma during times of relative peace, we are destined to repeat the same patterns of conflict over and over again." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "That's usually when people go into deep mental imbalances or when people run into schools and shoot around." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "the disembodiment that is an effect of trauma is a root cause of the climate change to start with" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "And then there is another beautiful finding of Stephen Porges that spoke about or speaks about the Polyvagal Theory" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "because my grandparents were deeply traumatized and epigenetically, I'm carrying this." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "there is broad consensus among experts [...] that each basic physiological assumption of the polyvagal theory is untenable." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyvagal_theory) "highlighted severe limitations in applying TEI to human behavioral cohorts." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgenerational_epigenetic_inheritance) "holding a higher source of wisdom that even within the absence, the numbing, and the denial, leads to an opening." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "I'd been inspired by inveterate sages and philosophers like Sri Aurobindo, Ramana Maharshi, and the writings of American philosopher Ken Wilber since I was twenty years old." (https://thomashuebl.com/about/) |
| Date | Stated in Hübl's interview with Tami Simon on Healing Collective Trauma (Sounds True, published 2 December 2020); the book appeared in 2020; restated in his December 2025 article on crisis and collective healing |
| In the vault | `notes/what was never metabolized lives on as absence and hubl reached it through inherited trauma.md`; The note is marked privated, meaning complete except in one named way, because Hübl's primary work had not been read, and it asks whether his 'absence' is structurally the book's privation. In the texts read here his mechanism is 'frozen' life that creates tension, and 'absence' appears once, in a co-authored 2022 article beside 'the numbing, and the denial'; so the vault's formulation is a fair summary of his symptoms and not of his mechanism. The independence question the note raises is confirmed from his own about page, which names Wilber. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/; https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/; https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168; https://thomashuebl.com/about/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_H%C3%BCbl; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyvagal_theory; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgenerational_epigenetic_inheritance |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *collective trauma* | the effects of large-scale disasters or violence carried by whole communities and passed on through generations |
| *frozen* | Hübl's word for parts of a person or society stuck in the past and unable to move with life |
| *integration* | the process by which unprocessed trauma comes back into awareness, is digested, and yields learning |
| *collective denial* | a group's shared turning away from its unprocessed history |

### Collective Trauma Integration Process (CTIP)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Hübl uses the process in his retreats, trainings, and large events on inherited and collective trauma, such as gatherings of Germans and Israelis around the Holocaust. |
| What the coach does | In Hübl's process a group first builds what he calls coherence, through synchronization and sensing practices and relational exercises that make people feel each other, until the room is safe enough for buried material to surface. Unprocessed trauma, for example around racism or the Holocaust, is then given 'an authentic protected space where it can be voiced and it can come up', followed by 'digestion spaces' and an integration phase in which the group draws out 'the pearl of wisdom within the shadow'. Large versions need skilled facilitators and a team of therapists, because when many people in the room share a history, one person's touching it can set many into a strong process at once. |
| Steps | (1) The facilitator builds coherence: synchronization and sensing practices and relational exercises in which participants become aware of each other. (2) The group holds the first phase of denial ('What am I doing here?') in presence until another level arises. (3) Unprocessed trauma is given a protected space in which it can be voiced and come up. (4) The group is given 'digestion spaces', because 'every trauma needs to be digested'. (5) The group moves into integration, drawing learning out of what came up. (6) For large groups, a trained team of facilitators and therapists holds participants who go into strong processes. |
| What it does | The process builds a group's shared safety and then lets buried collective history come up, be digested, and yield learning. |
| What is facilitated | The process makes it easier for a group to let buried shared history come up and be digested together; the facilitator supplies coherence, safety, and a trained team, and the participants supply the history. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The facilitator shapes the process and holds the group, while what comes up is the participants' own material; Hübl says he tries not to 'impose a methodology' on a region's healing. |
| The evidence | No controlled study of Hübl's process was found; the 2022 article describing it is a descriptive framework illustrated by one participant's account, and his site's claim to integrate 'evidence-based research' cites no study of the process itself. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The process builds its container before anything is opened: coherence, safety, digestion spaces, and a team able to hold many people in strong reactions at once. Change at the scale of a team or organization: Hübl says the process can be applied in 'smaller versions' in organizations facing, for example, questions of racial justice. Harm and its conditions: Hübl names the risk that in a room sharing one history many people can go into 'a very strong process' at once, and answers it with large trained teams. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The process holds that a group can let up content 'way too threatening for an individual to allow', which the book's solitary reader cannot draw on; the book's form addresses one reader, and Hübl's process addresses a room. |
| Limits and critiques | The process deliberately invites trauma to surface in large groups; its safety depends on the team's skill, and no outcome study of it was found. |
| Quoted from its sources | "that we allow for the trauma in the room to have an authentic protected space where it can be voiced and it can come up, and that we provide digestion spaces, because every trauma needs to be digested." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "And then there is an integration process, and through the integration process, we get the learning, like the pearl of wisdom within the shadow." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "and then the system is kind of connected enough to allow deeper content to come up that is way too threatening for an individual to allow" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "So, we are all bringing a certain amount of inner coherence" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "And we also wrote this in the book, that I think it needs skilled facilitators to hold many people." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "we brought many, many therapists and we had a huge team to hold the group" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "And the reason is that if in regions—let's say in the US, we do such a process around racism or the Native American genocide, many people in the room share in a way a similar traumatization." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "This model promotes a safe exploration of sharing and reflection, guided by a facilitation process that supports radical openness, transparent communication, mindful awareness, and refined relational competencies." (https://thomashuebl.com/about/) "In the initial stages of a TIP, a group is guided through a synchronization and sensing practice." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "This is a kind of group process that requires highly skilled facilitation." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "it has always felt important to not bring in assumptions from the outside nor impose a methodology" (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "So, when a few people touched it, it might be that suddenly many people are in a very strong process." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "but I think it's being able to apply smaller versions the same principles to do integration work in groups and organizations, definitely." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) |
| Date | Stated in Hübl's interview with Tami Simon on Healing Collective Trauma (Sounds True, published 2 December 2020); the book appeared in 2020; developed over the two decades after retreats in the early 2000s; described in 2022 as the Trauma Integration Process (TIP) |
| In the vault | `notes/the solitary form stands against seven witnesses who hold a second person constitutive.md`; The note counts Hübl, unread, among the seven thinkers the vault holds as requiring a second person for the inner life. His own words bear on the vault's question whether that requirement is strict or a matter of degree: the group allows up content 'way too threatening for an individual to allow', but he also says the first capacity is the person's own and a group is a further step ('there's nowhere written in the universe that we have to do it alone'). Read here, the requirement is a matter of degree; this is the researcher's reading. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/; https://thomashuebl.com/about/; https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168; https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_H%C3%BCbl |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *coherence* | Hübl's word for a state in which mind, emotion, body, and relationship are aligned, which a group can build together as a shared resource |
| *digestion* | the stage in which surfaced trauma is taken in and processed, which Hübl compares to digesting food |
| *integration* | the final stage, in which what was frozen becomes available as learning |
| *trauma eruption* | the sudden surfacing of collective historical pain in a group, which Hübl says first happened unplanned in his German retreats |

### Synchronization and sensing (the 4-sync meditation)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Hübl assigns the practice for calm moments between difficulties, and uses a group version at the start of his process. |
| What the coach does | Hübl teaches a guided practice he calls a 4-sync meditation, 'a meditation to synchronize different parts of our nervous system', to be done several times a week in calm moments so that the resource is available when a person is triggered. In his group process a similar synchronization and sensing practice opens the work, as participants attune to one another and sense wounds that 'may be silently held but can be sensed'. He presents regulation as preparation for engagement, not as withdrawal: 'Taking the time to reflect, process, and tune in with yourself is not selfish.' |
| Steps | (1) The person follows the guided 4-sync meditation, available as video or audio. (2) The person practices it several times a week, in relaxed and safe moments. (3) The person notices 'both the awareness effects and the regulation effects'. (4) When triggered, the person returns to the practiced state as a resource. (5) In a group, the facilitator opens with a synchronization and sensing practice in which participants attune to one another. |
| What it does | The practice has the person, or the group, build a steadier state that they can return to under stress. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it easier for a person to return to a steadier state when stress comes, which Hübl calls finding 'a friendly relationship to stress'; the person does the practice, alone or in a group. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. Alone, a person follows Hübl's guided recording; in a group, the facilitator guides the practice at the start of the process. |
| The evidence | No controlled study of Hübl's process was found; the 2022 article describing it is a descriptive framework illustrated by one participant's account, and his site's claim to integrate 'evidence-based research' cites no study of the process itself. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: Hübl tells people to build the resource in calm times so it is available in hard ones. The container that holds people while they change: In the group process the shared synchronization is the first layer of the container. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Hübl presents the practice as synchronizing parts of the nervous system, a physiological claim, while the book makes no physiological claim for its practices. |
| Limits and critiques | The practice's stated mechanism, synchronizing parts of the nervous system, is not supported by a study in the sources read. |
| Quoted from its sources | "So, I will introduce you to what we call a 4-sync meditation. It is a meditation to synchronize different parts of our nervous system." (https://thomashuebl.com/grounding-practice-for-times-of-crisis/) "And I invite you to practice that multiple times a week and to notice both the awareness effects and the regulation effects." (https://thomashuebl.com/grounding-practice-for-times-of-crisis/) "Practicing in more relaxed and safe moments helps us to build a resource that will be available when we really need it" (https://thomashuebl.com/grounding-practice-for-times-of-crisis/) "When stress comes up, we will have more tools to regulate, to find a friendly relationship to stress and not try to get rid of it." (https://thomashuebl.com/grounding-practice-for-times-of-crisis/) "Taking the time to reflect, process, and tune in with yourself is not selfish." (https://thomashuebl.com/grounding-practice-for-times-of-crisis/) "In the initial stages of a TIP, a group is guided through a synchronization and sensing practice." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "These wounds may be silently held but can be sensed." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) |
| Date | Described in Hübl and Lori Shridhare, 'The Tender Narrator Who Sees Beyond Time', Journal of Awareness-Based Systems Change 2(2), 30 November 2022 (group form); the solo recording is on an undated page of his site |
| In the vault | `notes/thomas hubl.md`; The vault's Hübl note records his work as collective by design and asks whether the collective requirement is strict; this solo practice, which he assigns for use several times a week, is evidence that part of his work is done alone. |
| Sources | https://thomashuebl.com/grounding-practice-for-times-of-crisis/; https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_H%C3%BCbl; https://thomashuebl.com/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *4-sync meditation* | Hübl's guided practice for synchronizing different parts of the nervous system |
| *resource* | a practiced state a person can return to when stressed |
| *regulation* | a person's capacity to calm and steady their own nervous system |

### The dyadic share with embodied witnessing (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Hübl uses the form throughout his retreats and trainings, whenever a participant wants to share or ask a question. |
| What the coach does | In a session of Hübl's process a participant raises their hand and talks with the facilitator for 15 to 50 minutes while the rest of the group, 100 to 1,000 people, practice becoming 'embodied witnesses' by observing their own emotions as they attune to the speaker. The facilitator asks clarifying questions, gives pointers for the speaker to notice somatic, emotional, and mental states, and at the end returns the speaker to a regulated state, with support from an assistant team if needed. Afterward the whole group reflects together or divides into triads to sense the share's impact on each of them. |
| Steps | (1) The facilitator invites shares, sometimes on a named event, such as the war in Ukraine. (2) A participant raises their hand and speaks with the facilitator. (3) The facilitator 'invites the other participants to practice observing their emotions as they attune to the person speaking.' (4) For 15 to 50 minutes the facilitator asks clarifying questions and gives pointers to the speaker's somatic, emotional, and mental states. (5) The facilitator and speaker conclude together, 'returning the speaker to a regulated state', with the assistant team available. (6) The group reflects together or divides into triads to sense the impact of the share. |
| What it does | The form has one participant work with the facilitator while the whole group witnesses and then reflects. |
| What is facilitated | The form makes it easier for one person to process something in front of many people who hold it with them, and for the witnesses to process their own responses; the facilitator supplies the questions and pointers, the speaker supplies the content, and the group supplies witnessing. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| The evidence | No controlled study of Hübl's process was found; the 2022 article describing it is a descriptive framework illustrated by one participant's account, and his site's claim to integrate 'evidence-based research' cites no study of the process itself. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: The speaker is seen by the facilitator and by hundreds of witnesses who are asked to attune to them. Participation rather than watching: The audience is given its own task, embodied witnessing and later reflection in triads, so watching becomes a practice rather than spectating. The container that holds people while they change: The form ends by returning the speaker to a regulated state, with an assistant team available. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The form depends on a live facilitator and a witnessing crowd, which the book, read alone, cannot supply; it resembles the corpus's grief-witness claim, which the vault could not source in McLaren. |
| Quoted from its sources | "invites the other participants to practice observing their emotions as they attune to the person speaking." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "Throughout the time the speaker shares, and the dyadic process unfolds (15 to 50 minutes), the facilitator provides pointers for the speaker to reflect and focus on their somatic, emotional, and mental states." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "Together, the speaker and facilitator conclude the dialogue, returning the speaker to a regulated state for further reflection, which may include support from the assistant team." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "the wider group of participants—which can range from 100 to 1,000 people—practice becoming embodied witnesses." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "After the completion of a complex share that touches upon both individual and collective trauma, the larger group reflects together or divides into triads." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "The focus is on all that spontaneously arises for the participant who is sharing, not on achieving an outcome." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "tender narrator as that voice which speaks from the whole" (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "is not a framework used in the TIP as a programmatic element" (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "This "voice of the collective" may be loud or subtle." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) |
| Date | Described in Hübl and Lori Shridhare, 'The Tender Narrator Who Sees Beyond Time', Journal of Awareness-Based Systems Change 2(2), 30 November 2022 |
| In the vault | `ops/second person ecology.md`; The survey of second persons lists 'Hübl's group' as a former, marked unread; this entry records how his group actually works at a share, from a published session. |
| Sources | https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_H%C3%BCbl; https://thomashuebl.com/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *embodied witnessing* | the audience's practice of attending to their own emotional response while attuning to the speaker |
| *tender narrator* | a term the 2022 article uses for a voice that 'speaks from the whole' when a group listens this way; the article notes it is not a programmatic element of the process |
| *share* | a participant's spoken turn with the facilitator in front of the group |

### You know your process

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hübl uses the move when a participant asks him to interpret their inner state. |
| What the coach does | When a Ukrainian participant asked whether the calm she sometimes felt amid news of the war was numbness or resilience, Hübl did not answer; he asked 'So what do you think? What's your feeling? You know your process.' She answered that it was probably both. When she then described anxiety pulsing in her solar plexus and constant watching of footage, he reflected her own words back to her: 'Maybe you said it now yourself, you see that you are not numb because it has a lot going on in you.' |
| Steps | (1) The participant asks the facilitator to judge their inner state. (2) Hübl returns the question: 'So what do you think? What's your feeling? You know your process.' (3) The participant answers from their own experience. (4) Hübl points to what the participant's own words showed: 'Maybe you said it now yourself'. |
| What it does | The move hands the question back to the participant and reflects what their own answer showed. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for the participant to trust their own reading of their state; the facilitator supplies the question back and a reflection of what they said. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. Hübl hands the question back to the participant, who decides the answer. |
| The evidence | No controlled study of Hübl's process was found; the 2022 article describing it is a descriptive framework illustrated by one participant's account, and his site's claim to integrate 'evidence-based research' cites no study of the process itself. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: Hübl declines to judge the participant's state and hands the judgment back to her. Remaining the author of one's own change: The participant, not the facilitator, answers whether she is numb or resilient. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move matches the book's stance that the person decides, but it happens in front of hundreds of witnesses rather than in private reading. |
| Quoted from its sources | "So what do you think? What's your feeling? You know your process." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "Maybe you said it now yourself, you see that you are not numb because it has a lot going on in you." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) |
| Date | Described in Hübl and Lori Shridhare, 'The Tender Narrator Who Sees Beyond Time', Journal of Awareness-Based Systems Change 2(2), 30 November 2022 (a session recorded in April 2022) |
| In the vault | `notes/thomas hubl.md`; The vault's Hübl note is derived rather than sourced; this move is taken from a published transcript of his facilitation. |
| Sources | https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168; https://thomashuebl.com/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *your process* | the participant's own unfolding experience, which Hübl treats them as knowing |
| *numbness or resilience* | the participant's question whether her calm was shutting down or strength, which Hübl returned to her |

### Do you have a sense that I'm here?

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hübl uses the move when a participant is in a strong feeling during a share, to bring the relationship into awareness. |
| What the coach does | While the participant was feeling fear, Hübl asked her to check the relationship between them: 'Do you have a sense that I'm here or do I feel a bit at a distance?' She said he felt a bit distant and that she felt alone. After she included that distance, he asked her to check again, and when she said she felt more present and connected, he told her what he felt from his side ('I feel more felt') and asked her to feel both her fear and their connection, since 'That fear exists within a relational context.' |
| Steps | (1) While the participant attends to her sensations, Hübl asks: 'Do you have a sense that I'm here or do I feel a bit at a distance?' (2) The participant reports the distance she feels. (3) Hübl has her include the distance rather than overcome it (see the separate entry). (4) Hübl asks her to check the relationship again after the shift. (5) Hübl tells her what he now feels: 'Like as if you feel me more; I feel more felt.' (6) Hübl invites her to feel the fear and the connection together. |
| What it does | The move has the participant notice and report how present the facilitator feels to them, and has the facilitator report back. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for the participant to notice the relationship she is in while she feels fear, so the fear is felt with someone rather than alone; Hübl supplies his own felt report as the other half of the relationship. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| The evidence | No controlled study of Hübl's process was found; the 2022 article describing it is a descriptive framework illustrated by one participant's account, and his site's claim to integrate 'evidence-based research' cites no study of the process itself. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: Hübl checks whether the participant feels him present and reports that he feels felt, making mutual recognition part of the session. The container that holds people while they change: The relationship between facilitator and participant is itself checked and adjusted as the container. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book's reader has no second person to check in with, so this move is one the solitary form cannot perform; it is direct evidence for the second-person side of the vault's tension. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Do you have a sense that I'm here or do I feel a bit at a distance?" (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "Like as if you feel me more; I feel more felt." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "That fear exists within a relational context." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) |
| Date | Described in Hübl and Lori Shridhare, 'The Tender Narrator Who Sees Beyond Time', Journal of Awareness-Based Systems Change 2(2), 30 November 2022 (a session recorded in April 2022) |
| In the vault | `notes/the solitary form stands against seven witnesses who hold a second person constitutive.md`; The note holds that seven traditions treat a second person as necessary, with Hübl unread; this move shows what his second person does, checking the relationship itself during the work. |
| Sources | https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168; https://thomashuebl.com/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *relational context* | the live relationship within which Hübl says a feeling such as fear is held |
| *feeling felt* | Hübl's report that he senses the participant sensing him, stated as 'I feel more felt' |

### Include it rather than overcome it

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hübl uses the move when a participant names a numbness, distance, or protection and tries to push past it. |
| What the coach does | When the participant said she felt distant and had 'developed a layer of protection' she was trying to overcome, Hübl asked that they not try to overcome it but include it: 'The distancing is part of your intelligence, it's not something that we want to get rid of.' She reported that her sense of herself grew into boundaries that had seemed far away, and he pointed out that including the distance 'immediately opened some of your energy'. He then had her include each new state in turn, first the distance and then the connection. |
| Steps | (1) The participant names a protective distance or numbness. (2) Hübl says: 'And I would like us not to try to overcome it, but to include it.' (3) Hübl names the protection as intelligent: 'The distancing is part of your intelligence'. (4) The participant feels the distance as part of her perception, without trying to remove it. (5) Hübl points to the change that follows and has her include the next state as well. |
| What it does | The move has the participant include a protective response in what they feel instead of trying to overcome it. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a protective response to relax on its own once it is not being fought; the participant does the including. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| The evidence | No controlled study of Hübl's process was found; the 2022 article describing it is a descriptive framework illustrated by one participant's account, and his site's claim to integrate 'evidence-based research' cites no study of the process itself. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: Hübl treats the participant's protective distance as intelligence to be included, not an obstacle to remove. Knowing that something happened: Hübl reads the participant's report that her energy opened as the sign that including worked. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move is close to the book's treatment of a guarded part as having a function, and differs only in being guided live. |
| Quoted from its sources | "And I would like us not to try to overcome it, but to include it." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "The distancing is part of your intelligence, it's not something that we want to get rid of." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "It's like I developed a layer of protection." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "it immediately opened some of your energy when you included it." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) |
| Date | Described in Hübl and Lori Shridhare, 'The Tender Narrator Who Sees Beyond Time', Journal of Awareness-Based Systems Change 2(2), 30 November 2022 (a session recorded in April 2022) |
| In the vault | `notes/what was never metabolized lives on as absence and hubl reached it through inherited trauma.md`; The note asks whether Hübl's absence is structurally the book's privation; this move shows him treating numbness and distance as protective intelligence to be included, which is close to the book's reading of contraction as having a function. |
| Sources | https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168; https://thomashuebl.com/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *include* | Hübl's word for letting a protective response be part of what is felt, instead of trying to get past it |
| *distancing* | the participant's protective sense of being apart, which Hübl calls part of her intelligence |

### Soften a bit into the fear

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hübl uses the move when a participant is in fear or anxiety during a share. |
| What the coach does | When the participant described fear and a pulsing in her solar plexus, Hübl asked her to make space for it and let it be, 'instead of it being a disturbance', and then proposed that she, he, and 'whoever is feeling you right now' soften a little into the fear together. He explained that people usually try to get away from fear, while the aim is to allow it so that 'your system can digest it rather than just get away from it or keep it in place.' He framed what came up in her as 'part of the issue, not something that is only separate from it.' |
| Steps | (1) Hübl invites the participant to make space for the anxiety and let it be, not treat it as a disturbance. (2) Hübl proposes that they soften into the fear together, with the witnessing group: 'that we can soften a bit into the fear.' (3) Hübl explains the aim: to allow the fear so that the system can digest it. (4) Hübl asks her to soften the way she feels the pulsing sensation as well. |
| What it does | The move has the participant, with the facilitator and group, soften their bracing so that the fear can be digested. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for fear to be taken in rather than escaped or held; the participant does the softening, with the facilitator and the group softening with her. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| The evidence | No controlled study of Hübl's process was found; the 2022 article describing it is a descriptive framework illustrated by one participant's account, and his site's claim to integrate 'evidence-based research' cites no study of the process itself. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: Hübl includes himself and the witnessing group in the softening, so the participant is not alone with the fear. Protection and defense: The move meets the bracing against fear with softening rather than effort. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Hübl frames the participant's fear as part of a collective issue, the war, rather than only her own, which goes beyond the book's focus on the individual reader's experience. |
| Quoted from its sources | "that we can soften a bit into the fear." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "to make some space and allow it to be, so that your system can digest it rather than just get away from it or keep it in place." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "That what comes up in us right now is part of the issue, not something that is only separate from it." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "and maybe we take a moment to just allow that to be, instead of it being a disturbance." (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) "that you and I and whoever is feeling you right now" (https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168) |
| Date | Described in Hübl and Lori Shridhare, 'The Tender Narrator Who Sees Beyond Time', Journal of Awareness-Based Systems Change 2(2), 30 November 2022 (a session recorded in April 2022) |
| In the vault | `notes/thomas hubl.md`; The vault's Hübl note is derived rather than sourced; this move comes from a published transcript of his facilitation. |
| Sources | https://jabsc.org/index.php/jabsc/article/download/4937/4198/14168; https://thomashuebl.com/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *soften* | to lessen one's bracing against a feeling, so that it can be taken in |
| *digest* | to take in and process an experience, as distinct from escaping it or holding it in place |

### Honoring numbness and the denial phase (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hübl uses the move at the start of collective trauma work, when a group or a person meets numbness or a wish to look away. |
| What the coach does | Hübl tells people that when they feel numb or that something is 'too much' and 'not digestible', they should honor that state as the intelligence that let people survive overwhelming events, while not treating it as the final truth. In his groups the first phase before trauma surfaces is a collective denial in which people feel they want to leave the room; he says that if this is held in presence, a further level opens and stored energy begins to arise. He treats the too-much feeling, held with compassion, as the beginning of feeling. |
| Steps | (1) The person or group notices numbness or the feeling that it is too much. (2) The facilitator names this as an intelligent survival function to be honored, not overridden. (3) The group holds the denial phase ('What am I doing here? Why am I doing this?') in presence rather than pushing past it. (4) Held with compassion, the too-much feeling turns into the beginning of feeling, and stored material begins to arise. |
| What it does | The move treats numbness and denial as survival intelligence to be honored and held until feeling can begin. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for numbness and denial to relax without being forced; the facilitator supplies acknowledgement and presence, and the participants' own feeling begins when it is ready. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| The evidence | No controlled study of Hübl's process was found; the 2022 article describing it is a descriptive framework illustrated by one participant's account, and his site's claim to integrate 'evidence-based research' cites no study of the process itself. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: Hübl names dissociation and numbness as survival intelligence and honors them before anything deeper is approached. Timing and pacing: The move waits through the denial phase rather than pushing a group past it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book reads a contraction as an absence with a function; Hübl's honoring of numbness is close to that reading, and his framing of it as collective rather than personal is where he parts from the book. |
| Quoted from its sources | "And so, whenever we are numb, whenever we feel, "It's too much, it's overwhelming, I can't do it. This is not digestible," that we honor that kind of inner state." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "there is this phase before the first wave arises, is this denial phase." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "And that's the collective denial, but if we hold that in presence, another level comes" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "If we can't be present with these emotions, our instinct is to disconnect from the pain, to go numb or shut down." (https://thomashuebl.com/grounding-practice-for-times-of-crisis/) "What am I doing here? Why am I doing this?" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) |
| Date | Stated in Hübl's interview with Tami Simon on Healing Collective Trauma (Sounds True, published 2 December 2020); the book appeared in 2020 |
| In the vault | `notes/what was never metabolized lives on as absence and hubl reached it through inherited trauma.md`; The note's claim that what was never metabolized lives on as absence finds its closest support in this move, where Hübl names numbness and denial as the first things met and honored. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/; https://thomashuebl.com/grounding-practice-for-times-of-crisis/; https://thomashuebl.com/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *denial phase* | the first stage in a group before trauma surfaces, in which people want to look away or leave |
| *dissociation* | the splitting off of experience that Hübl calls an important survival function |

### Sequencing collective trauma work by how close the violence is (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Hübl applies the sequence when deciding what kind of collective trauma work a region in or after conflict can take on. |
| What the coach does | Writing in December 2025 about Israel and Gaza, Hübl sets out an order for collective trauma work that depends on how close the violence is. While acute trauma is unfolding, the work is to change conditions on the ground to end the violence and to provide aid; once basic safety is restored, deeper trauma healing can address the symptoms of post-traumatic stress; this prepares a path for collective healing 'such as through restorative justice, reparations, and other reconciliation efforts'. A further level, group processes in which historical and ancestral trauma is allowed to emerge in a safe container, requires highly skilled facilitation and is, he writes, 'a new field of practice that is still in its early stages of development'. |
| Steps | (1) During acute violence: work to change conditions on the ground, end the violence, and provide aid and support. (2) After basic safety returns: do trauma healing work on individual and collective symptoms of post-traumatic stress. (3) Then: pursue collective healing through restorative justice, reparations, and reconciliation. (4) Where possible: hold group processes in which historical and ancestral trauma emerges and is witnessed and processed in a safe container, with highly skilled facilitation. |
| What it does | The sequence orders the work from ending violence and giving aid, through trauma healing and reparation, to group processes. |
| What is facilitated | The sequence makes it easier to match a community's need to the kind of work its situation allows; the communities, aid workers, and institutions do the work, and Hübl's role is training and support. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Hübl describes the sequence as a way of choosing what kind of work a situation allows; he says it is important not to 'impose a methodology' from outside. |
| The evidence | The sequence is Hübl's stated practice, with no evaluation cited; he describes the group level as still early in its development. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Hübl puts changing conditions, ending violence, restorative justice, and reparations ahead of or alongside inner healing, so his position includes structural remedies. Timing and pacing: The sequence makes the kind of work depend on how recent and how ongoing the violence is. Change at the scale of a team or organization: Hübl describes training health workers and working with diplomats and mediators to build 'collective healing architectures'. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book's material concerns one reader's inner change, while this sequence addresses whole societies and gives structural and legal remedies a place that inner work does not replace. |
| Quoted from its sources | "When acute traumas are unfolding, we work to change the conditions on the ground to end the violence, and we mitigate the suffering by providing aid and support." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "During times of stability and relative calm, when basic existential safety has been restored, we can do deeper trauma healing work to address the individual and collective symptoms of PTSD." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "This prepares a pathway for collective healing, such as through restorative justice, reparations, and other reconciliation efforts." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "There is another level of collective trauma work that is possible when a group comes together to consciously allow layers of historical and ancestral traumas to emerge, and to be witnessed, held, and processed within a safe container." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "It is also a new field of practice that is still in its early stages of development." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "it has always felt important to not bring in assumptions from the outside nor impose a methodology, but rather to listen, to facilitate dialogue, and help build bridges that can lay the groundwork for healing." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "to support trauma-informed conflict resolution and the creation of collective healing architectures on a larger level." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) "Healing from collective trauma cannot happen for large groups and countries while violent conflict is actively unfolding and people are suffering." (https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/) |
| Date | Stated in Hübl's article on crisis, interdependence, and collective healing, December 2025 |
| In the vault | `notes/thomas hubl.md`; The vault's Hübl note asks how his account relates to the position the corpus calls the material voice, that 'the cure for a structure is structural'. In this 2025 article he puts ending violence, aid, restorative justice, and reparations ahead of or beside healing work, so his position includes structural remedies rather than opposing them; this is the researcher's reading of one article. |
| Sources | https://thomashuebl.com/crisis-interdependence-and-collective-healing/; https://thomashuebl.com/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *acute trauma* | violence or disaster still unfolding, during which Hübl says healing work on collective trauma cannot happen |
| *healing architecture* | Hübl's phrase for large-scale structures that support collective healing and trauma-informed conflict resolution |

### The facilitator's own integration work (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hübl states the requirement when training facilitators and describing what his work asks of those who lead it. |
| What the coach does | Hübl holds that a facilitator of collective trauma work must do their own integration work for life, because without it they 'cannot discern my own trauma and the collective trauma', and he warns against the trap of bypassing one's own pain to deal with the collective's. He also requires a deep spiritual practice so that the facilitator stays connected to guidance beyond their person while entering large collective fields; his book, as quoted by his interviewer, says facilitators must 'access and channel light'. He says that expecting this personal work to end means not wanting to do it. |
| Steps | (1) The facilitator commits to their own integration work for the rest of their life. (2) The facilitator learns to tell their own trauma apart from the collective trauma in the room. (3) The facilitator avoids bypassing their own pain in order to work with a group's. (4) The facilitator keeps a contemplative practice that lets them stay connected while holding large collective material. |
| What it does | The move has the facilitator keep doing their own trauma integration so they can tell their own reactions from the group's. |
| What is facilitated | The practice keeps the facilitator able to tell their own reactions from the group's; the facilitator does it for themselves. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The move concerns the facilitator's own practice, whose content the facilitator decides. |
| The evidence | No controlled study of Hübl's process was found; the 2022 article describing it is a descriptive framework illustrated by one participant's account, and his site's claim to integrate 'evidence-based research' cites no study of the process itself. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | What care costs the one who gives it: Hübl makes the facilitator's lifelong personal work the price of holding others' collective pain, and warns that without it the facilitator becomes identified with the forces in the field. Knowing that something happened: The facilitator's discrimination between their own trauma and the collective trauma is the check Hübl relies on. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Hübl's requirement rests partly on a mystical claim, that the facilitator channels light, which the book does not make; the demand that a practitioner know their own history is also made in social work and family therapy, which the survey records as 'use of self'. |
| Quoted from its sources | "I need my personal integration, otherwise I cannot discern my own trauma and the collective trauma. And many people too early bypass their own pain, and then they deal with the pain of the collective, that's also a trap." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "And so, when I'm willing to do as a facilitator, deeply my own integration work, and I'm committed to that for the rest of my life." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "Facilitators need to be able to steadily and consistently access and channel light for the benefit of collective trauma integration work." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) "And if I lose that connection, then I start to become identified with the forces of that collective field, so I cannot hold a bigger perspective." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/) |
| Date | Stated in Hübl's interview with Tami Simon on Healing Collective Trauma (Sounds True, published 2 December 2020); the book appeared in 2020 |
| In the vault | `archive/research — counterpoint round background.md`; The research record found that Hübl's independence certification fails because his formation includes Wilber; his own about page confirms Wilber as an influence from age twenty, and the mystical framing of the facilitator here is one of the ways that formation shows. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/thomas-hubl-healing-collective-trauma/; https://thomashuebl.com/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *bypass* | to deal with others' or the collective's pain in order to avoid one's own |
| *channel light* | Hübl's phrase, from his book, for a facilitator staying connected to a source of guidance beyond their personal identity |

## Joe Hudson

| Move | Type | When the coach uses it | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| The Golden Algorithm | a theory | Hudson states this account when teaching on decisions, emotions, or recurring problems. | The account tells a person that a recurring problem contains an emotion they are avoiding, which becomes what they work with. |
| Finding the avoided emotion in a problem (the Golden Algorithm exercise) | a technique | Hudson uses the exercise when a person brings a recurring problem, and in his decisions course. | The exercise has the person find the emotion they are avoiding in a problem and see how avoiding it recreates it. |
| Moving anger cleanly, not at anybody | a technique | Hudson teaches the practice for anger that has been held back, and adapts it for people who collapse into shame, turn anger on themselves, or lose control. | The practice has the person let anger move through body and voice in a safe place, directed at no one, until it leaves clarity. |
| What would the answer get you? | a move | Hudson uses the move at the start of a coaching session, when a person has brought a question or problem. | The move has the person find the want beneath the question they brought. |
| Reading gaze, posture, and hedging as cues of shame | a move | Hudson uses the move throughout a live coaching session, from the first exchange. | The move has the coach read the person's gaze, posture, and hedging to judge how present they are and what to ask next. |
| Show me what it would be like to welcome yourself right now | a move | Hudson uses the move once a person has named that what they want is to love or welcome themselves. | The move has the person welcome themselves in the session and find and welcome whatever stops them. |
| If you can't love the thing, love the resistance to the thing | a move | Hudson uses the move when a person cannot welcome a feeling or a part of themselves. | The move has the person welcome their resistance when they cannot welcome the thing itself. |
| You know this place | a move | Hudson uses the move when a person reaches a state of open self-welcome during a session. | The move tells the person that the state they have reached is one they already know. |
| Naming the pattern, the story, and the need | a move | Hudson uses the move after a person has moved in and out of a welcoming state several times in a session. | The move names the person's repeating sequence and asks what story and need keep it going. |
| Welcoming oneself while being seen by the group | a move | Hudson uses the move at the end of a public coaching session, once the person has reached a welcoming state. | The move has the person hold self-welcome while being seen by everyone on the call. |

### The Golden Algorithm

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| When the coach uses it | Hudson states this account when teaching on decisions, emotions, or recurring problems. |
| What the coach does | Hudson holds that 'whatever we are avoiding emotionally, we are inviting into our lives in the exact way that we are avoiding it', so that every recurring problem contains an emotion someone is trying not to feel, and the strategy of avoiding it recreates it. He adds that the emotion itself is not what hurts; the resistance to it is. He describes a layering of emotions: shame, judgment, and guilt are 'blocker' emotions that stall the others; beneath them are sadness, anger, and helplessness; and when those are felt, the positive emotions follow, which can themselves be frightening. He sums this up as 'joy is the matriarch of a family of emotions, and she won't come into a house where her children aren't welcome.' |
| What it does | The account tells a person that a recurring problem contains an emotion they are avoiding, which becomes what they work with. |
| What is facilitated | On Hudson's account the person's own welcoming of an avoided emotion lets it move and stops the pattern that recreates it; the coach helps the person find the emotion and does not supply the feeling. |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| The evidence | No published study of Hudson's methods was found; the support is his own account and recorded coaching sessions published with transcripts on the Art of Accomplishment site. Hudson himself invites counter-examples: 'If somebody in the audience can find one where this isn't the case ... then please let us know.' |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Hudson says the avoided emotion did not cause a tree to fall on a car and that the tool 'isn't meant to assign blame', while holding that how a person recovers depends on what they allow themselves to feel. Protection and defense: The account treats avoidance, a protective strategy, as the mechanism that keeps a problem recurring, and treats self-blame as one more avoidance. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Hudson says every recurring problem contains an avoided emotion and asks listeners to find an exception, which is a stronger claim than the book makes; the book treats an absence where something should be, rather than an avoided feeling, as the shape of a difficulty. |
| Limits and critiques | Hudson warns that the idea can be turned into self-blame ('To beat yourself up over this is just another way not to feel it') and that finding the avoided emotion without learning to feel it is 'only half of a tool'. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The most basic way to say it is whatever we are avoiding emotionally, we are inviting into our lives in the exact way that we are avoiding it." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "The cause of the pain is in the avoidance of the negative emotions." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "The resistance is uncomfortable. What I mean to say is the emotions themselves are not the painful piece. It is the resistance to them." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "I would call these shame, judgment and guilt. What they do is stagnate the emotional experience." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "joy is the matriarch of a family of emotions, and she won't come into a house where her children aren't welcome." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "What I am talking about here are the recurring problems in our lives, specifically." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "We are never fully a part of any of the dynamics that create what we call problems in our lives." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "If somebody in the audience can find one where this isn't the case and they can't find an emotion they are avoiding inside of a problem they are having, then please let us know" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "To beat yourself up over this is just another way not to feel it." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "the emotion itself isn't what's painful, it's your resistance to it that makes it painful" (notes/joe hudson.md) |
| Date | Stated in the Art of Accomplishment podcast episode 'The Golden Algorithm — Decisions Series #3', published 10 February 2023; the underlying claim that resistance, not the emotion, is painful is in his Substack essay 'How to actually feel your emotions' |
| In the vault | `notes/joe hudson.md`; The vault's reading (2026-09-02) established that the theory of resisted feeling is Hudson's own and that 'completes itself' is the corpus's phrase; this entry builds on it with the Golden Algorithm, which the vault does not record, and his layering of emotions. |
| Sources | https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3; https://feeds.simplecast.com/zksImfUP; notes/joe hudson.md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Golden Algorithm* | Hudson's name for the pattern in which the way a person avoids an emotion brings that emotion back to them |
| *blocker emotions* | shame, judgment, and guilt, which Hudson says stall the feeling of other emotions |
| *welcome* | Hudson's word for inviting an emotion warmly, which he distinguishes from merely accepting it |
| *resistance* | the effort not to feel an emotion, which Hudson says is the actual source of the pain |

### Finding the avoided emotion in a problem (the Golden Algorithm exercise)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Hudson uses the exercise when a person brings a recurring problem, and in his decisions course. |
| What the coach does | A person names a recurring problem, names one emotion that might be avoided in it, and names the strategy used to avoid that emotion; then they look at how the strategy brings the emotion back. Hudson demonstrates with a person who wants to exercise but does not: the avoided emotion is guilt, the strategy is going to the fridge instead, and eating creates more guilt. He runs the exercise several times in a row, notes that seeing all one's problems this way can make people feel bad, and says that is the motivation to change; the second half is learning to feel the emotion. |
| Steps | (1) The person names 'a problem you are very familiar with', their own or someone else's. (2) The coach asks 'what's one, just one, of the emotions that might be being avoided there?' (3) The coach asks for the avoiding strategy, for example 'What would be a strategy that a person uses to avoid feeling that guilt?'
