(4) The person traces how the strategy produces more of the same emotion. (5) The person repeats the exercise with three or four problems. (6) The person looks for deeper emotions under the first; Hudson says that finding one is avoiding 'helplessness' works better than finding one is avoiding pain. (7) The person then learns to feel the emotion: 'until you can feel into them, welcome them, love them, and invite them in, nothing is going to change.' |
| What it does | The exercise has the person find the emotion they are avoiding in a problem and see how avoiding it recreates it. |
| What is facilitated | The exercise makes it easier for a person to see the emotional logic of a pattern they keep repeating; the person supplies the problem and the emotion, and the coach supplies only the questions. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| The evidence | No published study of Hudson's methods was found; the support is his own account and recorded coaching sessions published with transcripts on the Art of Accomplishment site. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Decisions: Hudson says that noticing the emotion one is running from 'makes our decisions cleaner', and presents the exercise as part of a course on decisions. Knowing that something happened: Hudson asks a person, after an emotional inquiry, whether sitting for ten minutes with something avoided for decades was hard, and uses their answer as evidence that welcoming works. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The exercise works by analysis of a problem into an emotion and a strategy, which the book's practice does not use; the book's reader is not asked to find the emotion behind every problem. |
| Limits and critiques | Hudson names three misuses: stopping at a shallow emotion, assuming there is only one, and using the discovery to blame oneself. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Name a problem you are very familiar with" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "what's one, just one, of the emotions that might be being avoided there?" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "What would be a strategy that a person uses to avoid feeling that guilt?" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "Seeing the feelings you avoid is one thing, but until you can feel into them, welcome them, love them, and invite them in, nothing is going to change." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "you just sat for 10 minutes with an experience that you have avoided for decades. How hard was it?" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "The tool isn't meant to assign blame. The tool isn't meant to be a way that you get to beat yourself up." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "It also makes our decisions cleaner." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) "If you ask what you think you are avoiding and it is pain, that's not going to work as well as understanding you are avoiding helplessness." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3) |
| Date | Stated in the Art of Accomplishment podcast episode 'The Golden Algorithm — Decisions Series #3', published 10 February 2023 |
| In the vault | `notes/joe hudson.md`; The vault's Hudson note does not record the Golden Algorithm or this exercise. |
| Sources | https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-golden-algorithm-decisions-series-3; https://feeds.simplecast.com/zksImfUP |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *avoidance strategy* | whatever a person does in order not to feel an emotion |
| *half of a tool* | Hudson's description of finding the avoided emotion without learning to feel it |

### Moving anger cleanly, not at anybody

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Hudson teaches the practice for anger that has been held back, and adapts it for people who collapse into shame, turn anger on themselves, or lose control. |
| What the coach does | A person finds a safe place and lets anger move through the body and voice: yelling into a pillow, playing music so no one hears, shouting in a car or in nature, or ranting, with breath used to build the anger up. The rules are not to hurt oneself or others, not to break things, and never to direct the anger at a person. Hudson adapts the method to the person: someone who gets dysregulated plays an actor performing their anger, while someone who freezes may need to believe the story a little until the anger can move. The sign that it worked is clarity and determination at the end; without them, Hudson says, the person has lost themselves in the story and is 'retraumatizing' themselves. |
| Steps | (1) The person finds a safe place: 'Yelling in a pillow, putting music on so nobody can hear you, going to the beach, doing it in the car, doing it in nature.' (2) The person tells their body it is safe, for example that no one is being hurt and no one can hear. (3) The person uses body, voice, sounds, and breath to let the anger build and move. (4) The person answers the inner voice that says not to be angry: 'I've listened to you for a decade. Now I'm just going to listen to anger for 20 minutes.' (5) If the person tends to get dysregulated, they act as 'an actor playing the role of you getting angry'; if they tend to freeze, they let themselves believe the story a bit until the anger moves. (6) The person keeps the rules: 'Don't hurt yourself. Don't hurt others. Don't do it at anybody. Don't break shit.' (7) The person checks the end state for 'clarity and determination' about themselves, and treats its absence as a sign of being lost in the story. (8) Before moving anger with anyone else, the person does it alone first; with a partner, they 'make sure you can stop it at any time.' |
| What it does | The practice has the person let anger move through body and voice in a safe place, directed at no one, until it leaves clarity. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it easier for held-back anger to be felt and finished without harming anyone; the person does the moving, and the coach supplies the rules and the adaptations. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. Hudson gives the rules and options, and the person does the moving alone or with one chosen partner. |
| The evidence | No study of Hudson's protocol was found. A 2024 meta-analysis of 154 studies found that anger-management activities that raise arousal, such as hitting a bag, were 'ineffective overall' at reducing anger and aggression, while arousal-lowering activities reduced both; it did not test a protocol like Hudson's, whose stated aim is clarity rather than less anger. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: Hudson names the conditions under which the practice harms: directing anger at a person, losing contact with the body, and moving anger with someone who has anger trauma and cannot stop it. Knowing that something happened: Hudson gives a test of completion, clarity and determination about oneself, and treats clarity about another person ('I am clear, fuck them') as the counterfeit. The container that holds people while they change: The container is a safe place, the rule of not at anybody, and a partner who can stop at any time. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Hudson's expression phase is obligatory and physical, while Singer tells people neither to suppress nor to express a feeling and the book attends to a feeling without requiring it to be acted out. |
| Limits and critiques | The 2024 meta-analysis cited above found arousal-increasing anger activities ineffective overall, which is the documented caution against physical venting; Hudson himself warns that anger can become 'the default thing' used 'to cover the underlying hurt or fear'. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Finding a safe place is important. Yelling in a pillow, putting music on so nobody can hear you, going to the beach, doing it in the car, doing it in nature." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "It's best if you are releasing anger with your physical body and your voice and your sounds." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "Literally telling your body it's safe." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "My favorite thing to say is, I've listened to you for a decade. Now I'm just going to listen to anger for 20 minutes." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "When I see this, the exercise I give people is, be an actor playing the role of you getting angry" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "For somebody who goes into freeze or flight, being in the observer position is going to completely shut off anger." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "Don't hurt yourself. Don't hurt others. Don't do it at anybody. Don't break shit." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "At the end of an anger movement, you are going to get clarity and determination." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "If you don't get there, it means anger isn't the proper emotion or you got completely lost in the story. You are just basically retraumatizing yourself at that point." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "where you have left your body, where you don't feel like you are in your body anymore" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "I suggest that everybody moves it in themselves first, by themselves." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "we will move anger with other people, which makes moving anger a lot easier, but there's a lot of pitfalls there." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "Do it with one person and make sure you can stop it at any time. You do not want to recreate trauma for anybody." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "You can use it as a way to cover the underlying hurt or fear." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-ii-emotion-series-4-3) "That's what anger looks like when it is not at somebody." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/the-wisdom-of-anger-part-i-emotion-series-3-3) "I am clear, fuck them. There is no clarity in that somatic experience." | Date | Stated in the Art of Accomplishment podcast episode 'The Wisdom of Anger: Part II — Emotion Series #4', published 29 October 2021; the companion Part I was published 22 October 2021 |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *moving anger* | Hudson's phrase for letting anger be felt and expressed through the body until it has passed |
| *not at anybody* | the rule that expression is never directed at another person |
| *clarity and determination* | the state Hudson says marks a completed anger movement, clarity about oneself rather than about another |
| *dysregulated* | Hudson's word for losing contact with the body while angry, for example watching oneself from above |

### What would the answer get you? (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hudson uses the move at the start of a coaching session, when a person has brought a question or problem. |
| What the coach does | When a person opens with a question, Hudson asks what answering it would do for them, and repeats until the underlying want appears. In the recorded session a man asked why he dismisses his accomplishments and needs; Hudson asked 'How is the answer to that question going to help you?' and then 'The strategy of getting that question answered gets you what?', and the man arrived at wanting to love himself. Brett Kistler notes that Hudson offered nothing of his own: the man laid out the path himself in response to the prompts. |
| Steps | (1) The person asks a surface question, such as why they dismiss their accomplishments. (2) Hudson asks: 'How is the answer to that question going to help you?' (3) Hudson asks again: 'The strategy of getting that question answered gets you what?' (4) The person names a deeper want, here to love himself, often with hedging words. (5) Hudson reflects the want back and asks what it would look like. |
| What it does | The move has the person find the want beneath the question they brought. |
| What is facilitated | The question makes it easier for a person to reach what they actually want beneath the problem they brought; the person supplies each answer, and the co-host observes that the man laid out the path 'without you offering anything'. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| The evidence | No published study of Hudson's methods was found; the support is his own account and recorded coaching sessions published with transcripts on the Art of Accomplishment site. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The move leaves all content with the person; the coach supplies only the repeated question. Remaining the author of one's own change: In the recorded session the person, not the coach, names the path from accomplishments to self-love. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move steers toward a single underlying want by repeated questioning in a live session, while the book offers its reader no live questioner. |
| Quoted from its sources | "How is the answer to that question going to help you?" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "The strategy of getting that question answered gets you what?" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "without you offering anything but him just responding to those reflections, to those prompts, he's now laid out the path" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) |
| Date | Recorded in the Art of Accomplishment episode 'How Do I Love Myself? (Coaching Session Breakdown)', published 6 June 2025 |
| In the vault | `notes/joe hudson.md`; The vault's Hudson note does not record his coaching questions; this move comes from a published coaching session and its breakdown. |
| Sources | https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself; https://feeds.simplecast.com/zksImfUP |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *strategy* | Hudson's word for the question a person brings, treated as a means to some deeper want |
| *hedging* | softening words such as 'maybe' that Hudson notes when a person first names what they want |

### Reading gaze, posture, and hedging as cues of shame (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hudson uses the move throughout a live coaching session, from the first exchange. |
| What the coach does | During a session Hudson watches where a person looks, how their head moves, and how they word things, and reads these as cues about shame in being seen. In the recorded breakdown he points out that the man looked down and to the right three or four times and tilted his head straight down when asked what the answer would get him, and that his speech was full of hedges such as 'maybe' and 'kinda'. He tracks the same cues to gauge progress: 'less hedging', one look down instead of four, and a reddening face as the man welcomed himself. |
| Steps | (1) Hudson watches the person's gaze and head movement from the first moment of the session. (2) Hudson notes repeated looking away as a cue that the person has 'a hard time seeing me' and seeing himself. (3) Hudson listens for hedging words that soften every want the person states. (4) Hudson uses changes in these cues, such as less hedging, to judge whether the person is becoming more present. (5) Hudson suggests to people in this pattern that 'not hedging is a very useful set of experiments to do.' |
| What it does | The move has the coach read the person's gaze, posture, and hedging to judge how present they are and what to ask next. |
| What is facilitated | The move lets the coach follow, moment by moment, how much of the person is present; it supplies the coach's timing and the person supplies every cue. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Hudson reads the cues silently and uses them to choose his next prompt; he names a pattern aloud only later, as an observation. |
| The evidence | No published study of Hudson's methods was found; the support is his own account and recorded coaching sessions published with transcripts on the Art of Accomplishment site. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: Hudson treats shame about being seen, and about wanting to be seen, as the difficulty the cues reveal. Knowing that something happened: Hudson uses observable changes, such as fewer hedges and looks away, as signs that the person has changed within the session. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move depends on a coach watching a person's face and posture, which a book read alone cannot do. |
| Limits and critiques | Reading gaze and head position as signs of shame is an interpretation by the coach, and the session records no check of it with the person. |
| Quoted from its sources | "He's looking down and to the right. So he is having a hard time seeing me." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "you'll notice a lot of hedging in their words, and if you're one of those people to play with, not hedging is a very useful set of experiments to do." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "And it's not no hedging, it's less hedging." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "the redder your face is getting" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) |
| Date | Recorded in the Art of Accomplishment episode 'How Do I Love Myself? (Coaching Session Breakdown)', published 6 June 2025 |
| In the vault | `notes/joe hudson.md`; The vault's Hudson note does not record his in-session observation; this entry comes from the published breakdown of a coaching session. |
| Sources | https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself; https://feeds.simplecast.com/zksImfUP |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *hedging* | softening or qualifying what one wants or feels, which Hudson reads as a sign of shame about being seen |
| *two stages of shame about being seen* | Hudson's distinction between shame at not being good enough to be seen and a deeper shame at wanting to be seen |

### Show me what it would be like to welcome yourself right now

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hudson uses the move once a person has named that what they want is to love or welcome themselves. |
| What the coach does | Instead of explaining self-love, Hudson asks the person to do it in the session: 'Show me what it would be like to welcome yourself right now.' When the person does it briefly and then stops, Hudson has him go back and notice what makes him stop, then welcome that too, naming each thing that comes up and saying how he is welcoming it. Hudson describes welcoming as a somatic feeling, like what one feels toward a pet one adores or a child, directed at oneself, not as words. |
| Steps | (1) Hudson asks: 'Show me what it would be like to welcome yourself right now.' (2) Hudson notes that the person did it for a moment and asks what happened. (3) Hudson asks the person to go back to welcoming and 'notice what makes you stop.' (4) Hudson has the person welcome whatever arises, including the trying and the resistance to being open. (5) Hudson asks the person to 'name the things that come up and then tell me how you're welcoming them.' (6) Hudson points out that welcoming is felt in the body, not said in words. |
| What it does | The move has the person welcome themselves in the session and find and welcome whatever stops them. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a person to feel self-welcome directly instead of thinking about it, and to find what interrupts it; the person does the welcoming. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| The evidence | No published study of Hudson's methods was found; the support is his own account and recorded coaching sessions published with transcripts on the Art of Accomplishment site. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Participation rather than watching: Hudson has the person carry out the welcome in the session rather than discuss it. Knowing that something happened: Hudson and his co-host read the change in the person's body, such as posture opening and the face reddening, as evidence the welcome occurred. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move asks for a performed welcome under a coach's eye, while the book's reader welcomes alone and unobserved. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Show me what it would be like to welcome yourself right now." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "Go back to welcoming yourself and notice what makes you stop." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "And then just name the things that come up and then tell me how you're welcoming them." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "It is a somatic feeling, the way that you feel when you're with a pet that you adore or a child" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) |
| Date | Recorded in the Art of Accomplishment episode 'How Do I Love Myself? (Coaching Session Breakdown)', published 6 June 2025 |
| In the vault | `notes/joe hudson.md`; The vault's Hudson note records that his core welcome is solitary meditation; this entry records the same welcome enacted in a coaching session. |
| Sources | https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself; https://feeds.simplecast.com/zksImfUP |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *welcoming yourself* | directing toward oneself the warm feeling one has toward a loved pet or child, as a bodily sense |
| *the trying* | the effortful attempt to welcome oneself, which Hudson has the person welcome as well |

### If you can't love the thing, love the resistance to the thing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hudson uses the move when a person cannot welcome a feeling or a part of themselves. |
| What the coach does | When a person cannot welcome a feeling or a part of themselves, Hudson moves the welcome one step back: they welcome their resistance to it instead. In the recorded session he gives the man this pointer when the man says he is trying hard, and has him welcome the trying and the resistance to being open. In a 2021 episode Hudson calls this and similar pointers 'hacks' and says the one most often missed is to ask what feeling one is resisting and then go and feel it. |
| Steps | (1) The person tries to welcome a feeling and finds they cannot. (2) Hudson says: 'if you can't love the thing, love the resistance to the thing.' (3) The person welcomes the resistance, including the trying. (4) The person may ask what wisdom the resistance holds. (5) The person asks what feeling they are resisting and goes and feels it. |
| What it does | The move has the person welcome their resistance when they cannot welcome the thing itself. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a person to stop fighting when they cannot yet welcome something; the person does the welcoming. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| The evidence | No published study of Hudson's methods was found; the support is his own account and recorded coaching sessions published with transcripts on the Art of Accomplishment site. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The move meets the protective part, the resistance, with welcome instead of trying to get past it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book's practice also refuses to fight a guarded part; the move differs in being a coaching instruction given at the moment of resistance rather than a stance the reader arrives at. |
| Quoted from its sources | "if you can't love the thing, love the resistance to the thing." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "If you can't love the thing, love your resistance to the thing." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/if-you-cant-love-the-feeling-love-the-resistance-3) "It's really how to be with the resistance without resisting that, and then whatever movement happens." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/if-you-cant-love-the-feeling-love-the-resistance-3) "What's the thing I don't want to feel?" And go and feel it." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/if-you-cant-love-the-feeling-love-the-resistance-3) |
| Date | Stated in the Art of Accomplishment episode 'If You Can't Love the Feeling, Love the Resistance', published 20 January 2021, and used in the coaching session published 6 June 2025 |
| In the vault | `notes/joe hudson.md`; The vault's Hudson note records his refusal of acceptance in favor of welcome; this move extends the welcome to the resistance itself, which the vault does not record. |
| Sources | https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself; https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/if-you-cant-love-the-feeling-love-the-resistance-3; https://feeds.simplecast.com/zksImfUP |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *resistance* | the pushing-away of a feeling, which Hudson treats as itself something to welcome |
| *pointer* | Hudson's word for a short instruction that directs a person toward an experience, such as loving the resistance |

### You know this place

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hudson uses the move when a person reaches a state of open self-welcome during a session. |
| What the coach does | When the man in the recorded session opened his arms and settled into welcoming himself, Hudson told him: 'You know this place, man. This is not your first time here.' The co-host reports that tears of recognition came to the man's eyes. Hudson says he cannot explain how he saw it and guessed from the care in the man's home that he reaches this state in his work; he then pointed out the pattern that follows, a learned shame after reaching that state, and had the man welcome the shame as well. |
| Steps | (1) The person reaches a state of open welcome, visible in posture. (2) Hudson tells them: 'You know this place, man. This is not your first time here.' (3) The person responds, in this case with tears of recognition. (4) Hudson names the pattern that follows: after being in that place, the person has learned to feel shame. (5) Hudson has the person return to welcoming and welcome the shame when it arises. |
| What it does | The move tells the person that the state they have reached is one they already know. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a person to recognize a state as familiar and their own rather than new or given by the coach; the coach supplies only the observation. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. Hudson offers his perception that the person has been in this state before; the person's response confirms or rejects it. |
| The evidence | No published study of Hudson's methods was found; the support is his own account and recorded coaching sessions published with transcripts on the Art of Accomplishment site. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The move tells a person that the state they reached is one they already know, and the recorded response was tears of recognition, which is the book's own promised event occurring in a coaching session. Being seen and recognized by another: The recognition is set off by the coach seeing and naming what the person already had. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | In Hudson's session the recognition is set off by a coach who says what he sees, while the book aims to occasion recognition with no one present; the move shows the event depending, in this instance, on a second person. |
| Limits and critiques | Hudson says he cannot explain how he saw it, so the move rests on the coach's intuition, which the session does not check except by the person's response. |
| Quoted from its sources | "You know this place, man. This is not your first time here." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "tears came to his eyes, like tears of recognition" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "I just saw that he'd been there before. I can't explain how I saw that." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "The pattern is that, you know, that place. After you are in that place, somehow you have a learning that you need to feel shame after that place." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "We have this sense in us, that we know the way home." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) |
| Date | Recorded in the Art of Accomplishment episode 'How Do I Love Myself? (Coaching Session Breakdown)', published 6 June 2025 |
| In the vault | `notes/the solitary form stands against seven witnesses who hold a second person constitutive.md`; The note sets the book's solitary form against the seven thinkers the vault holds as requiring a second person for the inner life; Hudson's move is a documented case of recognition occasioned by a second person, while the vault's reading record finds his core welcome practice solitary, so he stands on both sides. |
| Sources | https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself; https://feeds.simplecast.com/zksImfUP |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *that place* | the state of open self-welcome the person reached, which Hudson says the person already knows |
| *tears of recognition* | the co-host's description of the client's response when told he already knew the state |

### Naming the pattern, the story, and the need (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hudson uses the move after a person has moved in and out of a welcoming state several times in a session. |
| What the coach does | After the person has moved in and out of welcoming, Hudson names the pattern he sees: the person reaches the state and then shames or criticizes himself out of it, in work as in the session. He then asks the person for the story they tell themselves about why the self-criticism is needed, and asks which need would not be met if they were welcoming. In the recorded session the man answered that he is 'not allowed to have it' and that the need was to do better, and Hudson reframed the comparative habit as having 'a brilliance in it' that can be welcomed too. |
| Steps | (1) Hudson says he wants to point out a pattern and names it: reaching the state, then shame pulling the person out. (2) Hudson links the pattern to the person's life, for example their work. (3) Hudson asks: 'What's the story you tell yourself?' (4) Hudson asks: 'So what's the need that wouldn't get met if you were welcoming?' (5) Hudson has the person welcome the part that criticizes, rather than fight it. |
| What it does | The move names the person's repeating sequence and asks what story and need keep it going. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a person to see a repeating sequence and the reason they keep it; the coach supplies the observation and the person supplies the story and the need. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. Hudson offers the pattern as his observation and then asks the person for their own story and need. |
| The evidence | No published study of Hudson's methods was found; the support is his own account and recorded coaching sessions published with transcripts on the Art of Accomplishment site. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The move asks what need the self-critical habit serves, treating the defense as having a reason. Being seen and recognized by another: Hudson ties the pattern to a childhood in which, he suggests, a parent could not bear the child's joy. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Hudson interprets the pattern's origin in the person's childhood, an interpretation the book leaves to the reader. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The pattern is that, you know, that place. After you are in that place, somehow you have a learning that you need to feel shame after that place." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "What's the story you tell yourself?" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "So what's the need that wouldn't get met if you were welcoming?" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "And so there's this natural in and out from the welcoming to the shame that happens and it's part of the process and it's to be felt and invited. It's not to be resisted." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "Just that it's, I'm not allowed to have it." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "I welcome my comparative mindset. It helps me be good in work. There's a brilliance in it." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "there's some way in which you do that false humility thing." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "It's a defensive thing." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "And typically it's because there was a parent where your joy was overwhelming to their senses." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) |
| Date | Recorded in the Art of Accomplishment episode 'How Do I Love Myself? (Coaching Session Breakdown)', published 6 June 2025 |
| In the vault | `notes/joe hudson.md`; The vault's Hudson note does not record his use of pattern-naming or his question about the need a habit serves. |
| Sources | https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself; https://feeds.simplecast.com/zksImfUP |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *pattern* | a sequence the person repeats, here welcoming followed by shame |
| *false humility* | Hudson's name for the man's way of shrinking himself in front of others, which the man himself then called 'a defensive thing' |

### Welcoming oneself while being seen by the group (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Hudson uses the move at the end of a public coaching session, once the person has reached a welcoming state. |
| What the coach does | Hudson's public coaching takes place on group calls, and he uses the audience as part of the work. In the recorded session, after the man had welcomed himself, Hudson asked him to stay with that welcoming 'with everybody here on the call' and to look at them, and a speaker in the session pointed out that the man was being seen and welcomed by all those people as he welcomed himself. The Art of Accomplishment's page for a 2026 session reports Hudson saying that what got hurt in groups is often best healed in groups. |
| Steps | (1) The person does their work in front of a group on a public call. (2) After the person reaches the welcoming state, Hudson asks them to stay with it: 'So just be with everybody here on the call with that welcoming, that deep welcoming.' (3) Hudson asks the person to look at the group with eyes open. (4) The person experiences being seen and welcomed by the group while welcoming themselves. |
| What it does | The move has the person hold self-welcome while being seen by everyone on the call. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a person whose shame is about being seen to hold self-welcome while actually being seen; the group supplies witnesses, and the person supplies the welcome. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| The evidence | No published study of Hudson's methods was found; the support is his own account and recorded coaching sessions published with transcripts on the Art of Accomplishment site. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: The move adds many witnesses to the coach, so the person is seen by a group while welcoming themselves. Participation rather than watching: The group's watching is part of the person's work, so the audience takes part as witnesses. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move uses a group's gaze as part of the change, while the book is read alone; it is evidence for the second-person side of the vault's tension over the solitary form. |
| Quoted from its sources | "So just be with everybody here on the call with that welcoming, that deep welcoming." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "you're actually being seen and welcomed by all these people." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "Eyes open. Yeah. Look at 'em." (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself) "the stuff that got hurt in groups is often best healed in groups" (https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/how-a-perfectionist-learns-to-accept-herself---coaching-with-joe) |
| Date | Recorded in the Art of Accomplishment episode 'How Do I Love Myself? (Coaching Session Breakdown)', published 6 June 2025; the episode page of 19 August 2026 reports Hudson's view that 'the stuff that got hurt in groups is often best healed in groups' |
| In the vault | `notes/the solitary form stands against seven witnesses who hold a second person constitutive.md`; The note sets the book's solitary form against traditions holding a second person necessary; Hudson's group use is a further case on that side, while his solo welcome meditation stays on the other. |
| Sources | https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/coaching-breakdown-how-do-i-love-myself; https://www.artofaccomplishment.com/podcast/how-a-perfectionist-learns-to-accept-herself---coaching-with-joe; https://feeds.simplecast.com/zksImfUP |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *public Q&A* | the open group calls at which Hudson coaches volunteers in front of others |
| *being seen* | the experience that clients in this pattern feel shame about, which the group's attention addresses directly |

## Byron Katie

| Move | Type | When the coach uses it | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| What Is Is | a theory | Katie states the account at the start of her booklet and events, before a person fills in a worksheet. | The account tells the person that their stress comes from believing a particular thought, which makes that thought the object of the inquiry. |
| Judge-Your-Neighbor Worksheet | a technique | Katie uses the worksheet at the start of every piece of inquiry, once a person has a stressful situation in mind. | The worksheet turns a person's reaction to someone into six written sentences that can each be questioned. |
| The four questions | a technique | Katie uses the questions on each statement from a completed worksheet, one statement at a time. | The questions have the person check whether they can know a thought is true and what believing it does to them. |
| Question 3: How do you react, what happens, when you believe that thought? | a move | Katie uses the question after the first two questions, whatever their answers were. | The question has the person observe and list what happens in their feelings, body, and behavior while they believe the thought. |
| Question 4: Who would you be without the thought? | a move | Katie uses the question after the person has listed their reactions to believing the thought. | The question has the person imagine the same situation without the thought and notice the difference. |
| Turnarounds | a technique | Katie uses the turnarounds after the four questions, on the same statement. | The turnarounds have the person test the opposite forms of their judgment against specific examples from their own memory. |
| The turnaround for statement 6: 'I am willing to…' and 'I look forward to…' | a move | Katie uses the move only on the sixth worksheet statement, after that statement has been through the four questions. | The move turns a vow never to experience something again into willingness, and then eagerness, to meet it. |
| Whose business am I in? | a move | Katie offers the question for any moment of stress, outside a worksheet session. | The question has the person sort the thought causing stress into their own concern, another person's, or what no one controls. |
| Anchoring the inquiry in one specific moment | a move | Katie uses the move before the worksheet is written and again at each of the four questions. | The move keeps the person answering from inside one remembered moment rather than about a relationship in general. |
| Katie facilitating a volunteer in the chair | a move | Katie uses the move at her public events, workshops, and the School for The Work, with a volunteer who has a completed worksheet. | The move has Katie take one volunteer through their worksheet in public while an audience watches. |

### What Is Is

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| When the coach uses it | Katie states the account at the start of her booklet and events, before a person fills in a worksheet. |
| What the coach does | Katie holds that a person suffers only when they believe a thought that opposes what is actually happening, such as the thought that another person should behave differently. She states that a thought is harmless in itself and that the suffering comes from believing it without having questioned it. She dates this account to her own change in 1986, when she saw that she suffered when she believed her thoughts and did not suffer when she did not believe them. Her booklet names a second source of stress, living mentally in another person's concerns or in matters no one controls, which she calls being in someone else's business. |
| What it does | The account tells the person that their stress comes from believing a particular thought, which makes that thought the object of the inquiry. |
| What is facilitated | On Katie's account the person's own questioning ends the suffering, and her booklet's cover line says 'No one can give you freedom but you.' The facilitator supplies the questions and does not supply the answers or the conclusions. |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| The evidence | The account is Katie's report of her own experience rather than a tested hypothesis, and the studies of the method test the procedure rather than the account. A 2021 systematic review of the clinical version, inquiry-based stress reduction, found 17 studies and reported that only three were randomized trials. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Katie places the cause of stress in the person's belief, and when asked about war and child abuse she answers that she suffers only if she believes they should not exist, while denying that she condones them. Recognition: Katie describes the state without the questioned thought as the person's natural state, so her promised event resembles the book's claim that a person comes to know again what was already there. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Katie treats all stress as produced by the person's believing mind, while the book keeps open that some suffering is structural or externally caused, which is the corpus's own objection to the coaches' 'radical interiority of cause'. Her texts also treat the questioned thought as untrue ('forgiveness means discovering that what you thought happened didn't'), where the book reads a difficulty as a contraction, an absence where something should be, rather than as a mistaken belief. |
| Limits and critiques | Katie's answer to questions about war, rape, and child abuse is that she does not condone them but suffers only if she believes they should not exist; a participant quoted in the Los Angeles Times in 2002 objected to 'the passivity', and the method's use with an incest survivor led the California Board of Psychology to review a tape of her work in 1999 (the review was dropped). |
| Quoted from its sources | "The only time we suffer is when we believe a thought that argues with what is." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "All the stress that we feel is caused by arguing with what is." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "A thought is harmless unless we believe it. It is not our thoughts, but the attachment to our thoughts, that causes suffering." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "Reality is always kinder than the story we are believing about it." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "I discovered that when I believed my thoughts, I suffered, but that when I didn't believe them, I didn't suffer" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byron_Katie) "How could I condone them? I'm not crazy. I simply notice that if I believe they shouldn't exist when they do exist, I suffer." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "Forgiveness is realizing that what you thought happened didn't." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "No one can give you freedom but you." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "I have a hard time with the passivity--what comes to you, be happy about it" (https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html) |
| Date | 1986 (the change Katie dates to that year); stated in Loving What Is (2002) and the booklet The Work of Byron Katie: An Introduction (2019) |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The note quotes the corpus's compression of Katie's axiom, 'suffering is arguing with what is'; her own wordings are 'The only time we suffer is when we believe a thought that argues with what is' and 'All the stress that we feel is caused by arguing with what is'. The corpus's 'reality is kinder than your story of it' (arc-gloss) is close to her 'Reality is always kinder than the story we are believing about it.' The vault has no note on Katie; ops/ecologies/the diagnostics.md asks whether she treats a belief as false content or as a clench with a payoff, and her booklet supports the false-content reading. |
| Sources | https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byron_Katie; https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/; https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A33538339&format=json&resultType=core |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *what is* | whatever is actually happening, which Katie also calls reality |
| *attachment to a thought* | believing that a thought is true without having questioned it |
| *inquiry* | questioning a thought with the four questions and then turning it around |
| *three kinds of business* | Katie's division of all concerns into her own, another person's, and reality's, which she calls God's |

### Judge-Your-Neighbor Worksheet

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Katie uses the worksheet at the start of every piece of inquiry, once a person has a stressful situation in mind. |
| What the coach does | A person picks one stressful situation with another person, recalls one specific moment of it, and writes six short statements about that person on a printed form. The six prompts ask whom the person is upset with and why, how they want the other to change, what advice they would give, what they need the other to do for them to be happy, what they think of the other, and what they never want to experience again. Katie instructs the person to write as pettily as they felt at the time and to aim the judgments at someone else before ever writing about themselves. |
| Steps | (1) The person chooses a stressful situation with someone, for example an argument, and meditates on one specific time and place. (2) The person answers statement 1: 'In this situation, who angers, confuses, hurts, saddens, or disappoints you, and why?' (3) The person answers statements 2 to 5: how they want the other to change, what advice they would give, what they need the other to think, say, feel, or do for them to be happy, and a list of what they think of the other. (4) The person answers statement 6: 'What is it about this person and situation that you don't ever want to experience again?' (5) Throughout, the person writes short, simple sentences without censoring: 'Allow yourself to be as judgmental, childish, and petty as you were in that moment.' (6) The person points the judgments outward at another person, following Katie's instruction 'Please do not write about yourself yet.' (7) The person then takes each statement, one at a time, to the four questions and the turnarounds. |
| What it does | The worksheet turns a person's reaction to someone into six written sentences that can each be questioned. |
| What is facilitated | The worksheet makes it easier for a person to catch their own stressful thoughts in words they can then question; Katie's reason is that 'Once the mind is stopped on paper, it's much easier to investigate.' The form supplies only prompts, and the person supplies every judgment. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The printed worksheet does the work when a person fills it in alone; at Katie's events the same sheet is read aloud to her. |
| The evidence | The worksheet is the entry point of every study of The Work; studies of the clinical version (inquiry-based stress reduction) are mostly uncontrolled, and a 2021 systematic review counted three randomized trials among 17 studies. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: The form fixes the six prompts and the order of work, while every sentence on it is the person's own judgment. Protection and defense: Katie invites the person to write the petty and childish judgments they had in the moment rather than wiser ones, so the defensive thoughts are put on paper instead of being tidied. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The worksheet begins by directing blame at another person so that it can later be turned back on the self, while the book's forms of practice do not begin from a written judgment of a named other. Katie also treats the whole of what upsets a person as located in what they wrote, which the book does not assume. |
| Limits and critiques | The worksheet's example is an argument, but Katie's booklet asks readers to write about 'someone whom you haven't yet totally forgiven', which can include a person who abused them, and the worksheet gives no separate guidance for that case (see the limits of the live-session entry). |
| Quoted from its sources | "In this situation, who angers, confuses, hurts, saddens, or disappoints you, and why?" (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/3-Judge-Your-Neighbor-Worksheet-v20250115-for-website.pdf) "What is it about this person and situation that you don't ever want to experience again?" (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/3-Judge-Your-Neighbor-Worksheet-v20250115-for-website.pdf) "In order for you to be happy in this situation, what do you need him/her/them to think, say, feel, or do?" (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/3-Judge-Your-Neighbor-Worksheet-v20250115-for-website.pdf) "Write without censoring yourself. Allow yourself to be as judgmental, childish, and petty as you were in that moment." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Please do not write about yourself yet." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "Mind's job is to be right, and it can justify itself faster than the speed of light." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Once the mind is stopped on paper, it's much easier to investigate." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "I encourage you to write about someone whom you haven't yet totally forgiven, someone you still resent." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "Point The Work outward in the beginning, and you may come to see that everything outside you is a direct reflection of your own thinking." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) |
| Date | 1986 onward; the current English worksheet is revised 15 January 2025 |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault holds only the corpus's summary of Katie as 'four questions and a turnaround'; it has not recorded the worksheet, which comes before the questions in her own instructions. |
| Sources | https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/3-Judge-Your-Neighbor-Worksheet-v20250115-for-website.pdf; https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/; https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf; https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/07/2-One-Belief-At-A-Time-Worksheet-v20250115.pdf; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A33538339&format=json&resultType=core |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Judge-Your-Neighbor Worksheet* | the six-statement form on which a person writes judgments about someone in one stressful situation |
| *I Complain About Worksheet* | a preliminary list of complaints from which a person can choose a situation |
| *One-Belief-At-A-Time Worksheet* | a second form on which a single written statement is taken through the four questions and turnarounds |
| *point the finger outward* | Katie's instruction to write judgments about someone else first rather than about oneself |

### The four questions

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Katie uses the questions on each statement from a completed worksheet, one statement at a time. |
| What the coach does | A person takes one statement from the worksheet and asks four fixed questions of it in order: whether it is true, whether they can absolutely know it is true, how they react when they believe it, and who they would be without it. The first two questions allow only a one-syllable answer, yes or no, and an answer that begins with 'because' or 'but' is treated as a sign that the person has stopped inquiring. Katie calls the whole process a meditation in which the person asks, waits, and lets an answer arise rather than working it out. |
| Steps | (1) The person isolates one statement from the worksheet and repeats it. (2) The person asks question 1, 'Is it true? (Yes or no. If no, move to question 3.)', and waits in stillness for a yes or a no. (3) If the answer is yes, the person asks question 2, 'Can you absolutely know that it's true?', again answering yes or no. (4) The person checks the answer for defense: an answer containing 'because' or 'but' 'is not the one-syllable answer you are looking for, and you're no longer doing The Work.' (5) The person asks question 3, 'How do you react, what happens, when you believe that thought?', and lists the reactions (see the separate entry). (6) The person asks question 4, 'Who would you be without the thought?', and imagines the situation without it (see the separate entry). (7) The person turns the statement around and finds examples (see the separate entry). |
| What it does | The questions have the person check whether they can know a thought is true and what believing it does to them. |
| What is facilitated | The questions make it easier for a person to notice the difference between a thought and what they actually know; the person's own answer is the content, and Katie says the answer 'will meet your question' if the person waits. The facilitator supplies neither the answer nor any interpretation of it. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The questions are fixed and the person answers them from within; Katie tells readers they are listening for their own answers, 'not other people's, and not anything you have been taught.'. |
| The evidence | The Los Angeles Times reported in 2002 that Katie's questions then read 'Is it true or can I really know that it's true? How do I react when I think that thought? Can I find one peaceful reason to believe that thought? Who would I be without the thought?', so the sequence has been revised. A single-group pilot study of 197 people at the nine-day School for The Work (2015) reported improvements on depression, anxiety, and anger scales with no control group. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: Katie separates an answer that arises after waiting from an answer produced by argument, and treats 'because' or 'but' in the answer as the mark of the second kind. Timing and pacing: Katie instructs the person to ask, be still, and wait for an answer rather than to answer at once. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The questions test a thought against what the person can know, which treats the difficulty as a belief to be checked; the book treats deep change as the person recognizing what was already there, and it does not rely on a single fixed script. |
| Limits and critiques | In a live session the one-syllable rule lets the questioner treat an answer as defensive, which places the judgment of what counts as an honest answer with the facilitator; this is a reading of the rule, not a documented case. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Is it true? (Yes or no. If no, move to question 3.)" (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "Can you absolutely know that it's true?" (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "The answer to the first two questions is just one syllable: either yes or no" (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "this is not the one-syllable answer you are looking for, and you're no longer doing The Work. You're looking for freedom outside yourself." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "This Work is a meditation practice. It's like diving into yourself." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Drop into the depths of yourself, listen, and wait. The answer will meet your question." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "You are listening for your answers now, not other people's, and not anything you have been taught." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "Is it true or can I really know that it's true? How do I react when I think that thought? Can I find one peaceful reason to believe that thought? Who would I be without the thought?" (https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html) "This study was designed as a single-group pilot clinical trial (open label)." (https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A25497228&format=json&resultType=core) |
| Date | 1986 onward; worded as now in Loving What Is (2002); the Los Angeles Times recorded a different wording in November 2002 |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault holds the corpus's phrase 'four questions and a turnaround' and nothing of the questions' wording or rules. |
| Sources | https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/; https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf; https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A25497228&format=json&resultType=core; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A33538339&format=json&resultType=core |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *the four questions* | the fixed sequence of questions Katie applies to one written thought |
| *one-syllable answer* | Katie's rule that the first two questions are answered only with yes or no |
| *don't-know mind* | Katie's phrase for the openness the questions aim at, being willing not to know |

### Question 3: How do you react, what happens, when you believe that thought?

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Katie uses the question after the first two questions, whatever their answers were. |
| What the coach does | The person closes their eyes, returns to the moment in the situation, and watches what happens in them while they believe the thought. Katie's prompts direct attention to emotions, body sensations, images of past and future, how the person treats the other person and themselves, and any compulsions that appear, such as drinking, eating, or watching television. The person lists these effects as a record of what believing the thought does. |
| Steps | (1) The person closes their eyes and witnesses 'the feelings, body sensations, and behaviors that arise when you believe that thought.' (2) The person notes the images of past and future that appear and the sensations that come with them. (3) The person asks, 'Do any obsessions or addictions begin to appear when you believe that thought?' (4) The person notices how they treat the other person, and also 'how you treat yourself in this situation and how that feels.' (5) The person asks whether the thought brings peace or stress, and writes the list of effects. |
| What it does | The question has the person observe and list what happens in their feelings, body, and behavior while they believe the thought. |
| What is facilitated | The question makes it easier for a person to see for themselves what believing a thought costs them; Katie says that once a person sees this 'all your suffering begins to unravel.' The facilitator does not tell the person what the costs are. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| The evidence | The move has not been tested on its own; it is one step of the procedure the pilot and randomized studies of inquiry-based stress reduction evaluated as a whole. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: The person gathers bodily and behavioral evidence of what a belief does, rather than judging the belief by argument. Protection and defense: The question lists the defensive behaviors a belief sets off, including compulsions, and presents them to the person without blame. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move treats the reactions as effects of a thought, while the book treats bodily contraction as part of the difficulty itself rather than as a downstream effect of a belief. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Close your eyes and witness the feelings, body sensations, and behaviors that arise when you believe that thought." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Do any obsessions or addictions begin to appear when you believe that thought?" (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "With this question, we begin to notice internal cause and effect." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Also, witness how you treat yourself in this situation and how that feels." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "I love question 3. Once you answer it for yourself, once you see the cause and effect of believing a thought, all your suffering begins to unravel." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) |
| Date | 1986 onward (Loving What Is, 2002; instructions on thework.com) |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault holds no record of the question's prompts; they bear on the vault's somatic verification material because they have the person watch the body while holding a thought. |
| Sources | https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/; https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *internal cause and effect* | Katie's name for the link a person notices between believing a thought and their own reactions |
| *witness* | Katie's instruction to observe one's reactions while recalling the situation, without changing them |

### Question 4: Who would you be without the thought?

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Katie uses the question after the person has listed their reactions to believing the thought. |
| What the coach does | With eyes closed, the person pictures themselves in the same situation, in front of the same person doing the same thing, but without the thought they have been questioning. The person notices how they see the other person and how they feel. Katie's booklet then asks which the person prefers, the situation with the story or without it, and which feels kinder. |
| Steps | (1) The person closes their eyes and returns to the situation: 'Take a moment to reflect, observe, and experience the situation again, this time without the thought.' (2) The person pictures standing in front of the person they wrote about while that person does what they think they should not do. (3) The person notices what they see and feel without the thought, and how they would see the other person. (4) The person compares: 'Which do you prefer—with or without your story? Which feels kinder? Which feels more peaceful?' (5) The person accepts 'I don't know' as a possible answer, since Katie notes that many people cannot yet imagine life without their story. |
| What it does | The question has the person imagine the same situation without the thought and notice the difference. |
| What is facilitated | The question lets the person experience directly, in imagination, the difference the belief makes; the facilitator supplies the question and nothing of the answer. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| The evidence | The move has not been tested on its own. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: Katie describes what the person finds without the thought as their natural state, which resembles the book's event of coming to know what was already there. Remaining the author of one's own change: The person discovers the alternative themselves by imagining it, and the facilitator does not propose what it should be. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The question asks the person to subtract a thought and see who remains, which fits the book's reading of change as uncovering; it differs in that it is a fixed step in a script rather than something the reader arrives at in their own time. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Who would you be without the thought?" (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "Closing your eyes, return to the situation. Take a moment to reflect, observe, and experience the situation again, this time without the thought." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Which do you prefer—with or without your story? Which feels kinder? Which feels more peaceful?" (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "For many people, life without their story is literally unimaginable." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "So "I don't know" is a common answer to this question." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Visualize yourself standing in the presence of the person you have written about when he or she is doing what you think he or she shouldn't be doing." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "Yeah, absolutely. She's great." (https://www.onecommune.com/blog/the-mind-architect-with-peter-crone) "who could you be and what could become available for you in the absence of that constraint?" (https://miraclemorning.com/peter-crone/) |
| Date | 1986 onward (Loving What Is, 2002; worksheet revised 2025 as 'Who or what would you be without the thought?') |
| In the vault | `notes/reading is remembering.md`; The note records the book's promise that reading is remembering; Katie's question 4 is a practitioner's version of asking a person to notice who they are without an acquired belief, with no link to the book's sources. |
| Sources | https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/; https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf; https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/3-Judge-Your-Neighbor-Worksheet-v20250115-for-website.pdf; https://www.onecommune.com/blog/the-mind-architect-with-peter-crone; https://miraclemorning.com/peter-crone/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *story* | Katie's word for an unquestioned thought about what is happening |
| *without the thought* | the imagined state in which the person does not believe the questioned thought |

### Turnarounds

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Katie uses the turnarounds after the four questions, on the same statement. |
| What the coach does | The person rewrites the original statement in its opposite forms: turned to the self, turned to the other, and turned to the opposite, and for statements about the body, a disease, or a career, with 'my thinking' in place of the subject. For each version the person finds at least three specific, genuine examples from the same situation showing how it is as true as, or truer than, the original. Katie warns that turning thoughts around without first asking the questions is 'of little value' and that turnarounds which make no sense should not be forced. |
| Steps | (1) The person starts from the original statement, for example 'Paul doesn't listen to me.' (2) The person turns it to the self ('I don't listen to myself'), to the other ('I don't listen to Paul'), and to the opposite ('Paul does listen to me'), always starting again from the original. (3) For subjects such as the body or a career, the person may 'substitute the words "my thinking" for the subject.' (4) For each turnaround the person finds at least three specific, genuine examples from that situation. (5) The person skips turnarounds that make no sense: 'Don't force these.' (6) The person keeps in mind that 'This is not about blaming yourself or feeling guilty.' |
| What it does | The turnarounds have the person test the opposite forms of their judgment against specific examples from their own memory. |
| What is facilitated | The turnarounds make it easier for a person to find, in their own memory of the situation, evidence against the judgment they wrote; the person supplies every example. Katie presents this as ending the stance in which 'anyone or anything else is responsible for your suffering.' |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| The evidence | The turnarounds are part of the whole procedure studied as inquiry-based stress reduction; the evidence for the procedure is promising but mostly uncontrolled, with three randomized trials counted in a 2021 review. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Conflict: The turnaround to the other asks the person to find how they did to the other what they accuse the other of doing. Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Katie writes that as long as the cause of a problem is thought to be 'out there' the situation is hopeless, which moves the cause of the stress from the other person to the one who judges. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The turnarounds relocate responsibility from the other person to the self, while the book keeps open that some harm comes from others or from structures and does not ask a reader to find themselves in the wrong. |
| Limits and critiques | Applied to a statement about abuse, the rule of turning a statement to the self produces a sentence in which the person who was harmed names themselves as the one who did the harm; Katie's instruction that the turnaround is 'not about blaming yourself or feeling guilty' is her stated guard, and no study of this risk was found. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Often a statement can be turned around to the self, to the other, and to the opposite." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Find at least three specific, genuine examples of how each turnaround is true for you in that situation." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "This is not about blaming yourself or feeling guilty. It's about discovering alternatives that can bring you peace." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Some turnarounds may not make any sense to you. Don't force these." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "To simply turn thoughts around keeps the process intellectual and is of little value." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "substitute the words "my thinking" for the subject" (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "For each turnaround, go back and start with the original statement." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "As long as you think that the cause of your problem is "out there"—as long as you think that anyone or anything else is responsible for your suffering—the situation is hopeless." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "she has done The Work in corporations, universities, schools, churches, prisons, and hospitals." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) |
| Date | 1986 onward (Loving What Is, 2002; instructions on thework.com) |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The corpus calls Katie's four questions and turnaround 'śāktopāya running out of an American storefront' (walked p. 17; atlas §IX.5 has 'śāktopāya's American storefront'), a comparison to one of the means of liberation in Kashmir Shaiva teaching that Katie's own texts never make; the vault's notes do not repeat the line. |
| Sources | https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/; https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf; https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/3-Judge-Your-Neighbor-Worksheet-v20250115-for-website.pdf; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID%3A33538339&format=json&resultType=core |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *turnaround* | a restatement of the original thought in an opposite form, to be tested with examples |
| *turnaround to the self, to the other, to the opposite* | the three standard directions in which a statement is reversed |
| *my thinking* | the words substituted for a non-human subject, so that 'my body should be strong' becomes 'my thinking should be strong' |

### The turnaround for statement 6: 'I am willing to…' and 'I look forward to…'

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Katie uses the move only on the sixth worksheet statement, after that statement has been through the four questions. |
| What the coach does | The sixth worksheet statement, 'I don't ever want to experience… again', is not turned around like the others. The person replaces 'I don't ever want' with 'I am willing to' and then with 'I look forward to', for example 'I look forward to Paul lying to me again.' Katie explains that the same feelings may return, if only in thought, and that a person who sees discomfort as a call to inquiry may come to look forward to it. |
| Steps | (1) The person takes statement 6, for example 'I don't ever want Paul to lie to me again.' (2) The person rewrites it as 'I am willing to have Paul lie to me again.' (3) The person rewrites it as 'I look forward to having Paul lie to me again.' (4) The person considers that the same situation or feeling may happen again, 'if only in your thoughts', and meets it as an occasion for more inquiry. |
| What it does | The move turns a vow never to experience something again into willingness, and then eagerness, to meet it. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a person to stop bracing against a feared repeat, by treating its return as more material for inquiry; the person decides whether the new sentence is true for them. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| The evidence | The move has not been tested on its own. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The future: The move reverses a vow about the future ('never again') into willingness, so a feared recurrence becomes an expected occasion for work. Protection and defense: The move asks a person to lower the guard they set against a repeated hurt, which is its risk as well as its aim. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Katie asks the person to go past acceptance to looking forward to the painful event, while the book does not ask a reader to welcome a harm; its welcome is directed at the reader's own experience rather than at another person's conduct. |
