| Limits and critiques | Taken literally in a situation of ongoing abuse, 'I look forward to' could be read as consent to harm; Katie's texts frame it as a statement about the person's thoughts and feelings, not as advice to stay in danger. |
| Quoted from its sources | "For the turnaround to statement 6, replace the words "I don't ever want…" with "I am willing to…" and "I look forward to…"" (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/3-Judge-Your-Neighbor-Worksheet-v20250115-for-website.pdf) "These turnarounds are about embracing all of life, just as it is." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "When you realize that suffering and discomfort are the call to inquiry and to the freedom that follows, you may actually begin to look forward to uncomfortable feelings." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "Some of us have learned to accept what is, and I invite you to go further, to actually love what is." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "I look forward to Paul lying to me again." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) |
| Date | 1986 onward; stated in the worksheet revised 2025 and the 2019 booklet |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault has no record of this move; the note's allowing camp (Hudson) welcomes feelings, whereas this move welcomes the recurrence of an event. |
| Sources | https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/3-Judge-Your-Neighbor-Worksheet-v20250115-for-website.pdf; https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf; https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *statement 6* | the worksheet's last prompt, what the person never wants to experience again |
| *loving what is* | Katie's name for going beyond accepting reality to welcoming it |

### Whose business am I in?

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Katie offers the question for any moment of stress, outside a worksheet session. |
| What the coach does | When a person feels stress, they ask themselves whose business they are in mentally: their own, another person's, or reality's, which Katie calls God's business. Being in another person's business means deciding what they should do; being in God's business means worrying about things no one controls, such as earthquakes or one's death. Katie says the question returns a person to their own concerns and can make them laugh. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices stress or discomfort. (2) The person asks whose business they are in mentally, sorting the thought into their own business, another person's, or God's. (3) If the thought concerns what someone else should do, or what no one controls, the person notes that they have left their own business. (4) The person returns attention to what is theirs to do. |
| What it does | The question has the person sort the thought causing stress into their own concern, another person's, or what no one controls. |
| What is facilitated | The question makes it easier for a person to notice when their attention has left their own life for someone else's; no facilitator is needed. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| The evidence | The move has not been tested on its own. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: Katie calls deciding what is best for another person 'arrogance', which is a rule against a helper supplying another's content. Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Worry about matters no one controls is classed as leaving one's own business, which removes structural matters from the person's concern rather than addressing them. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move treats concern for what others do and for what no one controls as a source of stress to be dropped, while the book includes relationships and shared conditions within the reader's concern. |
| Quoted from its sources | "I can find only three kinds of business in the universe: mine, yours, and God's." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "Much of our stress comes from mentally living out of our own business." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "If I am mentally in your business or in God's business, the effect is separation." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "To think that I know what's best for anyone else is to be out of my business." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) "The next time you're feeling stress or discomfort, ask yourself whose business you're in mentally, and you may burst out laughing!" (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf) |
| Date | 1986 onward (Katie dates the observation to early 1986); stated in the 2019 booklet |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault has no record of this move; it is Katie's rule for sorting concerns and sits beside the note's list of five theories as a second source of stress in her own account. |
| Sources | https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *my business, your business, God's business* | Katie's three kinds of business: what is mine to decide, what is another's to decide, and what no one controls |
| *God* | Katie's word for reality, 'because it rules' |

### Anchoring the inquiry in one specific moment (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Katie uses the move before the worksheet is written and again at each of the four questions. |
| What the coach does | Before writing and throughout the questions, the person returns in memory to one specific moment of one situation, sees it, and feels the emotion they had then. The worksheets and questions are answered from inside that remembered moment rather than about the relationship in general. Katie's instructions repeat the anchoring at each question, asking the person to close their eyes and revisit the situation, time, and place. |
| Steps | (1) The person relaxes and becomes still. (2) The person travels in memory to a specific situation: 'Witness the situation. Be there now.' (3) The person notices, names, and feels the emotion they had at that time. (4) The person writes and answers each question while 'Staying anchored in the situation, at a specific moment in time.' (5) At each question the person closes their eyes and revisits the specific situation, time, and place. |
| What it does | The move keeps the person answering from inside one remembered moment rather than about a relationship in general. |
| What is facilitated | Anchoring keeps the inquiry on a concrete moment the person can actually examine, rather than on a general grievance; the person chooses the moment. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| The evidence | The move has not been tested on its own. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The fixed return to one moment limits the inquiry's scope, so the person works on a bounded memory rather than their whole history. Timing and pacing: The person re-enters the moment at every question, which slows the process to the pace of recall. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move roots each inquiry in one remembered moment, while the book's arc works over a longer span of preparation, onset, and integration rather than within one recalled scene. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Travel in your mind to a specific situation where you were angry, hurt, sad, or disappointed with someone. Witness the situation. Be there now." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Notice, name, and feel the emotion you were experiencing at the time." (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Staying anchored in the situation, at a specific moment in time, write down your responses" (https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/) "Mindfully revisit the specific situation, time, and place. Close your eyes, quietly open to what appears to you, and answer the following questions." (https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/07/2-One-Belief-At-A-Time-Worksheet-v20250115.pdf) |
| Date | 1986 onward; stated on thework.com and in the One-Belief-At-A-Time Worksheet revised 2025 |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault has no record of this instruction; it resembles the specificity rules in the vault's Gendlin reading only in that both keep attention on a concrete instance. |
| Sources | https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/; https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/07/2-One-Belief-At-A-Time-Worksheet-v20250115.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *witness the situation* | to recall the moment vividly and observe it rather than analyze it |
| *specific situation* | one particular time and place with one person, which Katie asks the person to revisit instead of a general grievance |

### Katie facilitating a volunteer in the chair (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Katie uses the move at her public events, workshops, and the School for The Work, with a volunteer who has a completed worksheet. |
| What the coach does | At Katie's public events a volunteer sits in a chair facing her on stage, reads aloud the answers on their worksheet, and is taken through the four questions and turnarounds in front of the audience, with sessions videotaped and volunteers wearing microphones. Katie addresses volunteers as 'sweetheart' or 'honey' while asking directly whether their stories are true, and she turns their statements around in public. The audience watches and, in Katie's framing, works on their own similar thoughts. |
| Steps | (1) A volunteer from the audience sits opposite Katie on stage, with a box of tissues between them. (2) Katie asks the volunteer to read their worksheet: 'Do you really want to know the truth? Let's hear No. 1'. (3) Katie takes each statement through the four questions, holding the volunteer to yes-or-no answers where the rules require. (4) Katie offers or elicits turnarounds, as when she told one volunteer, 'You be the mother figure. It's not God's responsibility to give you that'. (5) The audience watches the dialogue and applies it to their own thoughts. |
| What it does | The move has Katie take one volunteer through their worksheet in public while an audience watches. |
| What is facilitated | The public dialogue makes it easier for the volunteer to question a belief with Katie holding them to the procedure, and lets the audience rehearse the same questions; Katie supplies the questions and pressure, while the volunteer supplies the content. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Katie shapes the process and asks the questions while the volunteer supplies the worksheet and the answers; in doing so she can also refuse an answer, as the one-syllable rule allows. |
| The evidence | The Los Angeles Times article (2002) is a journalist's account; a clinical psychologist it quoted said Katie's first two questions 'are fundamental to cognitive behavioral therapy' and that individual work in public walks 'a fuzzy line of what is therapy.' |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: In 1999 the California Board of Psychology reviewed a tape of Katie working with an incest survivor, asking what would happen if the woman broke down afterward; Katie answered that the woman's state was her own responsibility, and the review was dropped. Participation rather than watching: One volunteer takes part while the audience watches, so most people present are spectators of another person's inquiry. What care costs the one who gives it: Katie places responsibility for a volunteer's state after the dialogue on the volunteer, which sets the limit of what she takes on as facilitator. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Katie holds that a participant's emotional state after a public dialogue is the participant's own responsibility, while the book treats the conditions that hold a person during change as part of what a facilitator owes them. |
| Limits and critiques | The Los Angeles Times also reported that Katie's New Year's program used a juice fast, which she described in her own words as producing deprivation on purpose ('It gets really radical and that's what I want'); combined with public dialogues about trauma, this is the setting in which a regulator raised concern in 1999. |
| Quoted from its sources | "A typical program begins with a volunteer from the crowd sitting in a chair opposite Katie onstage, a box of Kleenex between them." (https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html) "Do you really want to know the truth? Let's hear No. 1" (https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html) "Though she calls everyone "sweetheart" or "honey," there is little coddling." (https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html) "You be the mother figure. It's not God's responsibility to give you that" (https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html) "In 1999 the California Board of Psychology raised that issue after listening to a tape of Katie working with an incest survivor." (https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html) "Katie argued that the woman's emotional state after the dialogue was her own responsibility, just as it had been before they met." (https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html) "are fundamental to cognitive behavioral therapy" (https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html) "But she's working with people individually. When you do that, you're walking a fuzzy line of what is therapy." (https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html) "You take away people's chewing and their mind goes nuts, they start getting angry, feeling like little children. They feel deprived. It gets really radical and that's what I want." (https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html) |
| Date | Early 1990s onward (public workshops); described by the Los Angeles Times, 24 November 2002 |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault's material on Katie is only the corpus's summary; it holds nothing on her public facilitation or its critics. |
| Sources | https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-nov-24-tm-katie47-story.html; https://thework.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/02/English_LB.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *the chair* | the seat facing Katie where a volunteer does The Work in public |
| *School for The Work* | Katie's nine-day residential course, directed by her since 1998 |

## Tony Robbins

| Move | Type | When the coach uses it | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| Unresourceful states and the six human needs | a theory | Robbins states this account in his books, at the start of his events, and on his organization's site. | The account tells a person that their behavior follows from their state, their needs, and their story, all of which they can change. |
| Priming | a technique | Robbins does priming first thing every morning, and teaches it to people who want a daily practice. | The practice puts the person in a grateful, energized state and has them picture their day's three main outcomes as done. |
| Using a grateful state to solve a problem state (hands on the heart) | a technique | Robbins uses the exercise at events and in recordings when people have a stressful situation they have been avoiding. | The exercise puts the person in a grateful state and then has them ask what to do about a stressful situation. |
| The Dickens Process | a technique | Robbins uses the process at Unleash the Power Within when participants are working on the beliefs that hold them back. | The process has the person feel what a limiting belief has cost and will cost them, so that they want to change it now. |
| Don't think about it. Go to it. | a move | Robbins uses the move with athletes and performers whose results have dropped and whose attention is on a problem. | The move has the person re-enter a remembered peak moment and notice that their current focus was absent from it. |
| Ecube: entertain, educate, empower | a move | Robbins uses the method across his multiday live events such as Unleash the Power Within and Date with Destiny. | The method raises the audience's energy, teaches while it is high, and has people act while still in that state. |
| Let me show you where the weeds are | a move | Robbins uses the move when a person explains why they cannot change, in private sessions and on stage. | The move confronts a person's explanation as an excuse made out of fear and pushes them to act. |
| The firewalk | a technique | Robbins uses the firewalk at Unleash the Power Within, after hours of preparation. | The firewalk has participants walk over hot embers as bodily proof that they can act despite fear. |
| Turn your shoulds into musts | a move | Robbins uses the move when a person keeps failing to do something they say they want, such as exercise. | The move has the person make a wanted action non-negotiable instead of optional. |

### Unresourceful states and the six human needs (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| When the coach uses it | Robbins states this account in his books, at the start of his events, and on his organization's site. |
| What the coach does | Robbins holds that people 'always do the best we can with the resources available to us' but are sometimes in unresourceful states, and that a state is made of internal representations and physiology working 'in a cybernetic loop' (the vault's reading). His organization's account of motive is six human needs: certainty, uncertainty or variety, significance, connection and love, growth, and contribution, and it states that 'All dysfunctional behaviors arise from the inability to consistently meet these core needs.' A person's ranking of the needs is said to explain their personality. His material also places the obstacle in the person's narrative: 'The only thing that's keeping you from getting what you want is the story you keep telling yourself.' |
| What it does | The account tells a person that their behavior follows from their state, their needs, and their story, all of which they can change. |
| What is facilitated | On Robbins's account a person who changes state, meets their needs in healthy ways, and changes their story acts from their resources; Robbins and his events supply the techniques and the intensity, and the person supplies the decision to change. |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| The evidence | The six-needs model is presented on his organization's site without research citations, and his methods descend from neuro-linguistic programming, whose empirical record the vault's reading records as poor (Witkowski 2010; Passmore and Rowson 2019). No controlled study of the six-needs model was found. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Robbins places the cause of dysfunctional behavior in unmet individual needs and in the stories a person tells, and the vault's reading records his objection to a 'therapeutic model that says we're at the mercy of everything from childhood traumas to raging hormones'. Remaining the author of one's own change: The vault's reading records Robbins's rule that 'We must be the source of our change if our change is going to last,' which is his version of the person remaining the author of the change. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus files Robbins under 'a disempowered state — change it'; his organization's six-needs model adds a diagnostic account of why behavior goes wrong, so his position is not only a technique for changing state. The book changes structure slowly through participation in a held setting, while Robbins holds that the change is available now by decision (vault reading record). |
| Limits and critiques | In May 2019 BuzzFeed News published an investigation reporting that Robbins had berated abuse victims at events and accusing him of sexual misconduct with fans and staff; Robbins denied the allegations, sued BuzzFeed News in Ireland after a further report in November 2019, and his lawyers filed a notice of discontinuance in 2025 (Wikipedia). His firm denied that his comments to abuse victims were harmful. |
| Quoted from its sources | "We always do the best we can with the resources available to us, but sometimes we find ourselves in unresourceful states" (archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md) "Internal representation and physiology work together in a cybernetic loop." (archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md) "All dysfunctional behaviors arise from the inability to consistently meet these core needs." (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/blog/do-you-need-to-feel-significant) "Everyone ranks these human needs differently, and the way they are ranked explain why you are the way you are as a person" (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/blog/do-you-need-to-feel-significant) "The only thing that's keeping you from getting what you want is the story you keep telling yourself." (https://cdnwp.tonyrobbins.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/04/Limiting-Beliefs.pdf) "priming is if you want to have a prime life, you've got to be in a prime state." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "Robbins denied the allegations" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Robbins) "Robbins denied wrongdoing and sued" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Robbins) "Robbins's lawyers filed a notice of discontinuance in 2025." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Robbins) "Most of us have adopted a therapeutic model that says we're at the mercy of everything from childhood traumas to raging hormones" (notes/tony robbins.md) "We must be the source of our change if our change is going to last" (archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md) "reveals how Robbins has berated abuse victims and subjected his followers to unorthodox and potentially dangerous techniques." (https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets) "The firm denied that Robbins' comments to abuse victims were harmful, or that he exposed his fans to potentially dangerous techniques." (https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets) |
| Date | Unlimited Power (1986) and Awaken the Giant Within (1991) for the account of state (read by the vault); the six human needs as published on tonyrobbins.com (undated page); the 'prime state' line in 2014 |
| In the vault | archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md; The reading record (2026-09-02) establishes Robbins's account of state, his concession that state change alone does not last, and his identity-level change; this entry builds on it and adds the six-needs model and the documented criticism, neither of which the vault holds. |
| Sources | archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md; notes/tony robbins.md; https://www.tonyrobbins.com/blog/do-you-need-to-feel-significant; https://cdnwp.tonyrobbins.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/04/Limiting-Beliefs.pdf; https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Robbins; https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *state* | a person's condition at a moment, made of what they picture and say to themselves and how they use their body |
| *six human needs* | certainty, uncertainty or variety, significance, connection and love, growth, and contribution, which Robbins's organization says drive all behavior |
| *peak state or prime state* | the resourceful condition Robbins's practices aim to put a person in |
| *Neuro-Associative Conditioning* | Robbins's name for his method of changing what a person links pain and pleasure to (vault reading record) |

### Priming

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Robbins does priming first thing every morning, and teaches it to people who want a daily practice. |
| What the coach does | Priming is Robbins's ten-minute morning practice, which he distinguishes from meditation because he found sitting still with no thoughts did not work for him. It begins with 'an explosive change in my physiology' (hot and cold water) and a breathing pattern of three sets of thirty breaths, then three minutes of feeling back inside the body and feeling grateful for three things, extended outward to the people he loves, three minutes of feeling a presence of God 'however you want to language that for yourself' healing every area of life, and three minutes on 'three things that I'm going to happen', his 'three to thrive', which he sees and feels as done. He says he does not miss it even on no sleep, and tells people 'If you don't have ten minutes, you don't have a life.' |
| Steps | (1) Robbins makes an explosive change in physiology, in his case alternating hot and cold water. (2) Robbins breathes in a specific pattern: 'I do 30 of these breaths, and I do them at three sets of 30'. (3) For three minutes he feels back inside his body and outside his head and feels grateful for three things, one of them very simple, such as the wind on his face. (4) He lets gratitude fill him rather than only thinking it ('I let gratitude fill my soul'), and in the same part extends it in a circle from the people closest to him to everyone he loves. (5) For three minutes he focuses on feeling a presence of God, in whatever words the person prefers, healing body, mind, emotions, relationships, and finances. (6) For the last three minutes he focuses on 'three things that I'm going to happen, my three to thrive', seeing and feeling them. (7) He does this every day, for at least ten minutes and often twenty to thirty. |
| What it does | The practice puts the person in a grateful, energized state and has them picture their day's three main outcomes as done. |
| What is facilitated | Priming makes it easier for Robbins, and for those who copy it, to start the day in a grateful and determined state; the person does the whole practice alone. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. Robbins supplies the format; the person chooses what to be grateful for and what to accomplish. |
| The evidence | No controlled study of priming as a whole was found. Robbins's organization says gratitude 'shifts your brain's chemistry by increasing dopamine and serotonin' without citing a study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The future: The practice ends by picturing three chosen outcomes as already accomplished. Timing and pacing: Robbins sets the practice first thing every morning, before anything else in the day. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Priming produces a chosen state every morning by breath, gratitude, and visualization, which the book would treat as managing a state; the book's practice attends to what is present rather than installing a state. |
| Limits and critiques | The practice includes strong breathing (three sets of thirty breaths) and cold exposure; Robbins's own description gives no safety guidance for them. |
| Quoted from its sources | "priming is if you want to have a prime life, you've got to be in a prime state." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "I do an explosive change in my physiology." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "I do 30 of these breaths, and I do them at three sets of 30" (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "Three minutes of just me feeling back inside my body and outside my head, feeling the Earth and my body and the experience, and then feeling totally grateful for three things." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "And I make sure that one of them is very very simple, the wind on my face" (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "But I don't just think gratitude. It's like I let gratitude fill my soul" (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "Then the second three minutes that I do is a total focus on feeling presence of God, if you will, however you want to language that for yourself" (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "And then the last three minutes are me focusing on three things that I'm going to happen, my three to thrive." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "If you don't have ten minutes, you don't have a life." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "Then trade your expectation for appreciation, and you'll have a whole new life." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "Gratitude shifts your brain's chemistry by increasing dopamine and serotonin" (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/health-vitality/train-your-brain-in-minutes-4/) |
| Date | Described by Robbins in The Tim Ferriss Show, episode #37, published 15 October 2014 |
| In the vault | `notes/tony robbins.md`; The vault's Robbins note records his claim that physiology gives the fastest leverage on state; priming is the daily practice in which he applies it, which the vault does not record. |
| Sources | https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf; https://tim.blog/2014/10/15/money-master-the-game/; https://www.tonyrobbins.com/health-vitality/train-your-brain-in-minutes-4/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *priming* | Robbins's daily ritual for putting himself in a resourceful state before the day begins |
| *three to thrive* | the three outcomes Robbins pictures as accomplished at the end of priming |
| *expectation versus appreciation* | Robbins's contrast between a life based on what one expects and a life based on gratitude |

### Using a grateful state to solve a problem state (hands on the heart)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Robbins uses the exercise at events and in recordings when people have a stressful situation they have been avoiding. |
| What the coach does | Robbins has people pick a piece of unfinished business that stresses them at seven or more on a ten-point scale, then put both hands on their heart, breathe into it, and feel gratitude for the heart itself. He then has them step into three remembered moments of gratitude, one after another, and one coincidence that led to something good, re-experiencing each through the body. Still breathing into the heart, they turn back to the stressful situation and ask what they need to focus on, remember, and do in it; Robbins says 'Your heart knows the answer' and that in a normal room 98 or 99 percent of people get one. |
| Steps | (1) The person picks unfinished business rated at least seven out of ten for stress, without saying what it is: 'You do not need to tell us the content.' (2) The person puts both hands on the heart and breathes deep into it, feeling its strength and gratitude for it. (3) The person recalls a moment they could feel grateful for and steps into it 'as if you were there', seeing, hearing, and breathing as they did then. (4) The person repeats this for a second and a third moment, then for a coincidence that led to something they are grateful for. (5) Still in that state, the person thinks of the stressful situation: 'Let us use the state to solve the problem state.' (6) The person asks: 'All I need to focus on, all I need to remember, all I need to do in that situation is what?' (7) The person takes the answer that comes as what to do next. |
| What it does | The exercise puts the person in a grateful state and then has them ask what to do about a stressful situation. |
| What is facilitated | The exercise makes it easier for a person to face a stressful situation from a calm, grateful state and find their own next step; Robbins supplies the guidance and the person supplies the situation and the answer. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Robbins guides the process aloud while each listener supplies a private situation: 'You do not need to tell us the content.'. |
| The evidence | Robbins claims that during the exercise the heart and brain recordings (EEG and EKG) 'literally sync up. They become identical', citing unnamed 'scientific studies'; no study was named and none was checked. No controlled study of this practice was found; the support offered is Robbins's own account and his organization's pages. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Decisions: The exercise ends with the person asking, from a changed state, what they need to do in a situation they have avoided. Who decides the content: Robbins tells participants not to share the content, so each keeps their situation and answer private while he directs the state. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The exercise changes state first and then consults 'the heart' for an answer, where the book lets a felt sense arise without first producing a chosen state; the book also does not claim that a person's answer is correct because it comes from the heart. |
| Limits and critiques | Robbins's claim about synchronized heart and brain recordings is presented as science without a citation, and no study was checked for this entry. |
| Quoted from its sources | "pick something that is unfinished business in your business or personal life, something or someone, and it is at least a seven, eight, nine, or a ten" (https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/) "You do not need to tell us the content." (https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/) "Put both of your hands on your heart and physically breathe deep into your heart." (https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/) "Then, step into that memory for a minute, like step in your body as if you were there." (https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/) "The reason we use gratitude by the way, is the two emotions that mess us up most is anger and fear." (https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/) "You cannot be grateful and angry simultaneously. It is the antidote." (https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/) "Let us use the state to solve the problem state." (https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/) "All I need to focus on, all I need to remember, all I need to do in that situation is what?" (https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/) "Your heart knows the answer." (https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/) "they literally sync up. They become identical." (https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/) "There are scientific studies that show that when your mind and your heart" (https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/) |
| Date | Led by Robbins in The Tim Ferriss Show, episode #186, published 18 September 2016 |
| In the vault | `notes/tony robbins.md`; The vault's Robbins note records his priority on physiology and state; this exercise, led live in his words, is not recorded in the vault. |
| Sources | https://tim.blog/2018/06/05/the-tim-ferriss-show-transcripts-tony-robbins-on-how-to-resolve-internal-conflict/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *unfinished business* | a situation with a person or matter that should have been handled and has not been, and causes stress |
| *beautiful state* | Robbins's term for the grateful, calm state produced by the first part of the exercise |

### The Dickens Process

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Robbins uses the process at Unleash the Power Within when participants are working on the beliefs that hold them back. |
| What the coach does | Named after the ghosts of Christmas past, present, and future in Charles Dickens, the process has a person examine one limiting belief across three times. The person asks what the belief has cost them in the past, what it is costing them and the people they love now, and what they will lose and regret one, three, five, and ten years from now if they keep it. Robbins's organization gives Tim Ferriss's belief that he 'just wasn't wired for happiness' as an example replaced by 'Happiness is my natural state.' |
| Steps | (1) The person names a limiting belief. (2) Past: 'What have your beliefs cost you in the past? What have you lost?' (3) Present: the person asks what the belief is costing them right now and what it is costing the people they love. (4) Future: 'What will you regret one, three, five and 10 years from now if you continue to hold on to your limiting beliefs?' (5) The person replaces the belief with a new one, as in the example 'Happiness is my natural state.' |
| What it does | The process has the person feel what a limiting belief has cost and will cost them, so that they want to change it now. |
| What is facilitated | The process makes a belief's cost vivid enough that the person wants to change it; the person supplies the belief and its costs, and Robbins supplies the questions and the setting. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The page describes the questions the participant answers about their own beliefs; how Robbins leads the process in the room is not described there. |
| The evidence | No controlled study of this practice was found; the support offered is Robbins's own account and his organization's pages. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The future: The process projects the cost of a belief ten years ahead to create motivation to change now. Timing and pacing: The process tries to produce, in one sitting, the pain that would otherwise build over years, which is the opposite of the book's gradual pacing. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The process uses the imagined future pain of keeping a belief to force a change now, while the book does not use pain as leverage and lets change arrive at the reader's pace. |
| Limits and critiques | The process deliberately intensifies pain about one's life; the organization's page describes no screening or aftercare for participants in distress. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The Dickens process helps you discover how to unlock your true potential by examining your limiting beliefs – your "ghosts" – across those three tenses." (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/personal-growth/unlocking-potential) "Yes, it's named after author Charles Dickens – because its inspiration comes from the ghosts of Christmas past, present and future." (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/personal-growth/unlocking-potential) "What have your beliefs cost you in the past? What have you lost?" (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/personal-growth/unlocking-potential) "What will you regret one, three, five and 10 years from now if you continue to hold on to your limiting beliefs?" (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/personal-growth/unlocking-potential) "Ferriss shared his top limiting belief: that he just wasn't wired for happiness." (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/personal-growth/unlocking-potential) |
| Date | Not confirmed; described on tonyrobbins.com (undated page) as an exercise at Unleash the Power Within |
| In the vault | archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md; The reading record describes Robbins's Neuro-Associative Conditioning and his concession that willpower alone does not produce lasting change; the Dickens Process, an event exercise that links pain to keeping a belief, is not recorded in the vault. |
| Sources | https://www.tonyrobbins.com/personal-growth/unlocking-potential; archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Dickens Process* | Robbins's exercise examining a limiting belief's cost in past, present, and future, named after A Christmas Carol |
| *limiting belief* | a belief that Robbins says holds a person back from acting on their potential |

### Don't think about it. Go to it.

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Robbins uses the move with athletes and performers whose results have dropped and whose attention is on a problem. |
| What the coach does | Robbins describes working with the tennis player Andre Agassi, who was focused on an injured wrist. Robbins told him to recall a time he hit a ball perfectly, and not to think about it but to go into it; once Agassi was in that state, Robbins asked whether he had been thinking about his wrist. When Agassi said no, Robbins asked how he expected to get back to peak performance by focusing on it, and said his job was to get performers 'back into the pattern' that made it work and then resolve whatever else was distracting them. |
| Steps | (1) Robbins asks the person to recall a time they performed perfectly: 'Think about the time you hit a tennis ball perfectly.' (2) Robbins has them enter the memory rather than describe it: 'Don't think about it. Go to it.' (3) Once the person is in that state, Robbins asks what they were focused on then, for example: 'Were you thinking about your wrist?' (4) Robbins contrasts it with their present focus: 'Then how the hell would you think you'd ever get back to that peak performance focusing on your wrist?' (5) Robbins then helps resolve any other issue in the person's life that is distracting them. |
| What it does | The move has the person re-enter a remembered peak moment and notice that their current focus was absent from it. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a performer to re-enter the state in which they did well and notice what their attention was on; Robbins directs the recall and draws the conclusion. |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| The evidence | The account is Robbins's own anecdote. No controlled study of this practice was found; the support offered is Robbins's own account and his organization's pages. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Who decides the content: Robbins directs the recall and states the lesson, while the person supplies the memory. Knowing that something happened: Robbins uses the person's own peak memory as evidence that their current focus is wrong. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move uses a remembered best moment to reset attention quickly, while the book does not direct a reader into a chosen past state. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Think about the time you hit a tennis ball perfectly." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "Don't think about it. Go to it." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "Were you thinking about your wrist?" (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "Then how the hell would you think you'd ever get back to that peak performance focusing on your wrist?" (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "So I've got to get them back into the pattern" (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "And then oftentimes I've got to help them resolve some other issue that's distracting them" (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) |
| Date | Described by Robbins in The Tim Ferriss Show, episode #37, published 15 October 2014 (the session with Andre Agassi it describes took place early in Agassi's career) |
| In the vault | `notes/tony robbins.md`; The vault's Robbins note records that he names Ericksonian hypnosis and NLP in his training; this recall-and-contrast move is recorded here from his own account. |
| Sources | https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf; https://tim.blog/2014/10/15/money-master-the-game/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *peak state* | the state a person was in when performing at their best, which Robbins has them re-enter |
| *pattern* | the way of using body and focus that produced a person's best performance, which Robbins tries to return them to |

### Ecube: entertain, educate, empower

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Robbins uses the method across his multiday live events such as Unleash the Power Within and Date with Destiny. |
| What the coach does | Robbins describes his event method as 'Ecube': he first entertains the audience, on the view that people would rather be entertained than educated, so that they have an experience of feeling fully alive; he then educates them with tools; and he then empowers them by having them, while in that state, 'do things that you won't forget' and that create momentum. He rejects the view that he is 'taking over weak people's minds and pumping them up' and calls himself a strategist. |
| Steps | (1) Entertain: Robbins raises the room's energy so participants feel fully alive. (2) Educate: while participants are in that state, he teaches strategies and tools. (3) Empower: while they are still in that state, he has them do things they will not forget and that create momentum. |
| What it does | The method raises the audience's energy, teaches while it is high, and has people act while still in that state. |
| What is facilitated | The method makes it easier for an audience to take in and act on teaching while in a heightened state; Robbins supplies the state, the teaching, and the tasks. |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| The evidence | The account is Robbins's own. BuzzFeed News's 2019 investigation reported that his multiday events are held in deliberately cold rooms and run 'from early in the morning to well past midnight, with few breaks for food and water', and cited internal company emails about 'sleep deprivation and dehydration'; his firm denied that he exposed fans to dangerous techniques. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: BuzzFeed News reported internal concern about breakdowns, sleep deprivation, and dehydration at long events; the conditions Robbins uses to raise state are the conditions critics name as harmful. Participation rather than watching: The third step requires participants to act, not only to listen, which Robbins treats as what makes the learning last. The container that holds people while they change: The container is an arena of thousands raised to a shared high state, the opposite of the small held setting the book assumes. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The method raises a crowd's state so that teaching goes in more deeply, while the book addresses a reader alone and treats a produced high state with caution. |
| Limits and critiques | Large-group awareness trainings of the 1970s drew reports of psychiatric casualties (survey entry); the BuzzFeed investigation reports similar concerns about Robbins's events, which his firm disputes. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Ecube is I entertain them first because people in our society want to be educated, but they'd much rather be entertained." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "And the third one is I empower you. While you're in this state, I get you to do things that you won't forget." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "The assumption is, "He's taking over weak people's minds and pumping them up and giving them all this enthusiasm."" (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "Robbins' intensive multiday events are often held in rooms kept deliberately cold and run from early in the morning to well past midnight, with few breaks for food and water." (https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets) "Internal company emails reveal concerns about fans suffering mental breakdowns after days of emotional exhaustion as well as "sleep deprivation and dehydration."" (https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets) "The firm denied that Robbins' comments to abuse victims were harmful, or that he exposed his fans to potentially dangerous techniques." (https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets) |
| Date | Described by Robbins in The Tim Ferriss Show, episode #37, published 15 October 2014 |
| In the vault | `notes/tony robbins.md`; The vault's Robbins note records his 'live intervention record' as a work but does not describe his event method; the vault's note on Werner Erhard holds the neighboring est lineage. |
| Sources | https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf; https://tim.blog/2014/10/15/money-master-the-game/; https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Ecube* | Robbins's name for his three-part event method: entertain, educate, empower |
| *state* | the heightened condition in which Robbins says learning goes deeper |

### Let me show you where the weeds are

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Robbins uses the move when a person explains why they cannot change, in private sessions and on stage. |
| What the coach does | Robbins describes his approach as confrontational rather than reassuring: he says he is not the one who says there are no weeds but the one who says 'Let me show you where the God damn weeds are', and that he does not tolerate the stories people make up out of fear of failing. BuzzFeed News reported in 2019, from leaked recordings, that this confrontation extended to telling abuse victims at events that they were playing the victim, and quoted experts on domestic violence who called berating traumatized women 'a dangerous strategy'. One woman Robbins had accused of 'lying' about her abusive husband told BuzzFeed the exchange was positive and helped her leave. |
| Steps | (1) The person tells a story about why they cannot change. (2) Robbins names it as a story made up out of fear of failing: 'And I don't tolerate that shit.' (3) Robbins points to what he sees as the real problem: 'Let me show you where the God damn weeds are'. (4) Robbins commits to doing the work with the person: 'You and I, I'll do it with you.' |
| What it does | The move confronts a person's explanation as an excuse made out of fear and pushes them to act. |
| What is facilitated | The move is meant to make it harder for a person to hide behind an excuse; Robbins supplies the confrontation and the person must respond. |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| The evidence | Robbins's description is his own; BuzzFeed News's account rests on leaked recordings, internal documents, and interviews, and his firm denied that his comments to abuse victims were harmful. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: Experts on domestic and sexual violence who reviewed transcripts said berating traumatized women and blaming them for their reactions is dangerous; Ruth Glenn called it 'a secondary assault'. Who decides the content: Robbins decides which parts of the person's account are stories to be dismissed. Protection and defense: The move treats a person's explanation for not changing as a defense to be broken down rather than understood. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book treats a person's protective stories as having functions and meets them without confrontation, while Robbins names them as excuses and does not tolerate them. |
| Limits and critiques | The documented harm is the reporting on his treatment of abuse victims, which Robbins's firm disputes; BuzzFeed also noted that self-help coaching 'requires no such qualifications or standards' as licensed therapy. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Let me show you where the God damn weeds are, and let's pull these son of a bitches out right now and stop bullshitting. Let's do it. You and I, I'll do it with you." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "And I don't tolerate that shit." (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "They make it much worse than it is because they're afraid of failing" (https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf) "Several leading national experts on domestic and sexual violence who reviewed transcripts of Robbins' private events said berating traumatized women and blaming them for their reactions to abuse is a dangerous strategy." (https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets) "It's not only secondary trauma, but a secondary assault," said Ruth Glenn, president of the National Coalition Against Domestic Violence." (https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets) "told BuzzFeed News it was a positive experience and that she was grateful for the advice not to be a victim, which had helped her leave that relationship." (https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets) "He spoke openly about his use of "taboo language," humor, and other shock tactics to try to shake fans out of emotional stagnation" (https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets) "Licensed professionals who treat mental health issues must undergo extensive training and follow strict ethical guidelines governing their relations with their clients. Self-help coaching requires no such qualifications or standards." (https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets) "The firm denied that Robbins' comments to abuse victims were harmful, or that he exposed his fans to potentially dangerous techniques." (https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets) "the documented criticisms of his own practice" (notes/tony robbins.md) |
| Date | Described by Robbins in The Tim Ferriss Show, episode #37, published 15 October 2014 |
| In the vault | `notes/tony robbins.md`; The vault's Robbins note says his documented criticisms remain to be recorded ('the documented criticisms of his own practice'); this entry records them from BuzzFeed News with his firm's denial. |
| Sources | https://tim.blog/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/37-tony-robbins-part-1.pdf; https://www.buzzfeednews.com/article/janebradley/tony-robbins-self-help-secrets |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *weeds* | Robbins's word for the problems and excuses he confronts directly |
| *taboo language* | swearing and shock tactics Robbins says he uses to break people out of stagnation (as reported by BuzzFeed News) |

### The firewalk

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Robbins uses the firewalk at Unleash the Power Within, after hours of preparation. |
| What the coach does | At Unleash the Power Within, after hours of preparation to reach what his organization calls a peak state, participants walk barefoot across a bed of glowing embers while thousands of others cheer. Robbins led his first firewalk in 1985 with about fifty people and says he walked first; he tells of a woman in 1998 who walked 'with a few words, a shift in her physiology, and the roar of thousands behind her'. He presents it as a declaration: 'I am bigger than my fears. I am the master of my state', and writes that 'the fire doesn't burn you. Your fear does.' |
| Steps | (1) Participants prepare for hours to reach a peak state. (2) Robbins or a leader walks first. (3) Each participant, encouraged by the crowd, changes their physiology and walks the bed of embers. (4) Participants treat the crossing as proof that fear, not fire, was the limit. |
| What it does | The firewalk has participants walk over hot embers as bodily proof that they can act despite fear. |
| What is facilitated | The firewalk makes it easier for a participant to have a bodily experience of doing something they feared; Robbins and the crowd supply the preparation, encouragement, and setting. |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| The evidence | Physics explains why most walkers are not burned: the foot does not touch the embers long enough and embers conduct heat poorly (Wikipedia, Firewalking), which contradicts Robbins's claim that fear, not fire, burns. A field study of a Hindu firewalk in Mauritius found that walkers showed a larger rise in heart rate and reported more happiness afterward than related non-walkers and spectators, but it was quasi-experimental and does not establish lasting change. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: Burns at Robbins's firewalks were reported in San Jose in 2012 and in Dallas in 2016, where 'dozens were burned and required medical attention'; his organization said only 5 of 7,000 participants asked for examination beyond on-site care. Participation rather than watching: In the Mauritius study the walkers, not the spectators, showed the largest changes, which fits the rule that change goes to those who take part. Knowing that something happened: Robbins presents the unburned crossing as proof of mastery over fear, while physics explains the crossing without reference to fear, so the proof does not show what it is said to show. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The firewalk produces a dramatic proof in one night, while the book holds that its own promised event cannot be engineered and treats manufactured peaks with suspicion. |
| Limits and critiques | Documented burns in 2012 and 2016 (Robbins and on-site medical staff challenged aspects of the 2012 reports); Wikipedia's article notes that people are burned when they stay on the embers too long, run, or step on foreign objects. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The first firewalk I led was in 1985, at a small seminar in San Diego." (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/blog/origin-story-of-the-firewalk) "But I also knew it had to be done with respect, safety, and deep intention." (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/blog/origin-story-of-the-firewalk) "with a few words, a shift in her physiology, and the roar of thousands behind her, she walked." (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/blog/origin-story-of-the-firewalk) "Firewalking is more than a physical feat. It's a declaration: I am bigger than my fears. I am the master of my state." (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/blog/origin-story-of-the-firewalk) "the fire doesn't burn you. Your fear does." (https://www.tonyrobbins.com/blog/origin-story-of-the-firewalk) "concluding that the foot does not touch the hot surface long enough to burn and that embers are poor conductors of heat." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firewalking) "found that firewalkers showed the greatest increase in heart rate and reported greater happiness after the ritual than related non-walkers and unrelated spectators." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firewalking) "The study was quasi-experimental and does not establish that firewalking produces lasting personality change" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firewalking) "People have burned their feet when they remained in the fire for too long, enabling the thermal conductivity of the embers to catch up." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firewalking) "several people had been burned and hospitalized during one of Robbins's fire-walking events on July 19, 2012" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Robbins) "dozens were burned and required medical attention after attempting to walk on hot coals during a fire-walking event at a Tony Robbins seminar in Dallas, Texas" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Robbins) "only 5 of 7,000 participants requested an examination beyond what was readily available on site." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Robbins) "Aspects of these reports were later challenged by Robbins himself along with some of the on-site medical professionals." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Robbins) |
| Date | 1985 (Robbins's first firewalk, at a seminar in San Diego); a standing part of Unleash the Power Within since then |
| In the vault | `notes/both poles of the convergence refuse the engineered epiphany.md`; The note records that both of the book's founding traditions refuse the engineered epiphany; the firewalk is a clear case of an engineered, crowd-produced peak experience. |
| Sources | https://www.tonyrobbins.com/blog/origin-story-of-the-firewalk; https://www.tonyrobbins.com/personal-growth/unlocking-potential; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firewalking; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Robbins |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *firewalk* | walking barefoot over a bed of hot embers, used by Robbins as a test of mastery over fear |
| *peak state* | the heightened condition participants are prepared into before walking |

### Turn your shoulds into musts

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Robbins uses the move when a person keeps failing to do something they say they want, such as exercise. |
| What the coach does | Robbins's material explains why some people act and others do not by whether the action is a 'must' or a 'should' for them: for some, working out is a must, and for others a should. The instruction is to turn one's shoulds into musts, raising a standard from something one would like to do into something one will not go without, and to begin each day with 'a definite plan'. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices an action they say they should do but do not. (2) The person raises it from a should to a must, a standard they will not break. (3) The person tracks where their time goes and begins each day with a definite plan. |
| What it does | The move has the person make a wanted action non-negotiable instead of optional. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a person to act on something they keep postponing by changing how binding it is to them; the person chooses which shoulds to raise. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| The evidence | No controlled study of this practice was found; the support offered is Robbins's own account and his organization's pages. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Decisions: The move converts a preference into a binding decision about one's standards. Remaining the author of one's own change: The person sets their own musts, which keeps the standard theirs. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move relies on raising the force of the will, which the book's middle voice, in which action happens through a person with their consent, sets beside rather than follows. |
| Quoted from its sources | "The key is for you to turn your shoulds into musts." (https://cdnwp.tonyrobbins.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/04/Limiting-Beliefs.pdf) "because for some people, working out is a MUST, and for others it's a SHOULD." (https://cdnwp.tonyrobbins.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/04/Limiting-Beliefs.pdf) |
| Date | Stated in the Robbins Research International booklet on limiting beliefs (copyright 2015) |
| In the vault | `notes/the middle voice is only earned against the voluntarist pole at full strength.md`; The note records Robbins as the will pole the book's middle voice must answer at full strength; turning shoulds into musts is a plain statement of that pole in his own organization's words. |
| Sources | https://cdnwp.tonyrobbins.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/04/Limiting-Beliefs.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *should versus must* | Robbins's distinction between what a person would like to do and what they have made non-negotiable |
| *definite plan* | the plan Robbins's booklet tells a person to begin each day with, so that a must has a place in the day |

## Michael Singer

| Move | Type | When the coach uses it | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| Samskaras and the blocked flow of energy | a theory | Singer states this account in his books and in talks at the Temple of the Universe before teaching any practice. | The account tells a person that their disturbances are stored resistances that will release if they stop resisting. |
| Are you in there? | a move | Singer uses the question at the very beginning of teaching, with a newcomer or at the start of a book. | The question has the person notice that there is someone inside who is aware. |
| Witness consciousness (sitting in the seat of consciousness) | a technique | Singer teaches the practice for any moment when a thought or feeling pulls the person's attention away. | The practice has the person watch their thoughts and emotions as the one who is aware of them. |
| Relax and release | a move | Singer teaches the move for the moment a familiar thought or feeling is set off again. | The move has the person relax and fall back behind a feeling so that it can pass. |
| Low-hanging fruit | a technique | Singer teaches the practice for daily life, long before any crisis. | The practice has the person let go of resistance to small daily annoyances as training for larger ones. |
| Replacing a negative thought with a willful positive one | a move | Singer uses the move when a habitual complaint or negative thought arises about something that cannot be changed. | The move has the person add a chosen positive thought and attend to it without fighting the negative one. |
| The mantra 'I can handle this' | a move | Singer recommends the move when the mind says 'I can't handle this' in response to something upsetting. | The move has the person shift attention to a repeated phrase rather than fight the distressing thought. |
| Transmutation: keep your hands off | a move | Singer teaches the move for the moment an old, stored feeling is set off and begins to rise. | The move has the person stop interfering with a rising feeling so that it can change on its own. |
| Living from a place of surrender (checking outside first) | a technique | Singer teaches the practice for daily choices, requests from others, and major decisions. | The practice has the person look first at what a situation presents and at their own motive before choosing. |
| Honoring the reality that took place, then asking how to help | a move | Singer describes the move for news of a public atrocity or any harm a person cannot undo. | The move has the person first accept that a harm happened and then act to help rather than to relieve their own distress. |

### Samskaras and the blocked flow of energy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| When the coach uses it | Singer states this account in his books and in talks at the Temple of the Universe before teaching any practice. |
| What the coach does | Singer holds that experiences come in through the senses and pass through consciousness unless the person resists one; the act of resisting, which he calls an act of will, keeps that experience's impression stored inside as a samskara. Stored samskaras, including pleasant ones a person clings to, block an inner flow of energy he calls Shakti and later push back up as the thoughts, emotions, likes, and dislikes that disturb the person. He says the roots of samskaras are stored in the heart, and he states the practical upshot as: 'The moment in front of you is not bothering you. You are bothering yourself about the moment in front of you.' |
| What it does | The account tells a person that their disturbances are stored resistances that will release if they stop resisting. |
| What is facilitated | On Singer's account the person's own letting go releases stored patterns, after which the energy flows without effort; the teacher supplies an explanation and practices, not the release itself. |
| Who decides | not applicable |
| The evidence | No study of Singer's practices was found; the support is his own account, the teaching of the tradition he draws on, and the wide readership of his books. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: Singer treats resistance to an unwelcome experience, a protective act, as the very thing that stores it and makes it return. Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Singer places the cause of disturbance in the person's resistance to events rather than in the events, while also saying acceptance does not mean inaction. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus files Singer under suffering as a believed thought, but in his own words the cause is resistance, 'an energetic thing' that is 'not a mental thing', which is nearer the corpus's resisted-feeling camp, and the corpus is itself split on him: arc-gloss p. 10 lists 'Singer's watcher' in the epistemic camp, while p. 13 names 'the allowers' camp vindicated at altitude' as 'Hudson's welcome and Singer's watcher'; his remedy, relaxing behind the feeling without suppressing or expressing it, differs from Joe Hudson's, whose welcome includes obligatory expression through the body. The book reads a difficulty as a contraction to be released, which is close to Singer's account of a blocked flow. |
| Limits and critiques | Singer's account treats each person's disturbance as something they are doing to themselves ('You are bothering yourself'), which a person facing injustice may hear as blame; Singer answers that acceptance 'doesn't end up meaning you don't do anything' (see the entry on accepting reality before acting). |
| Quoted from its sources | "That's an energetic thing inside where you resist; it's called resistance." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "That is what a samskara is, a blocked internal experience that you did not let go through." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "The roots of samskaras are stored in the heart." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "The moment in front of you is not bothering you. You are bothering yourself about the moment in front of you." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "It starts with acceptance, but it doesn't end up meaning you don't do anything." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "In case you haven't noticed, you have a mental dialogue going on inside your head that never stops." (https://michaelsingerpodcast.com) |
| Date | Stated in Singer's interview with Tami Simon about Living Untethered (Sounds True, published 11 June 2022); the book appeared in 2022; the earlier account is in The Untethered Soul (2007) |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The note records the corpus's summary of Singer, 'the untethered witness, surrender as method', under the believed-thought camp; his own account, read here, places the cause in resistance rather than belief. The vault has no note on Singer, and the practice-model survey calls him 'the one reflexive instance' in that camp. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/; https://michaelsingerpodcast.com; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Alan_Singer |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *samskara* | in Singer's usage, the stored impression of an experience a person resisted or clung to, which keeps coming back up |
| *Shakti* | Singer's word for the inner energy a person feels as enthusiasm, love, or its absence |
| *resistance* | the inner act of pushing an experience away, which Singer says is 'an energetic thing' rather than a mental one |
| *untethered* | free of the stored patterns that hold the energy down, in Singer's image of a hot air balloon whose ropes are cut |

### Are you in there?

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Singer uses the question at the very beginning of teaching, with a newcomer or at the start of a book. |
| What the coach does | Singer opens his teaching by asking a person whether they are in there, meaning whether there is someone inside who is aware. He notes that no one has ever answered no, since whoever answered would have to be in there. He follows with questions about who sees and hears through the senses and where inside one listens from, which he calls the beginning of all spiritual journeys. |
| Steps | (1) The teacher or the book asks: 'Are you in there?' (2) The person notices that they answer yes, and that someone must be present to answer. (3) The teacher asks who sees through the eyes and hears through the ears. (4) The teacher asks where inside the person listens from, which Singer calls the deep question. |
| What it does | The question has the person notice that there is someone inside who is aware. |
| What is facilitated | The question makes it easier for a person to notice that they are the one who is aware, not the things they are aware of; the person supplies the noticing. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. Singer puts the question in his book and talks; the reader answers it alone. |
| The evidence | No study of Singer's practices was found; the support is his own account, the teaching of the tradition he draws on, and the wide readership of his books. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Recognition: The question asks a person to notice what has been present all along, the one who is aware, which is close to the book's promised event of recognizing what never left. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Singer's question points to an awareness he calls 'not human' and 'divine' at its source, a metaphysical claim the book does not require of its reader. |
| Quoted from its sources | "It's for a person to ask or someone to ask someone the question, "Are you in there?" I've never had anybody say, "No, I'm not in here."" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "That's a real centering beginning that anybody can deal with." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "There's a place inside of yourself from which you listen." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "They're not used to focusing on the consciousness. They're used to focusing on the object of consciousness." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "The source of consciousness is not human. It's divine. It's universal." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) |
| Date | Stated in Singer's interview with Tami Simon about Living Untethered (Sounds True, published 11 June 2022); the book appeared in 2022 |
| In the vault | `notes/reading is remembering.md`; The note records the book's promise that reading is remembering; Singer's opening question asks a reader to notice what is already present, without the book's sources behind it. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *seat of awareness* | the place inside from which Singer says a person experiences everything |
| *object of consciousness* | anything a person is aware of, as distinct from the one who is aware |

### Witness consciousness (sitting in the seat of consciousness)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Singer teaches the practice for any moment when a thought or feeling pulls the person's attention away. |
| What the coach does | The person stays in what Singer calls the seat of consciousness and watches three kinds of objects he calls the 'three-ring circus': the outside world, the thoughts in the mind, and the emotions in the heart. The person does not try to change, suppress, or express what they see; they notice that they are the one noticing. Singer ranks this above positive thinking and mantra because it does nothing with the mind at all. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices that they are aware of the outside world, of thoughts, and of emotions: the three rings. (2) The person remains as the one who notices, 'sitting in the seat of consciousness and being willing to watch what your mind's doing.' (3) The person does not interfere: 'Don't mess with the mind. Don't mess with the emotions. Don't suppress them or express them.' (4) When pulled into a thought or feeling, the person notices the pull and returns to watching. |
| What it does | The practice has the person watch their thoughts and emotions as the one who is aware of them. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it easier for a person to experience thoughts and emotions without being carried off by them; the person does the watching, and no teacher is present. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work. The practice is learned from Singer's books and talks and carried out alone. |
