| The evidence | No study of Singer's practices was found; the support is his own account, the teaching of the tradition he draws on, and the wide readership of his books. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Remaining the author of one's own change: The practice is carried out entirely by the person, with the teacher's role limited to describing it. Protection and defense: Singer tells the person not to suppress or express a feeling but to watch it, which removes both the defensive push and the discharge. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus pairs the two practices: 'Hudson's welcome and Singer's watcher are, at the peak, not techniques but survival equipment' (arc-gloss p. 13), but Hudson refuses a watching stance from above and requires expression through the body, while Singer asks the person to stay behind the feeling and neither suppress nor express it. The book's own practice attends from inside a feeling rather than from behind it. |
| Quoted from its sources | "So those are the three things in the three-ring circus: the outside world that you're aware of; the thoughts that are inside your head, your mind; and the emotions that emanate from your heart." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "It's about sitting in the seat of consciousness and being willing to watch what your mind's doing." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "Don't mess with the mind. Don't mess with the emotions. Don't suppress them or express them." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "Practice letting go, and eventually you will find yourself in a seat of awareness that can't be disturbed by anything you are experiencing." (https://michaelsingerpodcast.com) "It's really funny that the word "distraction" becomes a spiritual word." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) |
| Date | Stated in Singer's interview with Tami Simon about Living Untethered (Sounds True, published 11 June 2022); the book appeared in 2022; taught earlier in The Untethered Soul (2007) |
| In the vault | archive/reading — the completion family (hudson's art of accomplishment, mclaren's language of emotions).md; The reading record finds that Hudson and McLaren both refuse watch-it-from-above meditation; Singer's witness consciousness is the practice they refuse, which places him opposite them rather than beside them as the corpus's pairing of 'Hudson's welcome and Singer's watcher' suggests. |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *witness consciousness* | the practice of being aware of thoughts and emotions without taking part in them |
| *three-ring circus* | Singer's name for the three kinds of things awareness gets distracted by: the world, thoughts, and emotions |
| *distraction* | Singer's word for consciousness being drawn out of its seat onto an object, which he calls 'a spiritual word' |

### Relax and release

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Singer teaches the move for the moment a familiar thought or feeling is set off again. |
| What the coach does | When a familiar disturbance comes up, the person does not try to make the thought or feeling relax; they relax themselves and 'fall back behind it'. Singer says the feeling will not relax on request, but the one experiencing it can, and that relaxing in the face of it lets its pull weaken. He describes this as relaxing and leaning back, and says the rising that follows happens by itself once the person lets go. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices a pattern that has always bothered them, now set off again. (2) The person asks themselves: 'Are you willing to just relax?' (3) The person does not ask the feeling to relax, since 'It's not going to relax.' (4) The person relaxes and leans back behind the feeling: 'You, who's experiencing it, can just fall back behind it.' (5) The person keeps relaxing each time the feeling pulls, without suppressing it. |
| What it does | The move has the person relax and fall back behind a feeling so that it can pass. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a stored pattern to pass through a person rather than being held; the person supplies the relaxing, and the release is not produced by anyone else. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| The evidence | No study of Singer's practices was found; the support is his own account, the teaching of the tradition he draws on, and the wide readership of his books. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: Singer tells the person the feeling will not relax on request and that release takes repeated practice ('Come on guys, it takes a while'). Protection and defense: The move meets a defensive tightening with relaxation instead of effort. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move relaxes behind a feeling, where the book's practice attends to the felt sense from within; both refuse to force the feeling to change. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Just for the moment, are you willing to relax and release and be in there and notice that this is going on inside of me?" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "Are you willing to just relax? But it won't relax. I know it won't relax. I didn't ask it to relax. It's not going to relax. You can relax. You, who's experiencing it, can just fall back behind it." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "You don't not experience your emotions; you just relax in the face of them." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "Samskaras need room to release. Give them room, get out of the way." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "Come on guys, it takes a while." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) |
| Date | Stated in Singer's interview with Tami Simon about Living Untethered (Sounds True, published 11 June 2022); the book appeared in 2022 |
| In the vault | `notes/surrender and will are adjudicated from the body rather than from doctrine.md`; The note records the corpus's pairing of 'Singer's let-go' against 'Robbins's take-hold'; relax and release is the concrete act Singer means by letting go. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *relax and release* | Singer's practice of relaxing in the face of a disturbance so that it can pass through |
| *lean back* | to withdraw attention behind a thought or feeling rather than into it |

### Low-hanging fruit

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Singer teaches the practice for daily life, long before any crisis. |
| What the coach does | Singer tells people to practice letting go on small, daily disturbances before large ones: the weather, rain on a delivery, a driver who does not signal, someone saying they need to talk later. The person asks whether they can handle the small thing ('All right, today it's hot. Am I okay with that?') and lets the resistance go. Singer says that practicing on small things builds the capacity to handle large ones, and that a person who has not practiced will get lost when something severe happens. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices whether they resist even small things, such as heat or rain. (2) The person asks: 'All right, today it's hot. Am I okay with that?' (3) The person lets go of the resistance, since 'saying no doesn't make it not hot.' (4) The person repeats this with slightly larger disturbances, such as someone not showing up. (5) Over time the person finds that things which used to knock them off center no longer do. |
| What it does | The practice has the person let go of resistance to small daily annoyances as training for larger ones. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it easier for a person to build up, in small steps, the ability to meet events without resisting them; the person does all of it. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| The evidence | No study of Singer's practices was found; the support is his own account, the teaching of the tradition he draws on, and the wide readership of his books. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: Singer sequences practice from trivial to serious disturbances and says it is 'too late' to begin practicing in the middle of a catastrophe. Participation rather than watching: The practice happens in the person's ordinary day and cannot be done by watching someone else. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book's practice also lives in ordinary days, but it aims at attending to what a disturbance holds rather than at no longer being disturbed by it. |
| Limits and critiques | Singer says a person who has not practiced beforehand will 'get lost' in a severe event, so the method offers little to someone already in crisis. |
| Quoted from its sources | "You start to practice little things, the low-hanging fruit." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "You can sit there and say, "All right, today it's hot. Am I okay with that?" You better say yes because saying no doesn't make it not hot." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "I really see the weather as a tremendous chance for growth." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "I almost want to sit there and say it's too late." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) |
| Date | Stated in Singer's interview with Tami Simon about Living Untethered (Sounds True, published 11 June 2022); the book appeared in 2022 |
| In the vault | `notes/the tuesday is the temple.md`; The note records the book's commitment to ordinary life as the site of the work; Singer's practice on weather and traffic is a practitioner's version of the same commitment. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *low-hanging fruit* | Singer's name for small, everyday disturbances used for practice |
| *handle* | to be able to take in a reality without resisting or freaking out, in Singer's usage |

### Replacing a negative thought with a willful positive one (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Singer uses the move when a habitual complaint or negative thought arises about something that cannot be changed. |
| What the coach does | When an automatic negative thought arises, such as complaining about rain or heat, the person deliberately creates a positive thought beside it and attends to that one, without trying to stop the first. Singer's own example is to marvel that he can feel the heat of a star 93 million miles away. When a voice says the positive thought is phony, he says to let it speak and keep attending to the higher thought; he describes once answering his own doubt with 'Here, God, here's the part of me that doesn't believe in you.' |
| Steps | (1) The person notices an automatic negative thought. (2) The person deliberately adds a positive thought, for example 'I love the rain.' (3) The person does not push the negative thought away: 'You're trying to replace, not beat down, not stop, but give an alternative for your consciousness.' (4) The person puts attention on the positive thought, or on something to marvel at, such as the sun's distance. (5) If a voice objects that this is false, the person lets it speak and keeps attending to the higher thought. |
| What it does | The move has the person add a chosen positive thought and attend to it without fighting the negative one. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a person to shift attention from a habitual complaint to a chosen thought; the person chooses both the thought and where to attend. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| The evidence | No study of Singer's practices was found; the support is his own account, the teaching of the tradition he draws on, and the wide readership of his books. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: Singer explicitly allows the objecting voice to remain, so the move does not suppress the part that resists. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move adds a chosen thought on top of a habitual one, which the book would treat as managing a state rather than recognizing what is there. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Every time I have a negative thought, I replace it with a positive one." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "You're not trying to not have the negative thoughts come up. You're trying to replace, not beat down, not stop, but give an alternative for your consciousness." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "You're not lying to yourself. You're just replacing this lower energy of resistance with an acceptance, with an awe" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "Here, God, here's the part of me that doesn't believe in you." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) |
| Date | Stated in Singer's interview with Tami Simon about Living Untethered (Sounds True, published 11 June 2022); the book appeared in 2022 |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault has no record of this move; it shows that Singer, filed in the see-through camp, also uses a state-management move. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *automatic thought and willful thought* | Singer's distinction between thoughts that arise by habit and thoughts a person chooses to create |
| *awe* | the wonder Singer puts in place of resistance, as when he marvels that he can feel the heat of a star |

### The mantra 'I can handle this'

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Singer recommends the move when the mind says 'I can't handle this' in response to something upsetting. |
| What the coach does | The person keeps a repeated phrase running in a background layer of the mind and, when a negative thought or feeling arises, shifts attention to the phrase rather than fighting the thought. Singer, who came up through yoga and uses Sanskrit mantras, recommends 'I can handle this. I can handle this.' He warns against using a mantra as a sledgehammer to beat thoughts down, and says the negative thought falls away over time because attention has moved. |
| Steps | (1) The person establishes a repeated phrase in the background of the mind. (2) When a thought such as 'I can't handle this' arises, the person shifts attention to the phrase: 'I can handle this. I can handle this.' (3) The person does not use the phrase to beat the thought down: 'No, you're using a sledgehammer inside your head.' (4) The person lets the other thought remain, trusting it to fall away as attention rests on the phrase. |
| What it does | The move has the person shift attention to a repeated phrase rather than fight the distressing thought. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a person to move attention off a distressing thought without a struggle; the person repeats the phrase themselves. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| The evidence | No study of Singer's practices was found; the support is his own account, the teaching of the tradition he draws on, and the wide readership of his books. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: Singer forbids using the mantra to suppress thoughts, so the move is meant to redirect attention without fighting the part that is afraid. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move steadies the person by repetition, which the book's practice does not use. |
| Quoted from its sources | "I can handle this. I can handle this. I can handle this. I can handle this." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "No, you're using a sledgehammer inside your head. You're using the mantra to beat down your thoughts." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "I don't want you fighting with your mind, ever." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) |
| Date | Stated in Singer's interview with Tami Simon about Living Untethered (Sounds True, published 11 June 2022); the book appeared in 2022 |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The vault has no record of this move. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *mantra* | a phrase repeated in a background layer of the mind to which attention can return |
| *layers of mind* | Singer's claim that a person can run a phrase in one layer while other thoughts continue in another |

### Transmutation: keep your hands off

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Singer teaches the move for the moment an old, stored feeling is set off and begins to rise. |
| What the coach does | When an old feeling such as embarrassment, anger, or fear comes up, the person relaxes and does not push it back down, even as it grows stronger. Singer says that if nothing resists it, the feeling rises and changes into love and a rush of energy, which he calls transmutation. He tells the person to 'keep your hands off' and let the energy do the work, and warns that it takes time and that people who expect an immediate rush will be disappointed. |
| Steps | (1) An old feeling, such as embarrassment, is set off by something in the present. (2) The person recognizes it as stored material from the past. (3) The person relaxes and stops pushing it down, even as it becomes more intense. (4) The person keeps 'hands off' and does not act on the feeling or suppress it. (5) The feeling rises and, in Singer's account, turns into love or energy. |
| What it does | The move has the person stop interfering with a rising feeling so that it can change on its own. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a stored feeling to rise and finish rather than being pushed back; in Singer's terms the energy does the work and the person only stops interfering. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| The evidence | No study of Singer's practices was found; the support is his own account, the teaching of the tradition he draws on, and the wide readership of his books. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Knowing that something happened: Singer names the sign of completion, the feeling turning into love or a rush of energy, and warns that expecting the rush can itself mislead. Timing and pacing: Singer says 'it takes a while' because of how long a person has been pushing things down. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Singer's account of a feeling rising and changing if left alone resembles the book's claim that a welcomed contraction releases; it differs in adding a claim that the released energy is divine, which the book does not assert. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Relax. Keep your hands off. And all of a sudden it becomes love." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "Ultimately, what you want to do is keep your hands off in there and let the Shakti do her work." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "Come on guys, it takes a while." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) |
| Date | Stated in Singer's interview with Tami Simon about Living Untethered (Sounds True, published 11 June 2022); the book appeared in 2022 |
| In the vault | `notes/joe hudson.md`; The Hudson note records that 'completes itself' is the corpus's phrase and that Hudson's engine is non-recreation; Singer's transmutation is a third account of a feeling that changes when not resisted, with no expression step. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *transmutation* | Singer's name for a released feeling changing into a higher energy such as love |
| *hands off* | the instruction not to interfere with a rising feeling in either direction |

### Living from a place of surrender (checking outside first)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Singer teaches the practice for daily choices, requests from others, and major decisions. |
| What the coach does | Singer's practice of surrender asks a person to look first at what life is presenting, rather than first consulting their own likes and fears, and to ask how they can take part in it. He says this does not mean saying yes to every request or letting a lion eat you; it means seeing the situation without the disturbance of past impressions. For decisions, he tells a person to look behind the choice and ask what they are trying to get from it, since, in his account, the motive is almost always to feel better or to avoid a feeling. His own example is saying yes to something he did not want to do, which he says set the direction of his life. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices the pull of their own preferences and fears about a situation. (2) The person does what Singer calls checking outside first ('you check outside first'), paying attention to what is unfolding rather than to what they want. (3) Facing a decision, the person asks: 'what are you trying to get from this decision? What's your motive?' (4) The person looks for what would serve the situation rather than what would make them feel better. (5) The person keeps disciplined practices, which Singer says are needed in order to surrender at all. |
| What it does | The practice has the person look first at what a situation presents and at their own motive before choosing. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes it easier for a person to choose from what a situation requires rather than from their own fears; the person makes every decision. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| The evidence | No study of Singer's practices was found; the support is his own account, the teaching of the tradition he draws on, and the wide readership of his books. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Decisions: Singer asks a person to examine the motive behind a decision before deciding, and holds that most decisions are attempts to feel better or avoid a feeling. Who decides the content: The practice places the direction of a life with what life presents rather than with the person's preferences, which Singer frames as participation rather than obedience. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus sets Singer's letting go against Robbins's taking hold, with the body as referee; in his own words surrender is 'not giving up' and includes action, and he says discipline is required in order to surrender, which narrows the gap the corpus describes. |
| Limits and critiques | Singer's account of following life's flow is the story of how he built Medical Manager; in 2009 he was prosecuted for securities fraud in connection with the company, entered a deferred prosecution agreement in 2010, agreed to forfeit $2.5 million, and had the charges dropped, while six executives pleaded guilty and Singer has contended that he was framed. |
| Quoted from its sources | "I don't want to check inside of me first to see what I want and what I don't want." (https://www.dailygood.org/story/1876/michael-singer-living-from-a-place-of-surrender/) "let me see how I can participate in that, be part of that instead of interfering with it with my desires and my fears." (https://www.dailygood.org/story/1876/michael-singer-living-from-a-place-of-surrender/) "like if a lion jumps at you, do I just surrender and let him eat me? That's not what it's saying. What it's saying is you check outside first." (https://www.dailygood.org/story/1876/michael-singer-living-from-a-place-of-surrender/) "doesn't mean you're saying yes to every single thing or no to every single thing that people are telling you." (https://www.dailygood.org/story/1876/michael-singer-living-from-a-place-of-surrender/) "Instead of looking at the decision you're trying to make, ask yourself why—what are you trying to get from this decision? What's your motive?" (https://www.dailygood.org/story/1876/michael-singer-living-from-a-place-of-surrender/) "The problem is you're going to require those discipline techniques in order to surrender." (https://www.dailygood.org/story/1876/michael-singer-living-from-a-place-of-surrender/) "Just was like getting on a magic carpet, following the flow of life instead of following the noise inside my head." (https://www.dailygood.org/story/1876/michael-singer-living-from-a-place-of-surrender/) "surrendering yourself to life itself" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Alan_Singer) "agreed to forfeit $2.5 million in settlements" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Alan_Singer) "Singer has contended he was framed by another top executive" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Alan_Singer) "Six executives of Medical Manager eventually pleaded guilty to their roles in the scheme." (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Alan_Singer) "They are not giving up, they are not a white flag, it's not that kind of surrender." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) |
| Date | Stated in Singer's 2017 Sounds True interview about the course Living from a Place of Surrender (transcript reprinted by DailyGood); The Surrender Experiment appeared in 2015; the underlying account is in The Surrender Experiment (2015) |
| In the vault | `notes/surrender and will are adjudicated from the body rather than from doctrine.md`; The note records the corpus's 'Singer's let-go vs. Robbins's take-hold' as its sharpest fault line; Singer's own words describe surrender as participation that 'doesn't mean you're saying yes to every single thing' and as requiring discipline. |
| Sources | https://www.dailygood.org/story/1876/michael-singer-living-from-a-place-of-surrender/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Alan_Singer; https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *surrender* | in Singer's usage, letting go of one's preferences so as to take part in what life presents, not giving up |
| *the surrender experiment* | Singer's decades-long commitment to follow what life brought him rather than his personal preferences, told in his 2015 memoir |
| *the flow of life* | the events that unfold without a person's control, which Singer's practice asks them to follow |

### Honoring the reality that took place, then asking how to help (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Singer describes the move for news of a public atrocity or any harm a person cannot undo. |
| What the coach does | Asked how to meet news of a school shooting, Singer said the first step is to honor and respect that it happened, since resisting the fact changes nothing, and to let oneself feel its weight without closing down. The second step is to ask what one can actually do to help, for example to help pass gun laws, rather than acting to relieve one's own anger or distress. He tells people to be activists and 'put your whole heart into it', but not because they cannot handle what happened. |
| Steps | (1) The person lets the news in and acknowledges that it happened. (2) The person feels the event's weight without freezing or closing down. (3) The person separates acting to relieve their own distress from acting to help. (4) The person asks: 'Now, what can I do to help? Not help me, not help my anger, not help my resistance, not help my hatred.' (5) The person acts, for example toward changing laws, from that place. |
| What it does | The move has the person first accept that a harm happened and then act to help rather than to relieve their own distress. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it easier for a person to act on a harm without the action being driven by their own resistance; the person chooses the action. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| The evidence | No study of Singer's practices was found; the support is his own account, the teaching of the tradition he draws on, and the wide readership of his books. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Whether the cause lies in the person or the system: Singer keeps the cause of distress in the person's resistance while endorsing action on the structure that caused the harm, such as gun laws. Decisions: Singer separates actions taken to relieve one's own distress from actions taken to help, and asks the person to choose the second. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus's charge against the coaches is 'radical interiority of cause'; this move shows Singer endorsing action on structures while keeping the inner work first, which the corpus's summary does not record. |
| Limits and critiques | Singer says that a person who has not practiced beforehand may be too overwhelmed to do this in the moment ('I almost want to sit there and say it's too late'). |
| Quoted from its sources | "Okay, so the first thing you do is honor and respect the reality that took place." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "Now, what can I do to help? Not help me, not help my anger, not help my resistance, not help my hatred." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "What can I do to help them pass gun laws or do whatever it is that needs to be done?" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "By all means, be an activist, put your whole heart into it, but not because you can't handle it" (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "What you're doing is surrendering your resistance to the reality of the situation." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) "I almost want to sit there and say it's too late." (https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/) |
| Date | Stated in Singer's interview with Tami Simon about Living Untethered (Sounds True, published 11 June 2022); the book appeared in 2022 |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md`; The note records the corpus's charge that the five camps cannot hold suffering as structural; this move is Singer's own answer to that charge, in which he recommends activism on causes such as gun laws. |
| Sources | https://www.soundstrue.com/a/resources/transcript/living-untethered/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *acceptance* | in Singer's usage, acknowledging that something happened, which he says is not approval and not inaction |
| *handle* | to take in a reality without resisting or freezing, which Singer says a person must be able to do before they can help |

## John Wineland

| Move | Type | When the coach uses it | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| Habits of closure and living from the core | a theory | Wineland teaches this account in men's groups, mixed workshops, and his book. | The account explains suffering in relationships as bodily closure set off by old patterns and prescribes opening the body and deepening awareness. |
| Dropping into the core | a technique | Wineland teaches it as a daily practice and for the moment before leading, relating, or making love. | The practice slows the breath down into the lower abdomen while pressing the feet or sitting bones into the floor. |
| Opening the body when triggered | a move | Wineland uses it at the moment a partner's words set off an old pattern. | The move shifts attention from self-referential thought to opening the belly, heart, and posture. |
| Breaking the karmic vines | a move | Wineland uses it in long-term relationships, at the moment a familiar fight begins. | The move has one partner respond differently at the exact point where a familiar fight usually starts. |
| From being present to having presence | a move | Wineland uses it with a partner, a child, or a man he is working with. | The move directs one's felt awareness of the moment outward through breath and gaze so another person can feel it. |
| Grounding a partner's nervous system | a move | Wineland uses it when a partner is anxious or overwhelmed. | The move grounds a distressed partner through the helper's slow breath, hands on the partner's legs, and eye contact. |
| The polarity exercise with the poles flipped | a technique | Wineland uses it in mixed workshops. | The exercise has one partner embody stillness and gaze while the other moves, with the roles assigned against gender. |
| Singing in public: the play assignment | a technique | Wineland assigns it to men in his groups who want more access to play. | The assignment sends a man to sing in public so that he meets, and moves through, what blocks his playful expression. |
| The questions of conscious warriorhood | a move | Wineland uses the questions with men to direct what he calls the proper use of their fierceness. | The questions ask what a person would die for, protect, or create, to find what their force serves. |
| Offering recognition as nourishment | a move | Wineland teaches it to partners in workshops and uses archetypes to help them find it. | The move has a partner offer, through the body, the specific recognition the other most needs. |

### Habits of closure and living from the core

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a theory |
| When the coach uses it | Wineland teaches this account in men's groups, mixed workshops, and his book. |
| What the coach does | Wineland describes how a stimulus in a relationship 'triggers a childhood pattern or childhood wound, which then makes us self-referential in our thought', and how the body follows: over years, he says, the heart space collapses, the sternum comes in, the shoulders round, and the belly tightens. He calls these 'the habits of closure, to use a term David uses', and treats the repeated pattern in a relationship as karma. His remedy is to train the body to open and to take awareness down from thought into 'the core': the lower abdomen, the heart, and the center of a person, which he calls 'the part of them that is unchanging and infinite'. He holds that every person can amplify both awareness and sensitivity, and that the goal is their integration rather than one amplified pole. |
| What it does | The account explains suffering in relationships as bodily closure set off by old patterns and prescribes opening the body and deepening awareness. |
| What is facilitated | The work makes it more likely that a person opens their own body and so becomes more available in relationship; the practice is the person's. Wineland supplies breath and posture instructions, exercises, and a vocabulary. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option. Wineland offers his frame and practices and invites people to relabel his terms if they do not fit. |
| The evidence | Wineland's evidence is his 'thousands of hours' of watching people in workshops; he explains effects with a 'mirror neuron effect' and nervous-system terms, which the sources opened do not test. The sources opened contain no outcome study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: Wineland reads bodily closure as a childhood pattern set off by a trigger and meets it by opening posture and breath. Recognition: He calls the deepest core 'unchanging and infinite' and speaks of 'that great I am that is always there', which is the vocabulary of recognition. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The corpus pairs Wineland with Deida in its fifth theory; Wineland says he differs from Deida's framework, asserting that people 'can benefit from developing capacity in both, but ultimately bringing both in an integration', and that people should drop the words masculine and feminine if the words trigger them. |
| Limits and critiques | Wineland warns about misuse of his own terms: 'Please, I see people weaponize these terms all the time'. Tracy Clark-Flory's 2021 critique in Jezebel of the polarity doctrine Wineland adapts calls Deida's argument 'fundamentally generalizing and essentialist'; the sources opened contain no critique that names Wineland. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Normally it’s some trigger that some stimulus in the environment, in the relationship, that triggers a childhood pattern or childhood wound, which then makes us self-referential in our thought." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "their heart space will actually collapse. Their sternum will come in. Their shoulders will round. Their belly will get tight." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "The habits of thinking and the habits of closure, to use a term David uses" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "And that I would label is the part of them that is unchanging and infinite." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "that field of awareness, that great I am that is always there" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "My assertion is that humans in particular can benefit from developing capacity in both, but ultimately bringing both in an integration." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "If these words sort of trigger something or don’t feel, like you said, native to your experience, then I would ditch them and think about emptiness and fullness." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Please, I see people weaponize these terms all the time" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "It’s a sort of a mirror neuron effect that happens." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "over thousands of hours of seeing people in workshops" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "And so even in atoms, they’ve discovered that most atoms are empty and they’re energy." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "followed him around for many, many years" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Drawing from intensive study with David Deida, as well as the deep lineages of Vajrayana, tantra, kundalini, and qigong" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/podcast/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Deida’s argument is fundamentally generalizing and essentialist." (https://www.jezebel.com/crazy-women-fiery-latinas-and-masculine-sexual-essenc-1847495117) "I don't need to change anything; however, if I want my love or consciousness to be felt in the world, I have to open my body through breath, relaxation, posture, or movement." (archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md) |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *habits of closure* | Habits of closure are the bodily and mental ways a person turns inward when an old pattern is triggered; Wineland takes the phrase from Deida. |
| *the core* | The core is Wineland's word for the lower abdomen, the truth of the heart, and the unchanging center of a person, taken together. |
| *transmission* | Transmission is Wineland's word for making one's inner state felt by others through breath, eyes, and body. |
| *integration* | Integration is Wineland's goal of developing both awareness and sensitivity and bringing them together in one person. |

### Dropping into the core

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Wineland teaches it as a daily practice and for the moment before leading, relating, or making love. |
| What the coach does | Wineland guided listeners through the practice live: press the soles of the feet into the floor, or the sitting bones if cross-legged, open the soles, and breathe, preferably through the nose, down into the base of the abdomen as slowly as possible for about 30 seconds or three breaths, with small holds at the top and bottom. He describes a 'five- or six-step process of grounding, of breathing, of relaxing the heart, of relaxing the body' in his book. He says the practice slows the nervous system and the heart rate, and that people around a grounded person experience them as more 'grounded, stable, trustable'. |
| Steps | (1) The person presses the soles of the feet into the floor, or the sitting bones if sitting cross-legged, and opens the soles of the feet. (2) The person breathes, preferably through the nose, down into the base of the abdomen, as slowly and elongated as they can. (3) The person continues for about 30 seconds, which might be three breaths. (4) The person holds briefly at the top of the in-breath and at the bottom of the out-breath. (5) The person takes awareness down into the base of the abdomen, where, Wineland says, they might 'feel the abdomen expand in all directions'. (6) The person then relaxes the heart and the body, in the fuller sequence Wineland gives in his book. |
| What it does | The practice slows the breath down into the lower abdomen while pressing the feet or sitting bones into the floor. |
| What is facilitated | The practice makes a slower, steadier state more likely in the person; the state is the person's own. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Wineland gives the instructions, and the person does the breathing and notices the effect. |
| The evidence | Wineland's account of effects on the nervous system is his own; he attributes to Tibetan teaching the claim that 'much neurosis tends to come from lack of lower belly breathing' and adds 'whether you buy that or not'. The sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Timing and pacing: The practice's first instruction is to slow the moment down, with pauses at the top and bottom of the breath. The container that holds people while they change: Wineland holds that a grounded person is experienced by others as more stable and trustable, which is his account of how a helper's state becomes part of the setting. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book reads contraction as protection to be understood; Wineland's breath practice changes the state directly and treats understanding as secondary. |
| Quoted from its sources | "if you press the soles of your feet down into the floor, or your sitz bones, if you’re sitting cross-legged" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "and then begin to breathe, preferably through the nose, down into the base of your abdomen as slowly and elongated as you can." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "And you do that, let’s say, for 30 seconds. Might just be three breaths." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Maybe a little hold at the top of the inhale. Maybe a little hold at the bottom of the exhale." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "And just slow the moment down by taking your awareness down into the base of the abdomen." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Like a five- or six-step process of grounding, of breathing, of relaxing the heart, of relaxing the body." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "we’re going to occur to the world around us as more grounded, stable, trustable." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "one of the first things they’ll teach you is to connect to your pelvic floor to your dantian" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "much neurosis tends to come from lack of lower belly breathing" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "And whether you buy that or not" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "And you might actually feel the abdomen expand in all directions." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) |
| Date | 2022 (From the Core: A New Masculine Paradigm for Leading with Love, Living Your Truth, and Healing the World, Sounds True; Insights at the Edge interview with Tami Simon, 2 August 2022) |
| In the vault | notes/the facilitator is an instrument in the field, so self-awareness is professional competence.md; The vault holds that the facilitator is an instrument in the field; Wineland's claim that his settled state relaxes the people around him makes the same claim about a teacher's body. |
| Sources | https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/; https://resources2.soundstrue.com/podcast/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *the core* | The physical core is the lower abdomen and pelvic floor, which martial arts call the dantian. |
| *grounding* | Grounding is lowering attention and breath into the lower body and the contact with the floor. |

### Opening the body when triggered (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Wineland uses it at the moment a partner's words set off an old pattern. |
| What the coach does | Wineland says that closure is 'almost always accompanied by self-referential thought', such as 'I’m not doing it right. They don’t like me. They’re going to leave.' The habit he teaches is to take awareness off that thought and put it on expanding the belly, relaxing the heart, dropping the shoulders, lifting the sternum, and planting the feet and sacrum, 'on opening the body'. He says this immediately changes the thought pattern and makes more responses available than 'the same old default possibilities'. |
| Steps | (1) The person notices the trigger and the self-referential thought, such as 'I’m not doing it right. They don’t like me. They’re going to leave.' (2) The person takes awareness off the thought. (3) The person puts awareness on expanding the belly and relaxing the heart. (4) The person drops the shoulders, lifts the sternum, and plants the feet and sacrum. (5) The person keeps awareness on opening the body and notices that the thought pattern changes. |
| What it does | The move shifts attention from self-referential thought to opening the belly, heart, and posture. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it more likely that a person stays open when triggered; the person does the noticing and the opening. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The person notices their own trigger and changes their own posture and attention. |
| The evidence | The source is Wineland's interview; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Protection and defense: The move treats a defensive thought and a collapsing posture as one pattern and changes it through the body. Conflict: The move is designed for the moment when an exchange with a partner sets off an old pattern. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book asks what a contraction protected before it opens; Wineland opens the body first and says the thought pattern changes as a result. |
| Quoted from its sources | "but it’s almost always accompanied by self-referential thought" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "I’m not doing it right. They don’t like me. They’re going to leave." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "So the habit will be take my awareness off of the self-referential thought, put my awareness on expanding the belly and relaxing the heart, maybe dropping the shoulders, maybe lifting the sternum, maybe planting the feet, planting the sacrum, and put my awareness on opening, on opening the body." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "And that immediately changes the thought pattern." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "not just the same old default possibilities that we find ourselves in, especially in relationships" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) |
| Date | 2022 (From the Core: A New Masculine Paradigm for Leading with Love, Living Your Truth, and Healing the World, Sounds True; Insights at the Edge interview with Tami Simon, 2 August 2022) |
| In the vault | `notes/suffering is contraction never corruption.md`; The vault's doctrine names suffering as fullness contracted for protection; Wineland describes the same contraction in bodily terms and prescribes opening, agreeing on what closure is and differing on whether to learn from it first. |
| Sources | https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *self-referential thought* | Self-referential thought is thinking that turns on oneself under threat, such as fearing one is being judged or left. |

### Breaking the karmic vines

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Wineland uses it in long-term relationships, at the moment a familiar fight begins. |
| What the coach does | Wineland says that between the second and tenth year of a relationship the patterns of childhood, which he calls karma, show up in the relationship, and that breaking these 'karmic vines' is 'a moment-to-moment practice of presence, of awareness, and sensitivity'. He points to the moment when 'your partner says the thing that they always say to you that causes you to say the thing back that you always say to them', and says that to 'drop deeper in' and choose a different response at that moment changes the pattern. He adds that emotional safety and sexual safety are 'intimately linked', so the same moment affects a couple's sex life. |
| Steps | (1) The partner identifies the moment when the other says what they always say and they are about to say what they always say back. (2) At that moment the partner will 'drop deeper in': open the body, deepen the breath, and feel the moment. (3) The partner looks for a new possibility that comes from that connection to the moment. (4) The partner chooses a different approach or a different response. (5) The partner repeats this as a practice; Wineland calls such acts 'gestures toward continual practice' that are never mastered. |
| What it does | The move has one partner respond differently at the exact point where a familiar fight usually starts. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it more likely that a couple's repeated fight takes a different course; the choice is the partner's. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. One partner chooses a different response in the moment. |
| The evidence | The source is Wineland's interview; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Conflict: The move targets the exact moment a repeated fight begins and asks for a different response there. Timing and pacing: The move depends on catching one precise moment rather than on a later conversation. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | Wineland treats the repeated fight as changeable by practice in the moment; the book's concern is how a person comes to see such a pattern, which Wineland treats as a matter of practice rather than insight. |
| Quoted from its sources | "And so breaking that, breaking those karmic vines, I argue, is a moment-to-moment practice of presence, of awareness, and sensitivity." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "And so let’s pick the moment that your partner says the thing that they always say to you that causes you to say the thing back that you always say to them, and then you have the same fight that you’ve had." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "And so the moment that any human can drop deeper in, access a new possibility from a connection to the moment and choose a different approach or a different response, they’re changing the karma" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Never master, really, or never complete. They’re gestures toward continual practice." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Our emotional safety and our sexual safety are intimately linked." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Well, what happens from the second to the fifth to the tenth year is that the patterns of our childhood and of our life, let’s call it our karma" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) |
| Date | 2022 (From the Core: A New Masculine Paradigm for Leading with Love, Living Your Truth, and Healing the World, Sounds True; Insights at the Edge interview with Tami Simon, 2 August 2022) |
| In the vault | `notes/the field between two people does work.md`; The vault holds that the field between two people does work; Wineland's karmic vine is a repeated pattern in that field, which he treats as changeable by one partner's response. |
| Sources | https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *karmic vines* | Karmic vines are Wineland's name for the repeated patterns, formed in childhood and over a person's life, that shape a relationship's recurring fights. |

### From being present to having presence

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Wineland uses it with a partner, a child, or a man he is working with. |
| What the coach does | Wineland distinguishes being present, 'a simple awareness practice' of noticing the desk, the room, the breath, and another's voice, from having presence. He says that when he takes the experience of feeling the moment and transmits it 'through the body, through my breath, through my eyes, literally through the pores', he is experienced as having presence, which turns 'a personal experience' into 'an interpersonal relational experience'. He calls this 'tantra'. |
| Steps | (1) The practitioner practices being present, becoming aware of the surface under their hands, the room, their breath, and the sound of another's voice. (2) The practitioner feels the moment more fully. (3) The practitioner transmits that experience through the body, the breath, and the eyes toward the space they are in or the person they are with. |
| What it does | The move directs one's felt awareness of the moment outward through breath and gaze so another person can feel it. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it more likely that another person feels met; what they feel is theirs, and the practitioner supplies only their own state. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content. The practitioner changes and directs their own state; the other person is not asked to do anything. |
| The evidence | The source is Wineland's interview; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Being seen and recognized by another: The move turns a private awareness practice into something another person can feel directed at them. The container that holds people while they change: The practitioner's presence is offered as part of the setting in which another person is met. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book is a text and cannot transmit presence through breath or eyes; the vault records that writing cannot transmit the thing itself. |
| Quoted from its sources | "Which is just a simple awareness practice to be aware of the desk under my hands and the room that I’m in and the breath and the sound of your voice." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Now, when I actually take that experience of feeling the moment more fully and I transmit it through the body, through my breath, through my eyes, literally through the pores, through the energetic, subtle body, I’m now experienced as having presence." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "It goes from a personal experience of being present in the moment to an interpersonal relational experience." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "And it’s tantra. It’s tantra." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "or into the eyes of a child or a man that I’m working with" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) |
| Date | 2022 (From the Core: A New Masculine Paradigm for Leading with Love, Living Your Truth, and Healing the World, Sounds True; Insights at the Edge interview with Tami Simon, 2 August 2022) |
| In the vault | `notes/writing cannot transmit the thing but can build the friction under which it may ignite in a reader who supplies the fire.md`; The vault holds that writing cannot transmit the thing but can create conditions for a reader; Wineland's move depends on transmission through a present body, which the book's medium lacks. |
| Sources | https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *presence* | Having presence, in Wineland's use, is being felt by others as present, as distinct from being present to oneself. |
| *transmission* | Transmission is directing one's felt state outward so that others can feel it. |

### Grounding a partner's nervous system (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Wineland uses it when a partner is anxious or overwhelmed. |
| What the coach does | Wineland says that when his partner is 'in a state of anxiety or even trauma', his practice can 'down regulate her nervous system', and that this applies to any person. He first uses his breath to settle himself, then places his hands on the partner's legs, uses eye contact 'to feel into a deep sensitivity', and uses his breath 'to help ground both of us in that moment'. He says this can turn what could be hours of disagreement into a heartfelt connection. |
| Steps | (1) The helper uses breath to settle himself. (2) The helper places hands on the partner's legs 'to just ground their nervous system'. (3) The helper uses eye contact to feel into a deep sensitivity to what the partner is experiencing. (4) The helper uses his breath 'to help ground both of us in that moment'. |
| What it does | The move grounds a distressed partner through the helper's slow breath, hands on the partner's legs, and eye contact. |
| What is facilitated | The move makes it more likely that a distressed partner settles; the settling happens in the partner's own body, and the helper supplies breath, touch, and attention. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. The helper supplies breath, touch, and eye contact, and the partner's own nervous system does the settling. |
| The evidence | The source is Wineland's interview; the sources opened contain no study of this procedure. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The container that holds people while they change: The move uses the helper's own steadiness, touch, and gaze as the setting in which the other person can settle. Conflict: Wineland says the move can turn what could be hours of disagreement into a heartfelt connection. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The move needs a body present to touch and breathe with, which the book as a text cannot supply. |
| Limits and critiques | The interview describes touching a partner who is anxious or traumatized and does not discuss consent or when touch is unwelcome. |
| Quoted from its sources | "suppose my partner is in a state of anxiety or even trauma, and her nervous system is lit up—and this obviously applies to any human, my practice has the ability to down regulate her nervous system." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "and then using hands on the legs to just ground their nervous system, using eye contact to feel into a deep sensitivity" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "and then literally using my breath to help ground both of us in that moment, has an immediate impact on her nervous system." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "can change moments that could be hours of disagreement into a very deep, heartfelt connection and hopefully softening." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) |
| Date | 2022 (From the Core: A New Masculine Paradigm for Leading with Love, Living Your Truth, and Healing the World, Sounds True; Insights at the Edge interview with Tami Simon, 2 August 2022) |

| *down regulate* | To down regulate is to help a nervous system move from alarm toward calm. |

### The polarity exercise with the poles flipped (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Wineland uses it in mixed workshops. |
| What the coach does | Wineland describes what he calls a spectrum in every person from stillness, emptiness, and awareness to energy, movement, and feeling, and says any person, 'regardless of sexual orientation, regardless of gender identification', can amplify either. Following Deida, he says an 'arc' of connection forms when one partner amplifies emptiness and the other amplifies energy and motion. In workshops he likes to 'flip it': he has a woman amplify the still pole, dropping into consciousness and using breath to transmit it through her eyes and body, while the man begins to move, and they create the same arc. He says people get 'tripped up' by 'suppressive history behind the terms masculine and feminine', and suggests calling the poles emptiness and fullness instead. |
| Steps | (1) One partner, in Wineland's words, will 'drop deeper into consciousness' and use breath to transmit it through the eyes and body. (2) The other partner begins moving, amplifying energy and motion. (3) The pair notices the arc of connection between the still pole and the moving pole. (4) Wineland assigns the still pole to the woman and the moving pole to the man, reversing the usual assignment. (5) Participants may call the poles emptiness and fullness instead of masculine and feminine. |
| What it does | The exercise has one partner embody stillness and gaze while the other moves, with the roles assigned against gender. |
| What is facilitated | The exercise makes a felt experience of each pole available to each participant regardless of gender; what they feel is theirs. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Wineland assigns the poles in the exercise, and the participants supply their own experience of them. |
| The evidence | The source is Wineland's interview; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Harm and its conditions: The flip is Wineland's response to the charge that polarity teaching fixes roles by gender, though in the same interview he describes what men and women want in what he calls 'heteronormative terms'. Participation rather than watching: The exercise is done in pairs with breath, gaze, and movement rather than discussed. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The book holds no doctrine of two poles; the exercise presupposes that depth and attraction arise between a still pole and a moving pole. |
| Limits and critiques | Clark-Flory's 2021 critique says Deida is careful 'ever-so-briefly' to detach his argument from gender but that his argument remains 'fundamentally generalizing and essentialist'; Wineland's flip is his own response to 'suppressive history behind the terms masculine and feminine'. |
| Quoted from its sources | "regardless of sexual orientation, regardless of gender identification" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "One of the things I like to do in workshops is flip it and have a woman amplify her masculine, drop deeper into consciousness, use breath to transmit it through her eyes, through her body, and then have the man begin moving and they create the same arc of polarity." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "People get tripped up because we have all this suppressive history behind the terms masculine and feminine." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Practice going deeper into emptiness. Practice going more into fullness" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "And I’m just using heteronormative terms now." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Deida is careful to ever-so-briefly, and in the spirit of a disclaimer, detach his argument from gender." (https://www.jezebel.com/crazy-women-fiery-latinas-and-masculine-sexual-essenc-1847495117) "about 90% of people have either a more masculine or a more feminine sexual essence" (archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md) |
| Date | 2022 (From the Core: A New Masculine Paradigm for Leading with Love, Living Your Truth, and Healing the World, Sounds True; Insights at the Edge interview with Tami Simon, 2 August 2022) |
| In the vault | archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md; The reading record documents Deida's essence doctrine, that about 90% of people have a more masculine or more feminine sexual essence; Wineland's exercise keeps the two poles and detaches them from gender in practice. |
| Sources | https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/; https://www.jezebel.com/crazy-women-fiery-latinas-and-masculine-sexual-essenc-1847495117 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *polarity* | Polarity is the attraction and depth Wineland and Deida say arise between a still, empty pole and a moving, full pole. |
| *emptiness and fullness* | Emptiness and fullness are Wineland's gender-free names for the two poles. |

### Singing in public: the play assignment (a plain description; the coach gives it no name)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a technique |
| When the coach uses it | Wineland assigns it to men in his groups who want more access to play. |
| What the coach does | To build what he calls the currency of play, Wineland has the men in his group 'go out and sing. Go out into public and practice busking', or 'Pick a song and go out and actually express themselves fully', or take an improv class. He says the practice is designed to bring a person to their block: 'the practice is actually going to get them to the block and they have to actually move the energy through that block and the block eventually dissolves.' He calls play 'a very liberating currency' and treats it as a trained capacity: 'you have to practice them. You have to train.' |
| Steps | (1) The man chooses a song. (2) He goes into public and sings, or busks, expressing himself fully; an alternative is an improv class. (3) He meets the block that inhibits his playfulness. (4) He moves the energy through the block until it dissolves. (5) He practices play outside the moments where it is needed, so that it is available when it counts. |
| What it does | The assignment sends a man to sing in public so that he meets, and moves through, what blocks his playful expression. |
| What is facilitated | The assignment makes it more likely that a man finds and passes through his inhibition about playful expression; the act and its effect are his. |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority. Wineland assigns the act; the man decides how to do it. |
| The evidence | The source is Wineland's interview; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | Participation rather than watching: The assignment works only if the man goes out and sings; watching someone else do it does not reach his block. Protection and defense: The assignment is designed to reach the inhibition that protects a man from looking foolish and to move through it. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The assigned act resembles Deida's weekly behavioral experiment, which the vault notes Deida justifies with a gendered doctrine; Wineland frames his as practice in play and mentions no consequence for refusing. |
| Quoted from its sources | "So something I’ll do with men in particular—I’m running a group now—I’ll have them go out and sing. Go out into public and practice busking for…" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Pick a song and go out and actually express themselves fully. Or I’ll have them take an improv class." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "So when I send somebody out to sing a song on the street, the practice is actually going to get them to the block and they have to actually move the energy through that block and the block eventually dissolves." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Because play is a very liberating currency, I would call it." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "these things are available, but you have to practice them. You have to train." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "Then, your friends should give you a behavioral experiment, something you can do that will reveal something to you" (archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md) |
| Date | 2022 (From the Core: A New Masculine Paradigm for Leading with Love, Living Your Truth, and Healing the World, Sounds True; Insights at the Edge interview with Tami Simon, 2 August 2022) |
| In the vault | archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md; The reading record holds Deida's mechanism of weekly witnesses who assign 'a behavioral experiment' with an agreed consequence for freezing; Wineland's street-singing assignment is an act at a threshold assigned in a men's group, with no consequence mentioned. |
| Sources | https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *currencies of presence and play* | Wineland calls presence and play currencies: gifts people can give each other in relationships. |
| *the block* | The block is whatever inhibits a person's playful expression, which the assignment is designed to reach. |

### The questions of conscious warriorhood

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Type | a move |
| When the coach uses it | Wineland uses the questions with men to direct what he calls the proper use of their fierceness. |
| What the coach does | Wineland ends From the Core with 'conscious warriorhood', which he defines as making 'your body, your presence, an instrument of consciousness'. He says the idea of the warrior has been 'co-opted by the film industry' as a killing machine, and that the warrior aspect he sees in men is the 'proper use of their fierceness'. He locates that use with four questions: 'What would they die for? What would they die to protect? What would they die to create?' and 'What would they literally give the depth of their heart to expand or liberate?' |
| Steps | (1) Wineland asks of the men he works with: 'What would they die for?' (2) He asks: 'What would they die to protect?' (3) He asks: 'What would they die to create?' (4) He asks: 'What would they literally give the depth of their heart to expand or liberate?' (5) The man directs his fierceness toward his answers, making his body, in Wineland's words, 'an instrument of consciousness'. |
| What it does | The questions ask what a person would die for, protect, or create, to find what their force serves. |
| What is facilitated | The questions make it more likely that a person names what their force is for; the answers are theirs. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content. Wineland poses the questions, and each man supplies his own answers. |
| The evidence | The source is Wineland's interview; the sources opened contain no study. |
| How it bears on the book (the researcher's reading) | The future: The questions direct a person's energy toward what they would protect or create. Protection and defense: The questions ask what a person would defend, and treat fierceness as something to aim rather than suppress. |
| Where it differs from the book (the researcher's reading) | The questions frame purpose through readiness to die, which ties purpose to a warrior ideal the book does not use. |
| Quoted from its sources | "So conscious warriorhood is to make your body, your presence, an instrument of consciousness." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "the idea of “warrior” has been co-opted by the film industry" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "The warriorhood aspect of it, which is something I see in men a lot, is proper use of their fierceness." (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "What would they die for? What would they die to protect? What would they die to create?" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) "What would they literally give the depth of their heart to expand or liberate?" (https://resources2.soundstrue.com/transcript/masculine-depth-and-power-from-the-core/) |
| Date | 2022 (From the Core: A New Masculine Paradigm for Leading with Love, Living Your Truth, and Healing the World, Sounds True; Insights at the Edge interview with Tami Simon, 2 August 2022) |
